Appendix CSolutions to the Exercises

This appendix collects complete solutions to the end-of-chapter exercises. Before reading a solution, please be sure to work through the problem yourself at least once. If you get stuck along the way, go back to the hints at the end of the chapter and the worked examples in the text, and then read the solution here. From the end of each solution you can return to its chapter.

Chapter 1 What Is Machine Learning? — Learning Rules from Data

Exercise 1.1: Fitting a line to three points

Solution

(1) For the candidate $(w_0,w_1)=(1,1)$, the predicted values at $x=0,1,2$ are $1,2,3$, and the residuals are $1-1=0$, $2-2=0$, $4-3=1$. The sum of the squared residuals is $0+0+1=1$, so $L=\tfrac12\times1=0.5$. For the candidate $(0,2)$, the predicted values are $0,2,4$ and the residuals are $1,0,0$. The sum of squares is 1, so this one also gives $L=0.5$. The loss cannot distinguish the two lines.

(2) We compute the sums. $N=3$, $\sum x_n=0+1+2=3$, $\sum x_n^{2}=0+1+4=5$, $\sum y_n=1+2+4=7$, $\sum x_ny_n=0\times1+1\times2+2\times4=10$. The normal equations, Eq. (1.5) of Chapter 1, are

$$ 3w_0+3w_1=7,\qquad 3w_0+5w_1=10 . $$

Subtracting the first equation from the second gives $2w_1=3$, so $\hat{w}_1=1.5$. Substituting back into the first equation gives $3w_0=7-4.5=2.5$, so $\hat{w}_0=5/6\approx0.833$. The predicted values at $x=0,1,2$ are $5/6,\ 7/3,\ 23/6$, and the residuals are $1-5/6=1/6$, $2-7/3=-1/3$, $4-23/6=1/6$. The sum of squares is $1/36+4/36+1/36=6/36=1/6$, so the minimum loss is $L=\tfrac12\times\tfrac16=\tfrac1{12}\approx0.0833$. This is smaller than the value 0.5 of the two lines in (1).

(3) Dividing the first normal equation, $Nw_0+\bigl(\sum_n x_n\bigr)w_1=\sum_n y_n$, by $N$ gives $w_0+\bar{x}w_1=\bar{y}$; that is,

$$ \hat{w}_0=\bar{y}-\hat{w}_1\bar{x}. $$

Substitute this into the second equation, $\bigl(\sum_n x_n\bigr)w_0+\bigl(\sum_n x_n^{2}\bigr)w_1=\sum_n x_ny_n$. Using $\sum_n x_n=N\bar{x}$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} &N\bar{x}(\bar{y}-w_1\bar{x})+\Bigl(\sum_n x_n^{2}\Bigr)w_1\\ &\qquad=\sum_n x_ny_n,\\ &\Bigl(\sum_n x_n^{2}-N\bar{x}^{2}\Bigr)w_1\\ &\qquad=\sum_n x_ny_n-N\bar{x}\bar{y}. \end{aligned} $$

Here the expressions in parentheses on the right-hand and left-hand sides can be rewritten as sums of deviations from the mean.

$$ \begin{aligned} &\sum_n(x_n-\bar{x})(y_n-\bar{y})\\ &\quad=\sum_n x_ny_n-\bar{y}\sum_n x_n\\ &\qquad-\bar{x}\sum_n y_n+N\bar{x}\bar{y}\\ &\quad=\sum_n x_ny_n-N\bar{x}\bar{y}\\ &\qquad-N\bar{x}\bar{y}+N\bar{x}\bar{y}\\ &\quad=\sum_n x_ny_n-N\bar{x}\bar{y}. \end{aligned} $$

The same calculation with $y_n$ replaced by $x_n$ gives $\sum_n(x_n-\bar{x})^{2}=\sum_n x_n^{2}-N\bar{x}^{2}$. Therefore, when the inputs are not all equal (so that the denominator is not 0),

$$ \hat{w}_1=\frac{\sum_n(x_n-\bar{x})(y_n-\bar{y})}{\sum_n(x_n-\bar{x})^{2}}. $$

We check this with the numbers from (2). $\bar{x}=1$, $\bar{y}=7/3$, and the deviations are $-1,0,1$ for $x$ and $-4/3,-1/3,5/3$ for $y$. The numerator $=4/3+0+5/3=3$ and the denominator $=2$, so $\hat{w}_1=1.5$ and $\hat{w}_0=7/3-1.5=5/6$, which agrees.

Exercise 1.2: Classifying materials tasks

Solution

(1) Regression, supervised. The output is a numerical value, the hardness, and learning requires pairs of composition and heat-treatment conditions with the measured hardness.

(2) Classification (two classes), supervised. The experts' judgments serve as the target values (labels). The number 300 is small for image classification, so one must watch out for overfitting.

(3) Clustering in a latent representation, unsupervised. The correct groups are not given; the groups are found from the distribution of the structural descriptors. The number of groups must also be decided from the data and the purpose.

(4) The stage of predicting the hidden word is self-supervised learning. The target value (the hidden word) is made from the text itself, and no person attaches labels. The task at this stage is a classification problem: deciding which word of the vocabulary it is. The subsequent stage, which takes the learned representation as input and predicts a physical property, uses labels of the property values and is supervised regression (or classification).

(5) Density estimation, unsupervised. The output is the distribution of the capacitance, and one computes the area (probability) outside the specification. When only a few units are out of specification, the assumption about the shape of the tail of the distribution (a Gaussian distribution or a heavy-tailed distribution) strongly affects the result.

Exercise 1.3: Extending from three points

Solution

The three points are $s=-1,-0.5,0$ with $y=74.2,81.4,87.3\ \%$. The means are $\bar{s}=-0.5$ and $\bar{y}=(74.2+81.4+87.3)/3$ $=242.9/3=80.967\ \%$. The deviations from the mean are $-0.5,0,0.5$ for $s$ and $-6.767,0.433,6.333$ for $y$.

$$ \begin{aligned} &\sum_n(s_n-\bar{s})(y_n-\bar{y})\\ &\quad=(-0.5)(-6.767)+0+(0.5)(6.333)\\ &\quad=6.55,\\ &\sum_n(s_n-\bar{s})^{2}=0.25+0+0.25=0.5 . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\hat{w}_1=6.55/0.5=13.1\ \%$ and $\hat{w}_0=\bar{y}-\hat{w}_1\bar{s}$ $=80.967+13.1\times0.5=87.517\ \%$.

At 1400 °C ($s=0.5$): $\hat{y}=87.517+6.55=94.07\ \%$ (observed 92.6 %, difference $+1.47$ %). At 1500 °C ($s=1$): $\hat{y}=87.517+13.1=100.62\ \%$ (observed 91.4 %, difference $+9.22$ %).

The prediction reaches 100 % when $87.517+13.1s=100$, that is, $s=12.483/13.1=0.953$, which in temperature is $T=1300+200\times0.953=1490.6$ °C. At temperatures higher than this, the prediction for the relative density exceeds 100 %, which is physically impossible. The causes are (a) that the three points at 1100–1300 °C contain no information that the densification levels off (extrapolation), and (b) that the form of the model, a straight line, does not contain the physical constraint that the relative density cannot exceed 100 %. Even in the true function of Data A, the relative density has its maximum at about 1425 °C ($s\approx0.62$) and decreases at higher temperatures.

Exercise 1.4: The number of pairs in self-supervised learning

Solution

(1) A sentence of $T$ words gives $T-1$ pairs, so $(6-1)+(9-1)+(12-1)$ $=5+8+11=24$ pairs.

(2) When predicting the 12th word, the input would originally be words 1 through 11, but since the context length is four words, only the four immediately preceding words (words 8 through 11) become the input. The target value remains the 12th word. The words that serve as target values (words 2 through 12) do not change, so the number of pairs stays at $12-1=11$. What changes is that the input of each pair is truncated to “at most the four preceding words,” so information from five or more words back can no longer be used.

(3) The target values are supplied by the text itself (no person attaches labels). What it shares with supervised learning: there are pairs of inputs and target values, and the mismatch between prediction and target value is minimized as a loss (a classification that picks the next word from the vocabulary). What it shares with unsupervised learning: it uses no labels given from outside and learns the distribution of the data itself (here, the way the words of the text are ordered). The difference is that in supervised learning the target values are prepared separately by experiments or human judgment, whereas in self-supervised learning the target values are created automatically by hiding a part of the data.

Exercise 1.5: The squared loss and the absolute-value loss

Solution

(1) Differentiating $L_2(w)=\tfrac12\sum_n(y_n-w)^{2}$ with respect to $w$ by the chain rule gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{L_2}{w}&=\frac12\sum_{n=1}^{N}2(y_n-w)\times(-1)\\ &=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}(y_n-w)=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}y_n+Nw . \end{aligned} $$

Setting this equal to 0 gives $w=\frac1N\sum_n y_n=\bar{y}$ (the sample mean). The second derivative is $N\gt0$, so this is the minimum. The value is $(95.1+95.4+94.8+95.2+89.0)/5$ $=469.5/5$ $=93.9\ \%$.

(2) $\abs{y_n-w}$ equals $y_n-w$ when $w\lt y_n$ (its derivative with respect to $w$ is $-1$), and $w-y_n$ when $w\gt y_n$ (its derivative is $+1$). Writing $N_{\lt}(w)$ for the number of $y_n$ smaller than $w$ and $N_{\gt}(w)$ for the number of $y_n$ larger than $w$, at any point where $w$ coincides with none of the data values we have

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{L_1}{w}&=N_{\lt}(w)\times(+1)+N_{\gt}(w)\times(-1)\\ &=N_{\lt}(w)-N_{\gt}(w). \end{aligned} $$

Arranging the values in increasing order gives $89.0,\ 94.8,\ 95.1,\ 95.2,\ 95.4$. When $w$ lies between $94.8$ and $95.1$, there are two smaller values and three larger ones, so the slope is $2-3=-1$ ($L_1$ decreases as $w$ increases). When $w$ lies between $95.1$ and $95.2$, there are three smaller values and two larger ones, so the slope is $+1$ (it increases as the variable increases). $L_1$ is a piecewise linear function, and it is minimized at $w=95.1\ \%$, where the slope changes from negative to positive. This is the middle value of the five, that is, the median. In general, the slope changes from negative to positive where the number of smaller values and the number of larger values balance, so the minimum point is the median (when the number of values is even, the minimum is attained anywhere between the two middle values).

(3) The mean of the four runs excluding the one outlier is $(95.1+95.4+94.8+95.2)/4$ $=380.5/4=95.125\ \%$. The median, 95.1 %, nearly agrees with this, but the mean, 93.9 %, is dragged down by the single outlier and is lower by more than 1.2 %. The squared loss penalizes large deviations more heavily, so it amounts to assuming that “the errors are small and uniform, and extreme outliers are rare” (as we will see in Chapter 8, this corresponds to the assumption that the errors follow a Gaussian distribution). The absolute-value loss is robust to outliers and suits data in which “values that occasionally deviate greatly” appear. Choosing a loss is choosing an assumption about the errors in the data.

Exercise 1.6: The curvature of the band gap and descriptors

Solution

(1) We use $E_g(x)=1.20+0.40x+0.80x^{2}$ obtained in Example 1.6 of Chapter 1. $E_g(0)=1.20\ \mathrm{eV}$, $E_g(0.5)=1.20+0.20+0.20=1.60\ \mathrm{eV}$, $E_g(1)=1.20+0.40+0.80=2.40\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(2) $\bar{x}=0.5$ and $\bar{E}=(1.20+1.60+2.40)/3$ $=5.20/3=1.7333\ \mathrm{eV}$. The deviations are $-0.5,0,0.5$ for $x$ and $-0.5333,-0.1333,0.6667$ for $E$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \text{numerator}&=(-0.5)(-0.5333)+0\times(-0.1333)\\ &\quad+(0.5)(0.6667)=0.6\ \mathrm{eV},\\ \text{denominator}&=0.25+0+0.25=0.5,\\ \hat{w}_1&=\frac{0.6}{0.5}=1.20\ \mathrm{eV}, \end{aligned} $$

$\hat{w}_0=1.7333-1.20\times0.5=1.1333\ \mathrm{eV}$. The predicted values at $x=0,0.5,1$ are $1.1333,\ 1.7333,\ 2.3333\ \mathrm{eV}$, and the residuals are $1.20-1.1333=+0.0667$, $1.60-1.7333=-0.1333$, $2.40-2.3333=+0.0667\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(3) The largest residual is $0.133\ \mathrm{eV}$, about 4.4 times the standard deviation of the error, $0.03\ \mathrm{eV}$. Moreover, the residuals have a regular pattern: “positive at both ends, negative in the middle.” This cannot be explained by observation error alone, so the linear model is insufficient (the form of the model cannot express the curvature of the true relationship).

(4) The model $w_0+w_1x+w_2x^{2}$, with $x^{2}$ added to the descriptors, agrees with the true relationship at $(w_0,w_1,w_2)=(1.20,\ 0.40,\ 0.80)\ \mathrm{eV}$, and the residuals at the three points all become 0. However, with three parameters for three points, any three points can be passed through with zero residual. Since actual measured values have an error of 0.03 eV, to check whether the model is correct one must measure sufficiently more points than the number of parameters and look at whether any regular pattern remains in the residuals.

Exercise 1.7: Repeated measurements and data leakage

Solution

(1) Fix one row of interest among the 12 test rows. The remaining 11 test rows are then chosen with equal probability from the 59 rows other than that row. The probability that both of the other two rows of the same sample go into the test set is 11/59 for the first, and, given that it went in, 10/58 for the second, so

$$ \frac{11}{59}\times\frac{10}{58}=\frac{110}{3422}\approx0.032 . $$

Therefore about $1-0.032=0.968$ (97 %) of the test rows have at least one measurement of the same sample among the training rows.

(2) The nearest row is, in most cases, another measurement of the same sample. The model merely returns that value, so the test error is almost equal to “the scatter when the same sample is measured again” (the repeatability error of the measurement). This is not the prediction error for a new sample (a material not yet made), which is much larger. The evaluation becomes optimistic, and data leakage has occurred.

(3) Split by sample, not by row. For example, of the 20 samples, put four (12 rows) in the test set and 16 (48 rows) in the training set, and always put the three rows of the same sample on the same side (a group-wise split; Chapter 6). Furthermore, if samples from the same raw-material lot or the same firing batch are also similar to one another, splitting by lot or by batch gives a more honest estimate of the prediction error for a new lot.

Exercise 1.8: The boundary between exploration and exploitation

Solution

(1) $\mathrm{UCB}(2.2)=-4.00+0.05\kappa$ and $\mathrm{UCB}(6.0)=-4.20+0.40\kappa$. They are equal when $-4.00+0.05\kappa=-4.20+0.40\kappa$, that is, $0.20=0.35\kappa$, so $\kappa=4/7\approx0.571$. If $\kappa\lt4/7$, $x=2.2$ is chosen; if $\kappa\gt4/7$, $x=6.0$ is chosen.

(2) $\mathrm{UCB}(9.0)=-4.70+0.30\kappa$. The difference from $x=6.0$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} &\mathrm{UCB}(6.0)-\mathrm{UCB}(9.0)\\ &\quad=(-4.20+4.70)+(0.40-0.30)\kappa\\ &\quad=0.50+0.10\kappa\gt0 \end{aligned} $$

which is positive for every value $\kappa\ge0$. Since $x=9.0$ is inferior to $x=6.0$ both in the mean and in the width, it is never chosen, however $\kappa$ is selected.

(3) The observed value $-3.95$ was higher than the predicted mean $-4.20$. When this observation is incorporated by Bayesian inference, the predicted mean at $x=6.0$ is pulled up toward the observed value (the standard deviation of the observation error, 0.05, is much smaller than the predictive width, 0.40, so the mean moves to nearly $-3.95$), and the predictive standard deviation shrinks greatly (to about the same size as the observation error). At dopant amounts near $x=6.0$ as well, the mean rises and the width shrinks. As a result, the prospect that the large peak lies in this neighborhood grows stronger, and at the next measurement the chance increases that a not-yet-measured point around 6.0 mol% is chosen. The concrete numbers can be computed with Gaussian process regression (Chapter 12).

Chapter 2 Probability, Expectation, and Conditional Probability — Tools for Counting Uncertainty

Exercise 2.1: Firing atmosphere and cracking

Solution

Let the atmosphere be $x\in\{\text{air},\text{Ar}\}$ and the cracking be $y\in\{\text{cracked},\text{intact}\}$. There are 50 samples fired in air (15 cracked, 35 intact), 100 fired in Ar (10 cracked, 90 intact), and 150 in total.

(1) Divide the counts by 150.

CrackedIntact$p(x)$
Air$15/150=0.100$$35/150\approx0.233$$50/150=1/3$
Ar$10/150\approx0.067$$90/150=0.600$$100/150=2/3$
$p(y)$$25/150=1/6$$125/150=5/6$1

(2) By the sum rule, $p(\text{cracked})=0.100+0.067$ $=25/150=1/6\approx0.167$. By the definition of conditional probability,

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\text{cracked}\mid\text{air})&=\frac{p(\text{air},\text{cracked})}{p(\text{air})}\\ &=\frac{15/150}{50/150}=\frac{15}{50}=0.30,\\ p(\text{air}\mid\text{cracked})&=\frac{15/150}{25/150}=\frac{15}{25}=0.60 . \end{aligned} $$

(3) We have $p(\text{air},\text{cracked})=0.100$, whereas $p(\text{air})\,p(\text{cracked})=\tfrac13\times\tfrac16=\tfrac1{18}\approx0.056$, so they do not agree. Therefore the two are not independent. In terms of conditional probabilities as well, $p(\text{cracked}\mid\text{air})=0.30$ and $p(\text{cracked}\mid\text{Ar})=10/100=0.10$, so the probability of cracking changes with the atmosphere.

(Supplement) In a 2×2 table, if $p(x,y)=p(x)p(y)$ holds in one cell, it holds in the others as well. For example, if it holds in the air–cracked cell, then by the sum rule

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\text{air},\text{intact})&=p(\text{air})-p(\text{air},\text{cracked})\\ &=p(\text{air})-p(\text{air})\,p(\text{cracked})\\ &=p(\text{air})\bigl(1-p(\text{cracked})\bigr)\\ &=p(\text{air})\,p(\text{intact}) \end{aligned} $$

(and likewise for the other two cells).

Exercise 2.2: Judging a secondary phase with two kinds of analysis

Solution

Let $z\in\{\text{present},\text{absent}\}$ denote the presence or absence of a secondary phase, $x_1$ the XRD judgment, and $x_2$ the SEM–EDS judgment ($+$ means “detected”). We have $p(\text{present})=0.10$, $p(x_1=+\mid\text{present})=0.80$, $p(x_1=+\mid\text{absent})=0.10$, $p(x_2=+\mid\text{present})=0.90$, and $p(x_2=+\mid\text{absent})=0.02$.

(1) By Bayes's theorem,

$$ \begin{aligned} &p(\text{present}\mid x_1=+)\\ &=\frac{0.80\times0.10}{0.80\times0.10+0.10\times0.90}\\ &=\frac{0.080}{0.080+0.090}=\frac{0.080}{0.170}\approx0.471 . \end{aligned} $$

(2) By conditional independence, $p(x_1=+,x_2=+\mid z)$ $=p(x_1=+\mid z)\,p(x_2=+\mid z)$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} &p(\text{present}\mid +,+)\\ &=\frac{0.80\times0.90\times0.10}{0.80\times0.90\times0.10+0.10\times0.02\times0.90}\\ &=\frac{0.0720}{0.0720+0.0018}=\frac{0.0720}{0.0738}\approx0.976 . \end{aligned} $$

Using the result of (1), $q_1=0.4706$ ($0.080/0.170$), as the prior probability and updating with the SEM–EDS result gives the same value:

$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{0.90\,q_1}{0.90\,q_1+0.02\,(1-q_1)}&=\frac{0.4235}{0.4235+0.0106}\\ &\approx0.976 . \end{aligned} $$

(3) Since $p(x_2=-\mid\text{present})=0.10$ and $p(x_2=-\mid\text{absent})=0.98$,

$$ \begin{aligned} &p(\text{present}\mid +,-)\\ &=\frac{0.80\times0.10\times0.10}{0.80\times0.10\times0.10+0.10\times0.98\times0.90}\\ &=\frac{0.0080}{0.0080+0.0882}=\frac{0.0080}{0.0962}\approx0.083 . \end{aligned} $$

SEM–EDS has a low false positive rate and a high sensitivity, so its “not detected” is strong evidence, and the probability falls below the prior probability 0.10.

(4) In the formula that updates in one step, the numerator is $p(x_1\mid\text{present})\,p(x_2\mid\text{present})\,p(\text{present})$, and the denominator is the sum of this and $p(x_1\mid\text{absent})\,p(x_2\mid\text{absent})\,p(\text{absent})$. A product does not depend on the order of multiplication, so using SEM–EDS first gives the same result. Indeed, if we use SEM–EDS first, $p(\text{present}\mid x_2=+)$ $=0.090/(0.090+0.018)$ $=0.833$, and then with XRD

$$ \begin{aligned} &\frac{0.80\times0.833}{0.80\times0.833+0.10\times0.167}\\ &=\frac{0.6667}{0.6833}\approx0.976 \end{aligned} $$

which agrees with (2). If conditional independence does not hold (for example, if both methods overlook the aggregation of the same particles), this calculation cannot be used.

Exercise 2.3: The covariance formula

Solution

Let $\mu_x=\Ex[x]$ and $\mu_y=\Ex[y]$.

(1) Expand the product and use the linearity of expectation (expected value) ($\mu_x,\mu_y$ are constants).

$$ \begin{aligned} \Cov[x,y]&=\Ex[xy-\mu_y x-\mu_x y+\mu_x\mu_y]\\ &=\Ex[xy]-\mu_y\mu_x-\mu_x\mu_y+\mu_x\mu_y\\ &=\Ex[xy]-\mu_x\mu_y . \end{aligned} $$

(2) Let $u=ax+by+c$. Then $\Ex[u]=a\mu_x+b\mu_y+c$ and $u-\Ex[u]=a(x-\mu_x)+b(y-\mu_y)$. Hence

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Var[u]\\ &=\Ex\!\left[\bigl(a(x-\mu_x)+b(y-\mu_y)\bigr)^{2}\right]\\ &=a^{2}\Ex[(x-\mu_x)^{2}]+b^{2}\Ex[(y-\mu_y)^{2}]\\ &\quad+2ab\,\Ex[(x-\mu_x)(y-\mu_y)]\\ &=a^{2}\Var[x]+b^{2}\Var[y]+2ab\,\Cov[x,y]. \end{aligned} $$

Setting $a=b=1$, $c=0$ gives the formula in the main text (the variance of a sum). Setting $a=1$, $b=-1$ gives $\Var[x-y]=\Var[x]+\Var[y]-2\Cov[x,y]$.

(3) Let $\bm{\mu}=\Ex[\bm{x}]$. Then $\Ex[\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{x}]=\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{\mu}$ and $\Ex[\bm{b}^{\tp}\bm{x}]=\bm{b}^{\tp}\bm{\mu}$. The scalar $\bm{b}^{\tp}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})$ is unchanged by transposition, so it can be written as $(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\tp}\bm{b}$. Hence

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Cov[\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{x},\bm{b}^{\tp}\bm{x}]\\ &=\Ex\!\left[\bm{a}^{\tp}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\tp}\bm{b}\right]\\ &=\bm{a}^{\tp}\,\Ex\!\left[(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\tp}\right]\bm{b}\\ &=\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{b}. \end{aligned} $$

In components this is $\sum_{i}\sum_{j}a_i b_j\Cov[x_i,x_j]$; we have only taken the constants outside the expectation. Setting $\bm{b}=\bm{a}$ gives $\Var[\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{x}]=\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{a}$ of Theorem 2.7 in Chapter 2.

Exercise 2.4: The correlation coefficient lies between −1 and 1

Solution

In Exercise 2.3 (2), let $a=1/\sigma_x$ and $b=\pm1/\sigma_y$. Since $\Var[x]/\sigma_x^{2}=1$, $\Var[y]/\sigma_y^{2}=1$, and $\Cov[x,y]/(\sigma_x\sigma_y)=\rho$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[u]&=\Var\!\left[\frac{x}{\sigma_x}+\frac{y}{\sigma_y}\right]\\ &=1+1+2\rho=2(1+\rho),\\ \Var[v]&=\Var\!\left[\frac{x}{\sigma_x}-\frac{y}{\sigma_y}\right]=2(1-\rho). \end{aligned} $$

A variance is nonnegative, so $1+\rho\ge0$ and $1-\rho\ge0$; that is, $-1\le\rho\le1$.

When $\rho=1$, $\Var[v]=0$. A random variable with variance 0 equals its mean with probability 1, so $x/\sigma_x-y/\sigma_y$ equals the constant $c=\mu_x/\sigma_x-\mu_y/\sigma_y$ with probability 1. That is, $y=\dfrac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x}x+\text{constant}$, which is the case where $y$ is a linear function of $x$ with positive slope (all the points lie on a line rising to the right). Similarly, $\rho=-1$ is the case where the points lie on a line with negative slope.

(Alternative solution) The 2×2 covariance matrix $\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_x^{2}&\rho\sigma_x\sigma_y\\\rho\sigma_x\sigma_y&\sigma_y^{2}\end{pmatrix}$ is positive semidefinite, so its determinant is nonnegative: $\sigma_x^{2}\sigma_y^{2}(1-\rho^{2})\ge0$, and hence $\rho^{2}\le1$.

Exercise 2.5: From the grain radius to the distribution of the volume

Solution

Take the unit of length to be $\mu\mathrm{m}$ and the unit of volume to be $\mu\mathrm{m}^{3}$. We have $p_r(r)=1$ ($1\le r\le2$).

(1) $V=\tfrac43\pi r^{3}$ is an increasing function, and $V$ ranges from $V_1=\tfrac43\pi\approx4.19$ to $V_2=\tfrac{32}{3}\pi\approx33.5$. The inverse function is $r=\left(\dfrac{3V}{4\pi}\right)^{1/3}$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{r}{V}&=\frac13\left(\frac{3V}{4\pi}\right)^{-2/3}\cdot\frac{3}{4\pi}\\ &=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\frac{3V}{4\pi}\right)^{-2/3}=\frac{1}{4\pi r^{2}} . \end{aligned} $$

By the change-of-variables formula,

$$ \begin{gathered} p_V(V)=p_r(r)\,\abs{\diff{r}{V}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\frac{3V}{4\pi}\right)^{-2/3}\\ \left(\tfrac43\pi\le V\le\tfrac{32}{3}\pi\right). \end{gathered} $$

The density is $1/(4\pi)\approx0.0796\ \mu\mathrm{m}^{-3}$ at $V_1$ and $1/(16\pi)\approx0.0199\ \mu\mathrm{m}^{-3}$ at $V_2$; the larger the volume, the more thinly the density is stretched out. For normalization, changing the integration variable back from $V$ to $r$ gives $\dd V=4\pi r^{2}\dd r$, so $\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p_V\,\dd V=\int_1^2\frac{1}{4\pi r^{2}}\cdot4\pi r^{2}\,\dd r=\int_1^2\dd r=1$.

(2) Using $p_r(r)=1$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[V]&=\int_1^2\tfrac43\pi r^{3}\,\dd r=\tfrac43\pi\cdot\frac{2^{4}-1^{4}}{4}\\ &=\tfrac43\pi\cdot\frac{15}{4}=5\pi\approx15.71\ \mu\mathrm{m}^{3}. \end{aligned} $$

On the other hand, from $\Ex[r]=1.5\ \mu\mathrm{m}$ we get $\tfrac43\pi(1.5)^{3}$ $=\tfrac43\pi\times3.375$ $=4.5\pi\approx14.14\ \mu\mathrm{m}^{3}$.

(3) The volume computed from the mean grain size underestimates the mean volume by about 10% ($4.5\pi$ versus $5\pi$). Since $r^{3}$ is a convex function, the effect by which large grains pull the mean volume up is larger than the effect by which small grains pull it down (in general, for a convex $f$, $\Ex[f(x)]\ge f(\Ex[x])$; this is Jensen's inequality, Appendix A, A.9). For a quantity that undergoes a nonlinear transformation, “the transform of the mean” differs from “the mean of the transform”; this is also a limitation of the first-order approximation of the propagation of error in this chapter.

Exercise 2.6: The area under the likelihood

Solution

Using the beta function,

$$ \begin{aligned} \int_0^1\theta^{m}(1-\theta)^{N-m}\,\dd\theta&=B(m+1,N-m+1)\\ &=\frac{m!\,(N-m)!}{(N+1)!}. \end{aligned} $$

Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} \int_0^1 L(\theta)\,\dd\theta&=\frac{N!}{m!\,(N-m)!}\cdot\frac{m!\,(N-m)!}{(N+1)!}\\ &=\frac{N!}{(N+1)!}=\frac{1}{N+1}. \end{aligned} $$

For $N=10$ and $m=7$ this is $1/11$, in agreement with Example 2.9 in the main text.

Meaning: the fact that the result does not depend on $m$ says that, when $\theta$ is chosen from the uniform distribution on $[0,1]$ and $N$ trials are made with that $\theta$, each number of successes $m=0,1,\dots,N$ occurs with the same probability $1/(N+1)$ ($\int_0^1 p(m\mid\theta)\cdot1\,\dd\theta=p(m)$ is the continuous version of the sum rule). This is the simplest example of the marginal likelihood $p(\data)$ treated in Chapter 4, Section 4.3. The area under the likelihood is not 1, so the likelihood cannot be regarded as a distribution of $\theta$. To obtain a distribution of $\theta$, we must multiply by the prior distribution and divide by this area (Bayes's theorem).

For 3 successes in 3 trials, $L(\theta)=\binom33\theta^{3}(1-\theta)^{0}=\theta^{3}$. Since this is an increasing function on $[0,1]$, the maximum is at $\theta=1$ (the maximum likelihood estimate $m/N=1$). $L(0.5)=0.125$. With only three data points, judging by the likelihood alone leads to the extreme conclusion that “it always succeeds.” This is the motivation for introducing the prior distribution in Chapter 4.

Exercise 2.7: Repeated measurements of hardness

Solution

(1) The sum is $612+598+631+605$ $+620+589+617+608$ $=4880$, so $\bar{x}=4880/8=610\ \mathrm{HV}$. The deviations from the mean are $2,\,-12,\,21,\,-5,\,10,\,-21,\,7,\,-2$, and the sum of their squares is

$$ \begin{aligned} &4+144+441+25\\ &+100+441+49+4=1208 . \end{aligned} $$

$s^{2}=1208/(8-1)\approx172.6\ \mathrm{HV^{2}}$ and $s\approx13.1\ \mathrm{HV}$. The standard error is $s/\sqrt{8}\approx13.14/2.828\approx4.6\ \mathrm{HV}$. The report is written as, for example, “$610\pm5\ \mathrm{HV}$ (mean of 8 measurements ± standard error; scatter of a single measurement $s=13\ \mathrm{HV}$).”

(2) From $s/\sqrt{N}\le3$ we get $N\ge(s/3)^{2}=(13.14/3)^{2}\approx19.2$. Therefore 20 measurements are needed.

(3) It must not be reported as it stands (at least, it cannot be called, as it stands, the “uncertainty of the hardness of the material”). $s/\sqrt{N}$ presupposes that the 8 measurements are independent samples from the “distribution of the hardness of the material.” Values measured within a narrow region of a single sample share that sample's local microstructure and its fabrication variability (batch), and are correlated with one another. As in Example 2.12 of Section 2.10, when there is correlation the standard error is underestimated, and no matter how many times a single sample is measured, the variability between samples and between batches does not become visible. Report $s/\sqrt{N}$ as “the uncertainty of the mean hardness of this region of this sample”; to state a value for the material, we must measure several samples (preferably from several batches).

Exercise 2.8: The number of batches and the number of samples

Solution

(1) Plan A: let $\bar{x}_b$ be the mean of the 4 samples in batch $b$ ($b=1,\dots,5$). By the result of Example 2.12 with $N=4$, $\Var[\bar{x}_b]=\dfrac{\sigma^{2}}{4}\bigl[1+3\rho\bigr]$. The overall mean is $\bar{x}=\frac15\sum_b\bar{x}_b$, and since the batch means are independent of one another,

$$ \Var[\bar{x}]=\frac{1}{5^{2}}\cdot5\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}(1+3\rho)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{20}(1+3\rho). $$

(2) For $\rho=0.5$, $\Var[\bar{x}]=\sigma^{2}\times2.5/20=\sigma^{2}/8$. Comparing with the variance $\sigma^{2}/N_{\mathrm{eff}}$ of the mean of $N_{\mathrm{eff}}$ independent measurements gives $N_{\mathrm{eff}}=8$. Even though 20 samples are measured, there is only as much information as in 8 independent measurements.

(3) Plan B has $\Var[\bar{x}]=\sigma^{2}/20$, which gives the smallest uncertainty from the same 20 measurements. Moreover, since all 20 samples come from different batches, the variability between batches (the reproducibility of fabrication) is included as it is in the scatter of the samples, so there is no risk of underestimating the variability of the material. The drawbacks are that making the batches costs four times as much money and time, and that we cannot estimate “the variability within the same batch” and “the variability between batches” separately. When there are several samples in one batch, as in Plan A, the two can be estimated separately (decomposition of variance into components).
From the standpoint of machine learning, if the data of Plan A are split at random into training data and test data sample by sample, samples from the same batch end up in both, and the test performance looks better than the true ability (data leakage; Chapter 6, Section 6.8). If the data of Plan A are used, the split must be made batch by batch. With Plan B this problem is unlikely to arise, but since there is one sample per batch, the reproducibility of measurements within a batch is unknown. If the cost allows, a design that “increases the number of batches as much as possible and measures a few samples within each” is advantageous for both the precision of the mean and the prevention of leakage.

Chapter 3 Common Probability Distributions — Patterns of Variability in Data

Exercise 3.1: Binomial distribution of the number of defectives and its Poisson approximation

Solution

We have $N=100$ and $\mu=0.02$.

(1) Using the binomial distribution (the form $\binom{N}{m}\mu^{m}(1-\mu)^{N-m}$ of Eq. (3.3) in Chapter 3),

$$ \begin{aligned} P(m=0)&=0.98^{100}=\exp(100\ln0.98)\\ &=\exp(-2.0203)\approx0.1326, \end{aligned} $$ $$ P(m=1)=100\times0.02\times0.98^{99}\approx0.2707, $$ $$ \begin{aligned} P(m=2)&=\binom{100}{2}0.02^{2}\,0.98^{98}\\ &=4950\times0.0004\times0.98^{98}\\ &\approx0.2734 . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $P(m\le2)$ $\approx0.1326+0.2707+0.2734$ $=0.6767$.

(2) In the Poisson approximation, $\lambda=N\mu=2$, so

$$ P(m=0)=e^{-2}\approx0.1353, $$ $$ \begin{aligned} P(m\le2)&=e^{-2}\left(1+2+\frac{2^{2}}{2}\right)\\ &=5e^{-2}\approx0.6767 . \end{aligned} $$

The probability of zero defectives differs by about 2 % (0.1326 versus 0.1353), but the probabilities of two or fewer almost coincide (the differences in the individual terms cancel). Since $\mu=0.02$ is sufficiently small, the Poisson approximation works well.

(3) The variance of the binomial distribution is $N\mu(1-\mu)=100\times0.02\times0.98=1.96$, and the variance of the Poisson distribution is $\lambda=2$. Their ratio is $1-\mu=0.98$, so they are almost the same.

Exercise 3.2: Properties of the Poisson distribution

Solution

(1) The terms with $m=0,1,2$ vanish because $m(m-1)(m-2)=0$, so we sum from $m=3$ and use $m(m-1)(m-2)/m!=1/(m-3)!$.

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Ex[m(m-1)(m-2)]\\ &=\sum_{m=3}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{m}e^{-\lambda}}{(m-3)!}\\ &=\lambda^{3}e^{-\lambda}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{j}}{j!}\\ &=\lambda^{3}e^{-\lambda}e^{\lambda}=\lambda^{3}. \end{aligned} $$

(In the third line we put $j=m-3$.) In general, $\Ex[m(m-1)\cdots(m-r+1)]=\lambda^{r}$.

(2) The event $m_1+m_2=n$ occurs exactly when $m_1=j$ and $m_2=n-j$ for some $j=0,\dots,n$, and these cases are mutually exclusive. By independence, the probability of each case is a product, so

$$ \begin{aligned} &P(m_1+m_2=n)\\ &=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\lambda_1^{j}e^{-\lambda_1}}{j!}\cdot\frac{\lambda_2^{n-j}e^{-\lambda_2}}{(n-j)!}\\ &=\frac{e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)}}{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{n!\,\lambda_1^{j}\lambda_2^{n-j}}{j!\,(n-j)!}\\ &=\frac{e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)}}{n!}(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)^{n}. \end{aligned} $$

In the last step we used the binomial theorem. This is $\mathrm{Pois}(n\mid\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$.

(3) The count over 20 seconds is the sum of the count $m_1$ over the first 10 seconds and the count $m_2$ over the last 10 seconds. If events occur independently at a constant rate, $m_1$ and $m_2$ are independent and both follow $\mathrm{Pois}(\lambda)$. By (2), the sum follows $\mathrm{Pois}(2\lambda)$. Both the mean and the variance are $2\lambda$, and the relative error decreases to $1/\sqrt{2\lambda}$, that is, by a factor of $1/\sqrt2$.

Exercise 3.3: Measurement time in XPS

Solution

(1) The count over a time $t$ (s) follows a Poisson distribution with mean $80t$ at the peak position and mean $30t$ in the background. The mean of the net intensity is $(80-30)t=50t$, and since the two counts are independent, the variance is the sum $80t+30t=110t$. The relative error is

$$ \frac{\sqrt{110t}}{50t}=\frac{\sqrt{110}}{50\sqrt{t}}\approx\frac{0.2098}{\sqrt{t}} . $$

(2) From $\sqrt{110}/(50\sqrt t)\le0.01$ we get $\sqrt t\ge\sqrt{110}/0.5\approx20.98$, so $t\ge440\ \mathrm{s}$ (440 s each for the peak and the background, 880 s in total).

(3) Without a background, the mean is $50t$, the variance is $50t$, and the relative error is $1/\sqrt{50t}$. From $1/\sqrt{50t}\le0.01$ we get $t\ge200\ \mathrm{s}$.

Although the net intensity is the same $50\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, the presence of a background requires 2.2 times as long at each position, and 4.4 times as long when the background measurement is included. The background increases only the variance without increasing the signal, so conditions with a low background (the measurement geometry and the choice of excitation source) directly shorten the measurement time.

Exercise 3.4: Marginalization and regression for a two-dimensional Gaussian distribution

Solution

(1) Setting $\rho=0$ in the decomposition of Chapter 3, Section 3.6.4, factor (A) becomes $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_a}\exp(-u^{2}/2)=\Normal(x_a\mid\mu_a,\sigma_a^{2})$, which does not depend on $x_b$. Therefore $p(x_a,x_b)$ $=\Normal(x_a\mid\mu_a,\sigma_a^{2})\,\Normal(x_b\mid\mu_b,\sigma_b^{2})$ $=p(x_a)p(x_b)$, and the variables are independent. For a Gaussian distribution, uncorrelated implies independent (this does not hold for general distributions; see Chapter 2, Section 2.5).

(2) Interchanging $a$ and $b$ gives

$$ \Ex[x_b\mid x_a]=\mu_b+\rho\frac{\sigma_b}{\sigma_a}(x_a-\mu_a), $$

whose slope in the $(x_a,x_b)$ plane is $\dd x_b/\dd x_a=\rho\sigma_b/\sigma_a$. On the other hand, solving the line $\Ex[x_a\mid x_b]=\mu_a+\rho\frac{\sigma_a}{\sigma_b}(x_b-\mu_b)$ for $x_b$, the slope is $\dd x_b/\dd x_a=\sigma_b/(\rho\sigma_a)$. The ratio of the two slopes is $\rho^{2}$, and for $0\lt\abs{\rho}\lt1$ they do not coincide (both lines pass through the point $(\mu_a,\mu_b)$ but have different slopes).

(3) $\rho\sigma_b/\sigma_a=0.8\times30/1.5=16\ \mathrm{HV}/\%$, and $\sigma_b/(\rho\sigma_a)=30/(0.8\times1.5)=25\ \mathrm{HV}/\%$.

The line that predicts hardness from density (slope 16) is flatter than the line that predicts density from hardness (slope 25). In both predictions, when the conditioning quantity deviates from its mean by $k$ standard deviations, the predicted quantity deviates by only $\rho k$ standard deviations (smaller than $k$ because $\abs{\rho}\lt1$). This is called regression to the mean. Unless the correlation is perfect, the best prediction is “if one is extreme, the other is probably not so extreme,” and as a result the two lines do not coincide.

Exercise 3.5: Mahalanobis distance and the probability inside an ellipse

Solution

(1) From the eigenvalue equation $\det(\bm{\Sigma}-\lambda\bm{I})$ $=(5-\lambda)(2-\lambda)-4$ $=\lambda^{2}-7\lambda+6=0$ we get $\lambda_1=6$ and $\lambda_2=1$.

For $\lambda_1=6$: $(5-6)u_1+2u_2=0$ gives $u_1=2u_2$, and the unit vector is $\bm{u}_1=(2,1)^{\tp}/\sqrt5$. For $\lambda_2=1$: $4u_1+2u_2=0$ gives $u_2=-2u_1$, and $\bm{u}_2=(1,-2)^{\tp}/\sqrt5$ (the sign of either may be reversed). Since $\bm{u}_1^{\tp}\bm{u}_2=(2-2)/5=0$, they are orthogonal.

The ellipse $\Delta=1$ has its major axis, of radius $\sqrt6\approx2.45$, along $\bm{u}_1$ (the direction of slope $1/2$), and its minor axis, of radius 1, along $\bm{u}_2$.

(2) Since $\abs{\bm{\Sigma}}=5\times2-2\times2=6$, we have $\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}=\dfrac16\begin{pmatrix}2&-2\\-2&5\end{pmatrix}$.

For $\bm{x}=(2,1)^{\tp}$: $\Delta^{2}=\dfrac16(2\cdot4-2\cdot2\cdot2\cdot1+5\cdot1)$ $=\dfrac{8-8+5}{6}=\dfrac56\approx0.833$.

For $\bm{x}=(1,-2)^{\tp}$: $\Delta^{2}=\dfrac16\bigl(2\cdot1-2\cdot2\cdot1\cdot(-2)$ $+5\cdot4\bigr)=\dfrac{2+8+20}{6}=5$.

Both points have the same ordinary distance $\sqrt5$ from the origin, but $(2,1)^{\tp}$ lies along the major axis ($\sqrt5\,\bm{u}_1$), so $\Delta^{2}=5/6$ is small, whereas $(1,-2)^{\tp}$ lies along the minor axis ($\sqrt5\,\bm{u}_2$), so $\Delta^{2}=5$ is large. This is also confirmed with $\Delta^{2}=\sum_iy_i^{2}/\lambda_i$ of Eq. (3.13) in Chapter 3: from $y_1=\sqrt5$, $y_2=0$ we get $5/6$, and from $y_1=0$, $y_2=\sqrt5$ we get $5/1$.

(3) By Chapter 3, Section 3.6.2, $y_1,y_2$ are independent and $y_i\sim\Normal(0,\lambda_i)$. Putting $z_i=y_i/\sqrt{\lambda_i}$, the variables $z_1,z_2$ are independent and follow the standard Gaussian distribution, and $\Delta^{2}=z_1^{2}+z_2^{2}$. Using polar coordinates $z_1=r\cos\theta$, $z_2=r\sin\theta$ in the $z$ plane (Jacobian determinant $r$), we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} P(\Delta\le c)&=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\int_{0}^{c}\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-r^{2}/2}\,r\,\dd r\,\dd\theta\\ &=\Bigl[-e^{-r^{2}/2}\Bigr]_{0}^{c}=1-e^{-c^{2}/2}. \end{aligned} $$

From $1-e^{-c^{2}/2}=0.95$ we get $c^{2}=-2\ln0.05=5.991$, so $c\approx2.45$. In two dimensions, the ellipse containing 95 % has $\Delta\approx2.45$, which is larger than the one-variable value 1.96.

Exercise 3.6: Weighted average and median for the Cauchy distribution

Solution

(1) We use the characteristic function $\varphi(k)=\exp(ix_0k-\gamma\abs{k})$ (Chapter 3, Section 3.7.3). By the independence of $x_1,x_2$,

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Ex\!\left[e^{ik(wx_1+(1-w)x_2)}\right]\\ &=\varphi(wk)\,\varphi((1-w)k)\\ &=e^{ix_0wk-\gamma w\abs{k}}\\ &\quad\times e^{ix_0(1-w)k-\gamma(1-w)\abs{k}}\\ &=e^{ix_0k-\gamma\abs{k}} . \end{aligned} $$

Since $w\ge0$ and $1-w\ge0$, we used $\abs{wk}=w\abs{k}$ and $\abs{(1-w)k}=(1-w)\abs{k}$. The result is exactly the characteristic function of the original Cauchy distribution, so the weighted average also follows the same Cauchy distribution $(x_0,\gamma)$.

(2) Applying (1) repeatedly, any weighted average $\sum_jw_jx_j$ with nonnegative weights summing to 1 also follows the same Cauchy distribution. That is, no matter how many data points we average or how we weight them, the spread of the estimate of the center $x_0$ (the half width at half maximum $\gamma$) is no better than that of a single data point. For this distribution, the operation of averaging itself gathers no information.

(3) From the cumulative distribution function $F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\arctan\frac{x-x_0}{\gamma}$, the median is $x_0$, where $F=1/2$, the quartiles are $x_0\mp\gamma$, where $F=1/4,3/4$, and the interquartile range is $2\gamma$. The median $\tilde x$ of three observations satisfies $P(\tilde x\le x)=3F^{2}-2F^{3}$, from the probability that “at least two of the three are at most $x$.” For $x_0=0$ and $\gamma=1$, solving $3F^{2}-2F^{3}=1/4$ gives $F=0.3264$, and $x=\tan(\pi(F-1/2))=-0.607$, so the interquartile range of $\tilde x$ is $1.21\gamma$, which is narrower than that of the sample mean (which remains $2\gamma$). Moreover, the tail of the density of $\tilde x$ decays as $\abs{x}^{-3}$ (even if one extreme value appears, the median is determined by one of the remaining two). In this way the median is not swayed by a single huge outlier, and it concentrates around $x_0$ as the sample grows.

Exercise 3.7: Finding the Weibull modulus from a Weibull plot

Solution

(1) $F_i=(i-0.5)/8$ takes the values $0.0625$, $0.1875$, $0.3125$, $0.4375$, $0.5625$, $0.6875$, $0.8125$, $0.9375$.

$i$$s_i$ / MPa$X_i=\ln s_i$$Y_i$
13065.7236−2.7405
23225.7746−1.5720
33365.8171−0.9816
43435.8377−0.5528
53695.9108−0.1903
63935.97380.1511
73965.98140.5152
84136.02341.0198

For example, for $i=1$: $1/(1-0.0625)=1.0667$, $\ln1.0667=0.06454$, and $\ln0.06454=-2.7405$.

(2) The means are $\bar X=5.8803$ and $\bar Y=-0.5439$. The sums of products and of squares of the deviations are $S_{XY}=\sum_i(X_i-\bar X)(Y_i-\bar Y)=0.8877$ and $S_{XX}=\sum_i(X_i-\bar X)^{2}=0.08194$.

$$ \hat m=\frac{S_{XY}}{S_{XX}}=\frac{0.8877}{0.08194}\approx10.83, $$ $$ \begin{aligned} c&=\bar Y-\hat m\bar X\\ &=-0.5439-10.83\times5.8803\\ &\approx-64.25 . \end{aligned} $$

The intercept of the line in Eq. (3.25) of Chapter 3 (the Weibull plot line) is $-m\ln s_0$, so $\ln\hat s_0=-c/\hat m=5.9305$ and $\hat s_0=e^{5.9305}\approx376\ \mathrm{MPa}$.

(3) The values used for generation were $m=10$ and $s_0=400\ \mathrm{MPa}$, and the estimates $\hat m=10.8$ and $\hat s_0=376\ \mathrm{MPa}$ are reasonably close. With only eight specimens, however, the random variation is large. If the same procedure is repeated thousands of times, drawing eight specimens each time from a Weibull distribution with $m=10$, $\hat m$ scatters over roughly the range 5–20 (95 %). A value of $m$ obtained from eight specimens is accurate only to “around 10,” and a reliability evaluation calls for several tens of test specimens. That $\hat s_0$ is smaller than 400 is also because these eight specimens happened to be on the weak side.

(4) $F(300)=1-\exp[-(300/376.3)^{10.83}]$. Since $(300/376.3)^{10.83}$ $=\exp(10.83\times\ln0.7972)$ $=\exp(10.83\times(-0.2266))$ $=\exp(-2.454)$ $=0.0859$, we get $F(300)=1-e^{-0.0859}\approx0.082$. About 8 % are estimated to break at 300 MPa. However, as noted in (3), the uncertainty in $\hat m$ is large, so this value also carries a large uncertainty.

Exercise 3.8: Fitting a Lorentzian peak with a Gaussian

Solution

(1) The integral of the Lorentzian $h\gamma^{2}/((x-x_0)^{2}+\gamma^{2})$ is $\pi\gamma h$, from the normalization of the Cauchy distribution ($\int\frac1\pi\frac{\gamma}{y^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\dd y=1$). A Gaussian with the same full width at half maximum $2\gamma$ satisfies $2\sqrt{2\ln2}\,\sigma=2\gamma$, so $\sigma=\gamma/\sqrt{2\ln2}=0.8493\gamma$, and the integral of a Gaussian of height $h$ is $\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma h$. The ratio is

$$ \frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma h}{\pi\gamma h}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\pi\sqrt{2\ln2}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\ln2}}\approx0.678 . $$

The Gaussian underestimates the integrated intensity by about 32 %.

(2) Lorentzian: the fraction inside $\pm3\gamma$ is $\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan3=0.7952$, so the fraction outside is $0.205$ (20.5 %). Gaussian: $3\gamma=3\times\sqrt{2\ln2}\,\sigma=3.53\sigma$, so the fraction outside is $2[1-\Phi(3.53)]\approx4.1\times10^{-4}$ (0.04 %).

(3) Only the peak of the phase with a strongly Lorentzian character is underestimated, by about 30 %, so the fraction of that phase is estimated to be smaller and that of the other phase larger. For example, if the true ratio of integrated intensities is 1 : 1, the apparent ratio becomes $0.678:1$, and the fraction of that phase comes out as $0.678/1.678\approx40$ % rather than 50 % (the factor that converts an intensity ratio into a fraction is taken to be the same for both). One should fit the peaks of both phases with the same shape (for example, a Voigt profile or a pseudo-Voigt function) and check the tails of the residuals on a logarithmic vertical axis.

Chapter 4 Maximum Likelihood, MAP, and Bayesian Estimation — Learning a Distribution from Data

Exercise 4.1: Posterior distribution and point estimates

Solution

The prior distribution is $\mathrm{Beta}(a,b)=\mathrm{Beta}(3,2)$, with $N=10$, $m=4$ successes, and $l=6$ failures.

(1) By conjugacy (Theorem 4.3 of Chapter 4), the posterior distribution is $\mathrm{Beta}(\mu\mid a+m,\ b+l)=\mathrm{Beta}(\mu\mid 7,\ 8)$.

(2) The maximum likelihood estimate is $m/N=4/10=0.4$. Since $A=7\gt1$ and $B=8\gt1$, the formula for the interior maximum point applies to the MAP estimate:

$$ \mu_{\mathrm{MAP}}=\frac{A-1}{A+B-2}=\frac{6}{13}\approx0.4615 . $$

The posterior mean is $\dfrac{A}{A+B}=\dfrac{7}{15}\approx0.4667$.

(3) $w=\dfrac{N}{a+b+N}=\dfrac{10}{15}=\dfrac23$. The prior mean is $\dfrac{a}{a+b}=\dfrac35=0.6$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} &w\,\frac{m}{N}+(1-w)\,\frac{a}{a+b}\\ &\quad=\frac23\times0.4+\frac13\times0.6\\ &\quad=\frac{0.8+0.6}{3}=\frac{1.4}{3}\approx0.4667 \end{aligned} $$

which agrees with the posterior mean in (2).

(4) By the formula for the variance of the beta distribution,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[\mu\mid\data]&=\frac{AB}{(A+B)^{2}(A+B+1)}\\ &=\frac{7\times8}{15^{2}\times16}=\frac{56}{3600}\\ &\approx0.01556 , \end{aligned} $$

and the standard deviation is $\sqrt{0.01556}\approx0.125$.

(5) The posterior predictive probability equals the posterior mean: $p(x=1\mid\data)=7/15\approx0.467$.

Seen from the data (0.4), the prior distribution (mean 0.6) lies above, so the posterior mean and the MAP estimate are pulled back to the side larger than the maximum likelihood estimate 0.4.

Exercise 4.2: How fast does the posterior distribution narrow?

Solution

Let $A=a+m=a+\hat\mu N$, $B=b+l=b+(1-\hat\mu)N$, and $A+B=a+b+N$. The variance of the beta distribution is

$$ \Var[\mu\mid\data]=\frac{AB}{(A+B)^{2}(A+B+1)} . $$

Expanding the numerator gives

$$ \begin{aligned} AB={}&\hat\mu(1-\hat\mu)N^{2}\\ &+\bigl[a(1-\hat\mu)+b\hat\mu\bigr]N+ab \end{aligned} $$

and the highest-order term in $N$ is $\hat\mu(1-\hat\mu)N^{2}$. The denominator is $(a+b+N)^{2}(a+b+N+1)$ $=N^{3}+(\text{terms of order $N^{2}$ or lower})$. Dividing the numerator and denominator by $N^{3}$ and letting $N\to\infty$, we get

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[\mu\mid\data]&=\frac{\hat\mu(1-\hat\mu)N^{2}+O(N)}{N^{3}+O(N^{2})}\\ &=\frac{\hat\mu(1-\hat\mu)}{N}\bigl(1+O(1/N)\bigr) . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\Var[\mu\mid\data]\approx\hat\mu(1-\hat\mu)/N$, and the standard deviation of the posterior distribution decreases at the rate $1/\sqrt{N}$.

Meaning: when $N$ trials with success rate $\mu$ are performed, the number of successes $m$ follows a binomial distribution with $\Var[m]=N\mu(1-\mu)$, so the variance of the maximum likelihood estimator $m/N$ is $\mu(1-\mu)/N$ (Chapter 3). When the data are plentiful, “the variability of the maximum likelihood estimate when the data are collected again” and “our ignorance about $\mu$ from a single data set (the width of the posterior distribution)” become about the same in magnitude. For this reason, when the data are sufficiently plentiful, the Bayesian credible interval and the frequentist confidence interval nearly coincide numerically. When the data are few, the contributions of $a,b$ remain, and the two do not coincide.

Exercise 4.3: The count rate of the XRD background

Solution

$N=5$ and $\sum_n x_n=12+9+15+11+13=60$.

(1) Because the observations are independent, the likelihood is a product.

$$ p(\data\mid\lambda)=\prod_{n=1}^{5}\frac{\lambda^{x_n}e^{-\lambda}}{x_n!}=\frac{\lambda^{\sum_n x_n}e^{-N\lambda}}{\prod_n x_n!} . $$

Taking the logarithm gives $\ln p=\left(\sum_n x_n\right)\ln\lambda-N\lambda-\sum_n\ln x_n!$. Differentiate with respect to $\lambda$ and set the result to 0.

$$ \begin{gathered} \diff{}{\lambda}\ln p=\frac{\sum_n x_n}{\lambda}-N=0\\ \Longrightarrow\quad\lambda_{\mathrm{ML}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_n x_n=\frac{60}{5}=12 . \end{gathered} $$

The second derivative $-\sum_n x_n/\lambda^{2}\lt0$, so this is a maximum. As a count rate, $12$ counts per 10 s $=1.2$ counts per second.

(2) The posterior distribution is proportional (∝) to the likelihood × the prior distribution; dropping factors that do not depend on $\lambda$, we get

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\lambda\mid\data)&\propto\lambda^{60}e^{-5\lambda}\times\lambda^{2-1}e^{-0.1\lambda}\\ &=\lambda^{61}e^{-5.1\lambda}=\lambda^{62-1}e^{-5.1\lambda}. \end{aligned} $$

This has the form of a gamma distribution $\mathrm{Gam}(\lambda\mid a',b')$ with $a'=a+\sum_n x_n=62$ and $b'=b+N=5.1$, so the posterior distribution is again a gamma distribution (the gamma distribution is the conjugate prior of the Poisson distribution). The posterior mean is $a'/b'=62/5.1\approx12.16$, and the mode is $(a'-1)/b'=61/5.1\approx11.96$ (the mode is obtained by differentiating $(a'-1)\ln\lambda-b'\lambda$ and setting the result to 0; since $a'\gt1$, it is an interior maximum). The posterior standard deviation is $\sqrt{a'}/b'=\sqrt{62}/5.1\approx1.54$.

(3) The posterior mean can be written as $\dfrac{a+\sum x_n}{b+N}=w\,\bar{x}+(1-w)\,\dfrac{a}{b}$ with $w=\dfrac{N}{b+N}=\dfrac{5}{5.1}\approx0.980$ (the prior mean is $a/b=20$). Indeed $0.980\times12+0.020\times20\approx12.16$ agrees. The weight of the prior distribution is about 2%, a weak prior distribution equivalent to “0.1 of a 10 s observation.” The posterior mean moves only 0.16 from the maximum likelihood estimate 12, which is small compared with the posterior standard deviation 1.54. Therefore the influence of the prior distribution is practically negligible.

Exercise 4.4: Sequential updating of the mean of a Gaussian distribution

Solution

Take the posterior distribution $\Normal(\mu\mid\mu_k,\sigma_k^{2})$ after $k$ observations as the prior distribution, and update it with one observation $x_{k+1}$ (likelihood $\Normal(x_{k+1}\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})$). This is the case of Theorem 4.5 of Chapter 4 with $N=1$, $\mu_0\to\mu_k$, $\sigma_0^{2}\to\sigma_k^{2}$, and $\bar{x}\to x_{k+1}$, so

$$ \begin{gathered} \frac{1}{\sigma_{k+1}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sigma_k^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}},\\ \frac{\mu_{k+1}}{\sigma_{k+1}^{2}}=\frac{\mu_k}{\sigma_k^{2}}+\frac{x_{k+1}}{\sigma^{2}} . \end{gathered} $$

(The second equation is the formula for the mean in Theorem 4.5 with both sides divided by $\sigma_{k+1}^{2}$.) Using the precision $\pi_k\equiv1/\sigma_k^{2}$ and the precision × mean $h_k\equiv\mu_k/\sigma_k^{2}$, the updates become the simple additions $\pi_{k+1}=\pi_k+1/\sigma^{2}$ and $h_{k+1}=h_k+x_{k+1}/\sigma^{2}$. Starting from $k=0$ ($\pi_0=1/\sigma_0^{2}$, $h_0=\mu_0/\sigma_0^{2}$) and repeating $N$ times,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pi_N&=\frac{1}{\sigma_0^{2}}+\frac{N}{\sigma^{2}},\\ h_N&=\frac{\mu_0}{\sigma_0^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}x_n\\ &=\frac{\mu_0}{\sigma_0^{2}}+\frac{N\bar{x}}{\sigma^{2}} . \end{aligned} $$

$\sigma_N^{2}=1/\pi_N$ and $\mu_N=h_N/\pi_N=\sigma_N^{2}h_N$ are the same as the batch formula (Eq. (4.18) of Theorem 4.5 in Chapter 4). Since the order of addition does not change the result, the final result is the same even if the order of the observations is permuted.

Exercise 4.5: A literature value that is too strong

Solution

(1) The prior precision is $1/\sigma_0^{2}=1/0.0025=400\ \mathrm{pm^{-2}}$, and the data precision is $N/\sigma^{2}=9/0.09=100\ \mathrm{pm^{-2}}$. Their sum is $500\ \mathrm{pm^{-2}}$, so

$$ \begin{gathered} \sigma_N^{2}=\frac{1}{500}=0.002\ \mathrm{pm^{2}},\\ \sigma_N=\sqrt{0.002}\approx0.045\ \mathrm{pm}. \end{gathered} $$ $$ \begin{aligned} \mu_N&=0.002\times(400\times390.50\\ &\qquad+100\times391.10)\\ &=0.002\times(156200+39110)\\ &=390.62\ \mathrm{pm}. \end{aligned} $$

The data weight is $w=\dfrac{N\sigma_0^{2}}{N\sigma_0^{2}+\sigma^{2}}$ $=\dfrac{9\times0.0025}{9\times0.0025+0.09}$ $=\dfrac{0.0225}{0.1125}=0.2$. The posterior mean lies 80% toward the literature value, and the posterior uncertainty of 0.045 pm looks very small.

(2) If both measure the same $\mu$, then $\bar{x}-\mu_0$ follows a Gaussian distribution with mean 0 and variance $\sigma_0^{2}+\sigma^{2}/N=0.0025+0.01=0.0125\ \mathrm{pm^{2}}$ (the variance of the difference of independent quantities is the sum of the variances). The standard deviation is $\sqrt{0.0125}\approx0.112\ \mathrm{pm}$, and

$$ \frac{\bar{x}-\mu_0}{\sqrt{\sigma_0^{2}+\sigma^{2}/N}}=\frac{391.10-390.50}{0.112}\approx5.4 . $$

The probability that a deviation exceeding 5 standard deviations of a Gaussian distribution occurs by chance is about $10^{-6}$ or less, so the prior distribution and the data are not consistent. In this case, $\mu_N=390.62\ \mathrm{pm}$ and $\sigma_N=0.045\ \mathrm{pm}$ in (1) are numbers based on the premise that “both are correct,” and they cannot be trusted. Reporting a narrow interval between the two values would be wrong.

(3) Examples of possible causes:

  • Difference in measurement temperature: the lattice constant changes with temperature through thermal expansion. If we assume a linear thermal expansion coefficient of $1\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$, a lattice constant of 390.5 pm changes by about $390.5\times10^{-5}\times100\approx0.39\ \mathrm{pm}$ for a temperature difference of 100 K, which can explain most of the 0.6 pm difference. Check the temperatures of the literature measurement and of your own.
  • Difference in the composition or state of the sample: oxygen nonstoichiometry, dissolved impurities, and, for a thin film, strain from the substrate can make the lattice constant differ even for a material with the same name. The literature sample is not necessarily the same as your sample.
  • Systematic error of the instrument: an offset of the zero of the diffraction angle, an offset of the sample height, or a wrong wavelength setting produces a constant offset that does not appear in the repeatability $\sigma$. Check whether the instrument has been calibrated with a standard sample.

It is also possible that the literature $\sigma_0=0.05\ \mathrm{pm}$ is only the repeatability of the measurement and does not include systematic errors. In that case $\sigma_0$ should be re-chosen larger.

Exercise 4.6: The MAP estimate changes with the choice of variable

Solution

We have $\mu=1/(1+e^{-\eta})$ and $\dd\mu/\dd\eta=\mu(1-\mu)\gt0$. By the change-of-variables formula,

$$ \begin{aligned} p_\eta(\eta\mid\data)&=\mathrm{Beta}(\mu\mid A,B)\,\mu(1-\mu)\\ &\propto\mu^{A-1}(1-\mu)^{B-1}\cdot\mu(1-\mu)\\ &=\mu^{A}(1-\mu)^{B}. \end{aligned} $$

When we differentiate with respect to $\eta$, the chain rule gives $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\eta}=\mu(1-\mu)\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\mu}$, and since $\mu(1-\mu)\gt0$, the stationary point with respect to $\eta$ is the same as the stationary point of $\mu^{A}(1-\mu)^{B}$ with respect to $\mu$. Differentiating the logarithm,

$$ \frac{A}{\mu}-\frac{B}{1-\mu}=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mu=\frac{A}{A+B}. $$

Since $A,B\gt0$, $\mu^{A}(1-\mu)^{B}$ vanishes at both ends $\mu=0,1$ ($\eta\to\mp\infty$), so this point is the maximum. That is, the density of $\eta$ has a finite maximum even when $A,B$ are less than 1. Note that $A/(A+B)$ is the same value as the posterior mean of $\mu$.

Comparison of magnitudes:

$$ \begin{aligned} &\frac{A}{A+B}-\frac{A-1}{A+B-2}\\ &\quad=\frac{A(A+B-2)-(A-1)(A+B)}{(A+B)(A+B-2)}\\ &\quad=\frac{B-A}{(A+B)(A+B-2)} . \end{aligned} $$

If $A\gt1$ and $B\gt1$, the denominator is positive, so if $B\gt A$ (the MAP estimate of $\mu$ is below 1/2), the value obtained with $\eta$ is larger, and if $A\gt B$ it is smaller. In either case, the value obtained with $\eta$ is closer to 1/2 than the MAP estimate of $\mu$. If $A=B$, both equal 1/2 and coincide.

For $\mathrm{Beta}(4,1)$, the MAP estimate of $\mu$ is the endpoint 1 (Example 4.5 of Chapter 4), but the density of $\eta$ takes an interior maximum at the point corresponding to $\mu=4/5=0.8$. The claim that “the most probable pass rate is 1, because all 3 of 3 passed” depends on having chosen $\mu$ as the variable; in terms of the log-odds, “the most probable value is 0.8.”

Discussion: since the MAP estimate depends on the arbitrary convention of the choice of variable, one should avoid reporting it alone. It is better to report quantities that are consistent under a change of variables (quantiles, the median, credible intervals), the posterior mean (= the posterior predictive probability), which can be used directly for prediction, or the posterior distribution itself (a figure, or the parameters of $\mathrm{Beta}(A,B)$). When reporting a MAP estimate, state clearly in which variable it was obtained.

Exercise 4.7: How many must we make to claim a defect rate of 1% or less?

Solution

The condition $c_{95}=1-0.05^{1/(N+1)}\le0.01$ is equivalent to $0.05^{1/(N+1)}\ge0.99$. Take the natural logarithm of both sides (the direction does not change because $\ln$ is monotone increasing).

$$ \frac{\ln0.05}{N+1}\ge\ln0.99 . $$

Multiply both sides by $(N+1)\gt0$, then divide by the negative number $\ln0.99\approx-0.010050$, which reverses the direction of the inequality.

$$ N+1\ge\frac{\ln0.05}{\ln0.99}=\frac{-2.9957}{-0.010050}\approx298.07 . $$

Since $N+1$ is an integer, $N+1\ge299$, that is, $N\ge298$. To check: for $N=298$, $c_{95}=1-0.05^{1/299}\approx0.00997\le0.01$, and for $N=297$, $c_{95}\approx0.01000$ ($0.0100024$) $\gt0.01$. Hence the smallest $N$ is 298.

With the rule of three, $3/N\le0.01$ gives $N\ge300$, which is almost the same value (because $c_{95}\approx-\ln0.05/(N+1)\approx3/(N+1)$).

If one cracked sample appears: with one crack among $N$, the posterior distribution is $\mathrm{Beta}(\mu\mid 2,\ N)$ ($a+m=1+1$, $b+l=1+(N-1)$). Find its 95% point $c$ by solving the cumulative distribution function (CDF) condition $P(\mu\le c\mid\data)=0.95$ numerically, and search for the $N$ with $c\le0.01$. Because of the one crack, the upper limit becomes larger, so more than 298 samples are needed for the same target (numerical computation gives about $N\ge472$). When making a plan of the form “accept if there are 0 cracks,” it is wise to decide in advance what to do if one appears (how many additional samples to make).

Exercise 4.8: When does the difference between prior distributions disappear?

Solution

(1) $m=18$ and $l=12$. A: $\mathrm{Beta}(1+18,\ 1+12)=\mathrm{Beta}(19,13)$, with posterior mean $19/32\approx0.594$. B: $\mathrm{Beta}(10+18,\ 2+12)=\mathrm{Beta}(28,14)$, with posterior mean $28/42\approx0.667$. The difference is about 0.073, and B is shifted well above the maximum likelihood estimate 0.6.

(2) If the fraction of successes stays 0.6 for $N$ trials, then $m=0.6N$, and the difference is

$$ d(N)=\frac{10+0.6N}{12+N}-\frac{1+0.6N}{2+N}. $$

Putting it over a common denominator, the numerator is

$$ \begin{aligned} &(10+0.6N)(2+N)-(1+0.6N)(12+N)\\ &\quad=(20+11.2N+0.6N^{2})\\ &\qquad-(12+8.2N+0.6N^{2})\\ &\quad=8+3N \end{aligned} $$

so $d(N)=\dfrac{3N+8}{(N+12)(N+2)}$. At $N=30$, $98/1344\approx0.073$, which agrees with (1). Solve $d(N)\lt0.02$. At $N=138$, $422/(150\times140)=422/21000\approx0.0201$, and at $N=139$, $425/(151\times141)=425/21291\approx0.0200$ ($0.01996$), so the smallest $N$ is 139. Roughly $d\approx3/N$, so the difference shrinks only as $1/N$.

(3) $\mathrm{Beta}(10,2)$ corresponds to pseudo-counts of “9 successes and 1 failure” and strongly asserts the mean $10/12\approx0.83$. If this track record does not apply to the new material, then while the data are few, the posterior mean of B comes out systematically higher than the true value. Moreover, the posterior distribution of B is narrower than that of A (it is regarded as having more information, by the amount of the prior distribution), so the credible interval is narrow, and a wrong value is reported with confidence. As in (2), nearly 140 trials are needed for the difference to fall below 0.02. Countermeasures include comparison with a weak prior distribution such as A (sensitivity analysis), examining the differences between the material on which the prior distribution was based and the new material, and reducing the pseudo-count of the prior distribution (for example, as in $\mathrm{Beta}(5,1)$, reducing $a+b$ from 12 to 6 while keeping the mean $5/6\approx0.83$).

Chapter 5 Information, Entropy, and the KL Divergence — Measuring the Difference Between Distributions

Exercise 5.1: Entropy and the effective number of choices

Solution

(1) Compute each term $-p\ln p$.

$$ \begin{aligned} -0.4\ln0.4&=0.4\times0.9163=0.3665,\\ -0.3\ln0.3&=0.3\times1.2040=0.3612,\\ -0.2\ln0.2&=0.2\times1.6094=0.3219,\\ -0.1\ln0.1&=0.1\times2.3026=0.2303 . \end{aligned} $$

The sum is $H[p]=1.2799\ \mathrm{nat}$. In bits, $1.2799/\ln2=1.2799/0.6931\approx1.846\ \mathrm{bit}$.

(2) $\exp(1.2799)\approx3.60$. There are four values, but because of the imbalance, we are effectively as uncertain as if we were choosing among about 3.6 options.

(3) Let $u=(1/4,1/4,1/4,1/4)$ and compute $\KL(p\,\|\,u)=\sum_x p(x)\ln\{4p(x)\}$ term by term.

$$ \begin{aligned} &0.4\ln1.6=0.1880,\quad 0.3\ln1.2=0.0547,\\ &0.2\ln0.8=-0.0446,\quad 0.1\ln0.4=-0.0916 . \end{aligned} $$

The sum is $0.1064\ \mathrm{nat}$. On the other hand, $\ln4-H[p]=1.3863-1.2799=0.1064\ \mathrm{nat}$, which agrees. This is a numerical example of the identity $\KL(p\,\|\,u)$ $=\sum p\ln p+\ln4\sum p$ $=-H[p]+\ln4$.

Exercise 5.2: Upper limit of the joint entropy

Solution

(1) Combine the chain rule of entropy $H[x,y]=H[x]+H[y\mid x]$ with the formula for mutual information $I[x;y]=H[y]-H[y\mid x]$, that is, $H[y\mid x]=H[y]-I[x;y]$. This gives

$$ H[x,y]=H[x]+H[y]-I[x;y]. $$

The mutual information is the KL divergence $\KL(p(x,y)\,\|\,p(x)p(y))$, so Gibbs's inequality gives $I[x;y]\ge0$. Hence $H[x,y]\le H[x]+H[y]$. Equality holds only when $I[x;y]=0$, that is, when $p(x,y)=p(x)p(y)$ (independence).

(2) For the joint distribution $(0.12,\,0.28,\,0.06,\,0.54)$, the terms $-p\ln p$ are $0.2544$, $0.3564$, $0.1688$, and $0.3327$, and their sum is $H[x,y]=1.1124\ \mathrm{nat}$. From Chapter 5, Example 5.7, $H[x]=0.6730$, $H[y]=0.4714$, and $I[x;y]=0.0320$, so $H[x]+H[y]-I$ $=0.6730+0.4714-0.0320$ $=1.1124$, which agrees. It is smaller than $H[x]+H[y]=1.1444$.

(3) Applying the chain rule with $y$ first gives $H[x,y]=H[y]+H[x\mid y]$, so $H[x\mid y]=1.1124-0.4714=0.6410\ \mathrm{nat}$. As a check, $H[x]-I=0.6730-0.0320=0.6410$ also agrees.

Exercise 5.3: Differential entropies of three distributions with the same variance

Solution

(1) The variance of a uniform distribution of width $a$ is $a^{2}/12$ (taking the interval $[-a/2,a/2]$, $\int_{-a/2}^{a/2}x^{2}\frac1a\,\dd x=\frac{1}{a}\cdot\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac a2\right)^{3}=\frac{a^{2}}{12}$). For variance 1, $a=\sqrt{12}\approx3.464$. The differential entropy is $\ln a=\ln\sqrt{12}=\tfrac12\ln12\approx1.2425\ \mathrm{nat}$.

(2) Since $\ln p(x)=-\ln(2b)-\abs{x}/b$,

$$ h=\Ex[-\ln p(x)]=\ln(2b)+\frac{\Ex[\abs{x}]}{b}. $$

$\Ex[\abs{x}]$ is, because the integrand is an even function, $2\int_0^{\infty}x\,\frac{1}{2b}e^{-x/b}\,\dd x$ $=\frac1b\int_0^{\infty}xe^{-x/b}\,\dd x$ $=\frac1b\cdot b^{2}=b$ (using $\int_0^\infty xe^{-x/b}\dd x=b^{2}$). Hence $h=\ln(2b)+1$. Similarly, $\Ex[x^{2}]=\frac1b\int_0^\infty x^{2}e^{-x/b}\dd x=\frac1b\cdot2b^{3}=2b^{2}$, so variance 1 requires $b=1/\sqrt2$. Then $h=1+\ln\sqrt2=1+0.3466=1.3466\ \mathrm{nat}$.

(3) Setting $\sigma=1$ in the formula for the differential entropy of the Gaussian distribution in Chapter 5, Section 5.3.1 gives $h=\tfrac12\ln(2\pi e)=\tfrac12(1.8379+1)=1.4189\ \mathrm{nat}$.

In order of size, $1.2425\lt1.3466\lt1.4189$, so for a given variance the Gaussian distribution has the largest entropy. This is an example of Theorem 5.2 (b).

Exercise 5.4: Cross-entropy loss for binary classification

Solution

(1) The loss of one sample is $L_n=-\{y_n\ln\hat{y}_n+(1-y_n)\ln(1-\hat{y}_n)\}$. For a sample with $y=1$ it is $-\ln\hat{y}$, and for a sample with $y=0$ it is $-\ln(1-\hat{y})$.

$$ \begin{aligned} L_1&=-\ln0.9=0.1054,\\ L_2&=-\ln(1-0.2)=-\ln0.8=0.2231,\\ L_3&=-\ln0.6=0.5108,\\ L_4&=-\ln0.4=0.9163 . \end{aligned} $$

The mean is $(0.1054+0.2231+0.5108+0.9163)/4$ $=1.7556/4$ $=0.4389\ \mathrm{nat}$. The fourth sample (a good item for which the probability of being good was given as 0.4) makes the largest contribution.

(2) If the classifier always outputs $c$, the mean loss over three good items and one defective item is

$$ \bar{L}(c)=-\frac14\bigl(3\ln c+\ln(1-c)\bigr). $$

Differentiating and setting the result to 0 gives $-\frac14\left(\frac3c-\frac{1}{1-c}\right)=0$, so $3(1-c)=c$ and $c=3/4$. The loss at this value is $-(0.75\ln0.75+0.25\ln0.25)=0.5623\ \mathrm{nat}$, which equals the entropy of the empirical distribution $(0.75,0.25)$ of the correct labels (the KL divergence from the empirical distribution is 0).

(3) For $c=0.5$, the loss is $-\ln0.5=0.6931\ \mathrm{nat}$. In order of size, $0.4389\lt0.5623\lt0.6931$. The value $0.75$, which learns only the proportion of the labels, is better than $0.5$, which learns nothing, and the classifier of (1), which uses the input, is better still. The value of a model that uses the input is best measured by how much it can lower the loss below $0.5623$, the loss of the “proportion-only model.”

Exercise 5.5: Probability 0 and the blow-up of the KL divergence

Solution

(1) For $\KL(p\,\|\,q)$: the third value has $p=0$, so its term is 0. The remainder is $2\times0.5\ln\frac{0.5}{0.45}=\ln\frac{10}{9}\approx0.1054\ \mathrm{nat}$.
For $\KL(q\,\|\,p)$: at the third value, $q=0.1\gt0$ but $p=0$, so the term $0.1\ln(0.1/0)$ becomes $+\infty$, and $\KL(q\,\|\,p)=+\infty$.

(2) The cross-entropy loss is $H[\hat{p},q]=H[\hat{p}]+\KL(\hat{p}\,\|\,q)$ with respect to the empirical distribution $\hat{p}$. If the model $q$ assigns probability 0 to some class and even one sample of that class is in the data, the loss of that sample is $-\ln0=+\infty$, and the mean loss is also $+\infty$. This has the same structure as $\KL(q\,\|\,p)$ in (1) (the right-hand distribution is 0 where the left-hand distribution places probability). In numerical computation, $\ln0$ becomes $-\infty$ or NaN, and the gradient can no longer be computed either.

(3) Examples of countermeasures: (a) Clip the output probabilities to $[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]$ (with $\varepsilon$ about $10^{-7}$) before taking the logarithm. (b) Instead of computing the probabilities first and then taking the logarithm, compute the log-softmax directly from the logits using log-sum-exp (the logarithm stays finite even if a probability is rounded to 0). (c) Mix the one-hot target slightly with the uniform distribution (label smoothing). (a) and (b) are numerical measures to keep the loss finite, and the optimal model hardly changes. (c) changes the target distribution itself, so the learned model no longer assigns probability 1 to the correct class (its degree of confidence decreases).

Exercise 5.6: A non-equimolar high-entropy alloy

Solution

(1) The mole fractions are $x_{\mathrm{Al}}=0.5/4.5=1/9$, and $1/4.5=2/9$ for each of Co, Cr, Fe, and Ni. The terms are

$$ \begin{aligned} -\tfrac19\ln\tfrac19&=\tfrac{\ln9}{9}=0.2441,\\ -\tfrac29\ln\tfrac29&=\tfrac29\ln4.5=0.3342 . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\Delta S_{\mathrm{conf}}/R=0.2441+4\times0.3342=1.5811$, and $\Delta S_{\mathrm{conf}}$ $=1.5811\times8.314$ $\approx13.15\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.

(2) Equimolar quaternary system: $R\ln4=1.3863R\approx11.53\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Equimolar quinary system: $R\ln5=1.6094R\approx13.38\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. $\mathrm{Al_{0.5}CoCrFeNi}$ is larger than the quaternary system and slightly smaller than the equimolar quinary system (the difference is about $0.24\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$).

(3) The equimolar composition $x_i=1/5$. This is because the configurational entropy is $R\,H[\bm{x}]$ ($R$ times the entropy obtained by regarding the composition as a probability distribution), and by Theorem 5.2 (a) the entropy of a distribution taking five values attains its maximum value $\ln5$ only for the uniform distribution. However, this concerns only the configurational entropy under the assumption of ideal random mixing, and the phase stability of a real alloy also depends on the enthalpy of mixing and on short-range order.

Exercise 5.7: Spin entropy of a paramagnet

Solution

(1) The energies of the two states are $\mp\mu_{\mathrm B}B$, so in the canonical distribution (Chapter 5, Section 5.8.2), $\beta E=\mp\xi$. The partition function is $Z=e^{\xi}+e^{-\xi}=2\cosh\xi$, and the probabilities are

$$ p_{\text{parallel}}=\frac{e^{\xi}}{2\cosh\xi},\qquad p_{\text{antiparallel}}=\frac{e^{-\xi}}{2\cosh\xi}. $$

The mean energy is $\bar{E}=-\mu_{\mathrm B}B\,(p_{\text{parallel}}-p_{\text{antiparallel}})=-\mu_{\mathrm B}B\tanh\xi$, so $\beta\bar{E}=-\xi\tanh\xi$. The entropy of the canonical distribution is $H=\beta\bar{E}+\ln Z$ (Chapter 5, Section 5.8.2), so

$$ H=\ln(2\cosh\xi)-\xi\tanh\xi . $$

The moments are independent of one another, so for 1 mol we multiply by $N_{\mathrm A}$ to get $S=N_{\mathrm A}k_{\mathrm B}H=RH$, that is, $S/R=\ln(2\cosh\xi)-\xi\tanh\xi$.

(2) $\xi=0.5$: $\ln(2\cosh0.5)=\ln2.2553=0.8133$ and $0.5\tanh0.5=0.2311$, so $S/R=0.5822$ and $S\approx4.84\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.
$\xi=1$: $\ln(2\cosh1)=\ln3.0862=1.1269$ and $\tanh1=0.7616$, so $S/R=0.3653$ and $S\approx3.04\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.
$\xi=2$: $\ln(2\cosh2)=\ln7.5244=2.0182$ and $2\tanh2=1.9281$, so $S/R=0.0901$ and $S\approx0.75\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.

(3) For $\xi\to0$ (high temperature or weak field): $\cosh\xi\to1$ and $\xi\tanh\xi\to0$, so $S\to R\ln2\approx5.76\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. The two directions are equally probable, and each moment carries 1 bit of uncertainty. For $\xi\to\infty$ (low temperature or strong field): $\ln(2\cosh\xi)=\xi+\ln(1+e^{-2\xi})\to\xi$ and $\xi\tanh\xi\to\xi$, so $S\to0$. All the moments align with the direction of the field, and the uncertainty disappears.

(4) $S$ is a function only of $\xi=\mu_{\mathrm B}B/(k_{\mathrm B}T)$, so keeping $S$ constant keeps $\xi$ constant, that is, $B/T$ constant. Therefore, when the field is weakened adiabatically, the temperature falls in proportion to the field. This is the principle of cooling by adiabatic demagnetization (in practice, lattice-vibration entropy and interactions between the moments exist, so this is limited to the range in which this idealized model holds).

Exercise 5.8: Mutual information between a descriptor and the phase formed

Solution

(1) In both tables the marginal distributions are $(0.5,0.5)$ for the descriptor and $(0.5,0.5)$ for the phase, so $p(\cdot)p(\cdot)=0.25$.
Descriptor $a$: the joint probabilities are $0.40,\,0.10,\,0.10,\,0.40$.

$$ \begin{aligned} I[a;y]&=2\times0.40\ln\frac{0.40}{0.25}\\ &\quad+2\times0.10\ln\frac{0.10}{0.25}\\ &=0.8\times0.4700+0.2\times(-0.9163)\\ &=0.3760-0.1833=0.1927\ \mathrm{nat}. \end{aligned} $$

Descriptor $b$: the joint probabilities are $0.30,\,0.20,\,0.20,\,0.30$.

$$ \begin{aligned} I[b;y]&=2\times0.30\ln\frac{0.30}{0.25}\\ &\quad+2\times0.20\ln\frac{0.20}{0.25}\\ &=0.6\times0.1823+0.4\times(-0.2231)\\ &=0.1094-0.0893=0.0201\ \mathrm{nat}. \end{aligned} $$

(2) $H[y]=\ln2=0.6931\ \mathrm{nat}$. Knowing descriptor $a$ reduces the uncertainty by $0.1927/0.6931\approx27.8\%$, and knowing descriptor $b$ reduces it by $0.0201/0.6931\approx2.9\%$. Descriptor $a$ is far more useful.

(3) We cannot say so. Mutual information measures only the strength of dependence; it does not distinguish the direction (which is the cause) or whether the dependence is only apparent because of a third factor. For example, if the compositions with a large $a$ are concentrated in the lineage of a particular element, and that lineage happens to form the perovskite type easily, then changing $a$ may not change the structure. If many compositions from the same lineage are included, the data are not independent, and the dependence looks spuriously strong. Moreover, mutual information computed from about 100 samples tends to be positive even when the true value is 0. To claim causation, we need syntheses that change only $a$ with the lineage held fixed (interventions) or support from a physical mechanism (Chapter 6, Section 6.11).
