Chapter 6 Generalization, Model Selection, and Regularization — Models That Predict Unseen Data

Exercise 6.1: Size of the optimism of the training error (derivation and computation)

Solution

(0) Properties of $\bm{H}$. Since the columns of $\bm{\Phi}$ are linearly independent, $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}\in\R^{M\times M}$ is invertible.

  • $\bm{H}\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})=\bm{\Phi}$.
  • $\bm{H}^{\tp}=\bm{\Phi}\bigl((\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bigr)^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}$ $=\bm{\Phi}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{H}$ (the inverse of a symmetric matrix is symmetric).
  • $\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{H}\bm{\Phi}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}$ $=\bm{\Phi}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{H}$ (we used the first property).
  • $\tr\bm{H}=\tr\bigl(\bm{\Phi}\,[(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}]\bigr)$ $=\tr\bigl([(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}]\,\bm{\Phi}\bigr)$ $=\tr\bm{I}_M=M$ ($\tr(\bm{A}\bm{B})=\tr(\bm{B}\bm{A})$).

Expectation of a quadratic form. For a constant matrix $\bm{A}\in\R^{N\times N}$, $\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}\bm{A}\bm{\varepsilon}=\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}\varepsilon_i\varepsilon_j$. Since $\Cov[\bm{\varepsilon}]=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}$ and $\Ex[\bm{\varepsilon}]=\bm{0}$, we have $\Ex[\varepsilon_i\varepsilon_j]=\sigma^{2}$ ($i=j$) and $0$ ($i\ne j$), so $\Ex[\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}\bm{A}\bm{\varepsilon}]=\sigma^{2}\sum_iA_{ii}=\sigma^{2}\tr\bm{A}$.

Training error. $\bm{y}-\hat{\bm{y}}=(\bm{I}-\bm{H})(\bm{\Phi}\bm{w}_0+\bm{\varepsilon})=(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}$ ($\bm{H}\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}$). Since $(\bm{I}-\bm{H})^{\tp}(\bm{I}-\bm{H})=\bm{I}-2\bm{H}+\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{I}-\bm{H}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex\bigl[\norm{\bm{y}-\hat{\bm{y}}}^{2}\bigr]&=\Ex\bigl[\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}\bigr]\\ &=\sigma^{2}\tr(\bm{I}-\bm{H})=\sigma^{2}(N-M) . \end{aligned} $$

A new measurement at the same inputs. Let $\bm{y}'=\bm{\Phi}\bm{w}_0+\bm{\varepsilon}'$ ($\bm{\varepsilon}'$ is independent of $\bm{\varepsilon}$ and has the same distribution). Then $\bm{y}'-\hat{\bm{y}}=\bm{\varepsilon}'-\bm{H}\bm{\varepsilon}$. The expectation of the cross term, $-2\,\Ex[\bm{\varepsilon}'^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{\varepsilon}]$, is 0 by independence and zero mean. Hence

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Ex\bigl[\norm{\bm{y}'-\hat{\bm{y}}}^{2}\bigr]\\ &\quad=N\sigma^{2}+\Ex\bigl[\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}\bm{H}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{\varepsilon}\bigr]\\ &\quad=N\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{2}\tr\bm{H}=\sigma^{2}(N+M) . \end{aligned} $$

(From the second line to the third we used $\bm{H}^{\tp}\bm{H}=\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{H}$ and the expectation of a quadratic form.)

Dividing both by $N$ gives $\sigma^{2}(1-M/N)$ and $\sigma^{2}(1+M/N)$ in Eq. (6.3) of Chapter 6.

(1) With $\sigma^{2}=0.25\ \%^{2}$, $M=4$, and $N=20$, the expectation of the mean squared training error is $0.25\times(1-4/20)=0.25\times0.8=0.20\ \%^{2}$, and for a new measurement it is $0.25\times(1+0.2)=0.30\ \%^{2}$.

(2) For $M=10$: $0.25\times(1-0.5)=0.125\ \%^{2}$ and $0.25\times1.5=0.375\ \%^{2}$. The difference (the size of the optimism) is $0.10$ for $M=4$ and $0.25\ \%^{2}$ for $M=10$, so it grows in proportion to $M$.

(3) By (0), the expectation of the residual sum of squares is $\sigma^{2}(N-M)$, so $\hat\sigma^{2}=\norm{\bm{y}-\hat{\bm{y}}}^{2}/(N-M)$ is an unbiased estimate of $\sigma^{2}$. We get $\hat\sigma^{2}=3.2/(20-4)=0.20\ \%^{2}$ and $\hat\sigma=\sqrt{0.20}\approx0.447\ \%$. Dividing by $N$ gives $3.2/20=0.16\ \%^{2}$, whose square root is $0.40\ \%$, which underestimates the noise by about 10 %.

Exercise 6.2: Shrinkage estimation and one-dimensional ridge regression

Solution

(1) As in Example 6.4 of Chapter 6, $\Ex[\hat\mu_c]=r+c(\mu-r)$, so the bias is $-(1-c)\Delta$ and the variance is $c^{2}\sigma^{2}/N$; thus

$$ m(c)\equiv\Ex[(\hat\mu_c-\mu)^{2}]=(1-c)^{2}\Delta^{2}+c^{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N} . $$

Differentiate with respect to $c$ and set the result to 0: $-2(1-c)\Delta^{2}+2c\,\sigma^{2}/N=0$, which gives

$$ c^{\ast}=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{\Delta^{2}+\sigma^{2}/N} . $$

The second derivative $2\Delta^{2}+2\sigma^{2}/N\gt0$, so this is a minimum. Writing $v=\sigma^{2}/N$, we have $1-c^{\ast}=v/(\Delta^{2}+v)$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} m(c^{\ast})&=\frac{v^{2}\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{4}v}{(\Delta^{2}+v)^{2}}=\frac{v\Delta^{2}(v+\Delta^{2})}{(\Delta^{2}+v)^{2}}\\ &=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{\Delta^{2}+v}\,v=c^{\ast}\,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N} . \end{aligned} $$

This is always smaller than the squared error $\sigma^{2}/N$ of the sample mean ($c=1$) (if $\sigma^{2}/N\gt0$, then $c^{\ast}\lt1$). For $\Delta=1$ and $v=0.25$ in Example 6.4, we get $c^{\ast}=0.8$ and minimum value $0.2$, which agrees. However, $c^{\ast}$ contains the unknown $\Delta$, so in practice the strength of the shrinkage must be chosen using validation data or the like.

(2) Let $z=\sum_nx_ny_n$. Substituting $y_n=w^{\ast}x_n+\varepsilon_n$ gives $z=w^{\ast}S+\sum_nx_n\varepsilon_n$, so $\Ex[z]=w^{\ast}S$ and $\Var[z]=\sum_nx_n^{2}\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}S$ (the $\varepsilon_n$ are independent). Since $w_\lambda=z/(S+\lambda)$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[w_\lambda]-w^{\ast}&=\frac{w^{\ast}S}{S+\lambda}-w^{\ast}=-\frac{w^{\ast}\lambda}{S+\lambda},\\ \Var[w_\lambda]&=\frac{\sigma^{2}S}{(S+\lambda)^{2}} . \end{aligned} $$

The expectation of the squared error is (bias)² + variance:

$$ m(\lambda)=\frac{(w^{\ast})^{2}\lambda^{2}+\sigma^{2}S}{(S+\lambda)^{2}} . $$

By the quotient rule,

$$ \begin{aligned} m'(\lambda)&=\frac{2(w^{\ast})^{2}\lambda(S+\lambda)}{(S+\lambda)^{3}}\\ &\qquad-\frac{2\bigl((w^{\ast})^{2}\lambda^{2}+\sigma^{2}S\bigr)}{(S+\lambda)^{3}}\\ &=\frac{2S\bigl((w^{\ast})^{2}\lambda-\sigma^{2}\bigr)}{(S+\lambda)^{3}} . \end{aligned} $$

It is negative for $\lambda\lt\sigma^{2}/(w^{\ast})^{2}$ and positive for larger values, so the minimum is at $\lambda^{\ast}=\sigma^{2}/(w^{\ast})^{2}$. The larger the noise and the smaller the true coefficient, the stronger the regularization that is best. The $\lambda=\sigma^{2}/\sigma_w^{2}$ of Bayesian linear regression, derived in Chapter 8, can be read as putting the anticipated size $\sigma_w^{2}$ of the coefficient in place of $(w^{\ast})^{2}$.

Exercise 6.3: Three-fold cross-validation and leave-one-out

Solution

The data are $y=(2.1,\ 2.5,\ 1.8,\ 2.9,\ 2.2,\ 2.6)$ eV.

(1) Leaving out pair 1 (2.1, 2.5), the training mean is $(1.8+2.9+2.2+2.6)/4=9.5/4=2.375$. The residuals are $2.1-2.375=-0.275$ and $2.5-2.375=0.125$. Leaving out pair 2 (1.8, 2.9), the mean is $(2.1+2.5+2.2+2.6)/4=9.4/4=2.350$, with residuals $-0.550$ and $0.550$. Leaving out pair 3 (2.2, 2.6), the mean is $(2.1+2.5+1.8+2.9)/4=9.3/4=2.325$, with residuals $-0.125$ and $0.275$. The squares are $0.075625,\ 0.015625,\ 0.3025,$ $0.3025,\ 0.015625,\ 0.075625$, and their sum is $0.7875$. The mean squared error of the three-fold cross-validation is $0.7875/6=0.131\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$ (RMSE 0.362 eV).

(2) The overall mean is $14.1/6=2.35$. The training residuals are $-0.25,\ 0.15,\ -0.55,\ 0.55,\ -0.15,\ 0.25$, the sum of squares is $0.0625+0.0225+0.3025$ $+0.3025+0.0225+0.0625$ $=0.775$, and the mean is $0.775/6=0.1292\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$. The LOO residuals are $\frac{N}{N-1}=\frac65=1.2$ times these: $-0.30,\ 0.18,\ -0.66,\ 0.66,\ -0.18,\ 0.30$. For example, the mean with the first point left out is $(2.5+1.8+2.9+2.2+2.6)/5$ $=12.0/5=2.40$, and the residual $2.1-2.40=-0.30$ agrees. The sum of squares is $0.09+0.0324+0.4356$ $+0.4356+0.0324+0.09$ $=1.116$, and the mean is $1.116/6=0.186\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$. This agrees with $(6/5)^{2}\times0.1292=1.44\times0.1292=0.186$.

That LOO (0.186) is larger than the three-fold value (0.131) is because, in this example, the residuals happened to cancel for the particular way the pairs were split; in general it is not determined which is larger. With only six data points, the cross-validation value changes greatly with the way the data are split.

Exercise 6.4: Duplicates in a database and splitting

Solution

(1) For each of the 120 duplicated rows, the probability that the row falls on the validation side is $40/200=0.2$. Given that, the probability that its partner (a specific one of the remaining 199 rows) falls in the 160 training slots is $160/199$. By the linearity of expectation, the expected number of validation rows whose partner is in the training data is

$$ 120\times\frac{40}{200}\times\frac{160}{199}=24\times0.804=19.3\ \text{rows} $$

which is about 48 % of the 40 validation rows ($19.3/40=0.482$).

(2) For a row that has a partner, the predicted value is the partner's measured value, so the error is the difference of two independent measurements, and the expectation of the squared error is $2\times0.04^{2}=0.0032\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$. For the other rows it is $0.35^{2}=0.1225\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$. The expectation of the mean squared validation error is $0.518\times0.1225+0.482\times0.0032$ $=0.0634+0.0015$ $=0.0650\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$, and its square root is $0.255\ \mathrm{eV}$. This looks about 27 % smaller than the true 0.35 eV.

(3) (a) Find the duplicates before splitting, and combine them into one row per composition, for example by averaging the measurements of the same composition. (b) Perform a group split with composition as the group, so that rows of the same composition do not straddle training and validation. In either case, decide and record beforehand the criterion for finding duplicates (variations in how compositions are written, and whether polymorphs are distinguished).

Exercise 6.5: Extrapolating the band gap of a mixed crystal from half of the composition range

Solution

(1) $E_g(x)=1.20-1.20x+2.40x$ $-0.80x+0.80x^{2}$ $=1.20+0.40x+0.80x^{2}$ eV.

(2) First find the straight line $\alpha+\beta x$ that approximates $x^{2}$ on $[0,h]$. Differentiate $J(\alpha,\beta)=\int_0^{h}(x^{2}-\alpha-\beta x)^{2}\dd x$ partially with respect to $\alpha,\beta$ and set the results to 0 (the integral version of the normal equations):

$$ \begin{aligned} \int_0^{h}(x^{2}-\alpha-\beta x)\,\dd x&=0,\\ \int_0^{h}x\,(x^{2}-\alpha-\beta x)\,\dd x&=0 . \end{aligned} $$

Integrating gives $\dfrac{h^{3}}{3}=\alpha h+\beta\dfrac{h^{2}}{2}$ and $\dfrac{h^{4}}{4}=\alpha\dfrac{h^{2}}{2}+\beta\dfrac{h^{3}}{3}$. Dividing the first equation by $h$ and the second by $h^{2}/2$ gives $\dfrac{h^{2}}{3}=\alpha+\dfrac{\beta h}{2}$ and $\dfrac{h^{2}}{2}=\alpha+\dfrac{2\beta h}{3}$. Taking the difference, $\dfrac{h^{2}}{6}=\dfrac{\beta h}{6}$, so $\beta=h$ and $\alpha=\dfrac{h^{2}}{3}-\dfrac{h^{2}}{2}=-\dfrac{h^{2}}{6}$.

The constant and $x$ terms can be represented exactly by a straight line, so the best straight line for $E_g$ is $1.20+0.40x+0.80(hx-h^{2}/6)$. Substituting $h=0.5$,

$$ \begin{aligned} a+bx&=1.20-\frac{0.80\times0.25}{6}\\ &\qquad+(0.40+0.80\times0.5)\,x\\ &=1.1667+0.80\,x\ \ \mathrm{eV}. \end{aligned} $$

(3) The error (line − true value) is, at $x=0$: $1.1667-1.20=-0.033$ eV; at $x=0.25$: $1.3667-1.35=+0.017$ eV; at $x=0.5$: $1.5667-1.60=-0.033$ eV. Within the range the error is at most 0.033 eV, comparable to the noise standard deviation of 0.03 eV, so it is hard to notice from the measurements that "a straight line is not enough." On the other hand, at $x=1$ the line gives $1.967$ eV while the true value is $2.40$ eV, so the error is $-0.433$ eV, more than 14 times the noise.

(4) That the error within the range was at the noise level does not guarantee correctness outside the range. A term such as the curvature (bowing) of $x(1-x)$, which looks weak inside the range, has a large effect outside. When predicting beyond the composition range, (a) state explicitly that this is extrapolation, (b) use a physically reasonable functional form (such as Vegard's law plus a bowing term; Chapter 8), and (c) if possible, measure even one point at the end of the range (BC at $x=1$) to check the model.

Exercise 6.6: Negative coefficient of determination

Solution

The mean of the test data is $\bar{y}=(3.0+3.4+2.8+3.6)/4=3.2$ eV, and $\sum_n(y_n-\bar{y})^{2}$ $=0.04+0.04+0.16+0.16$ $=0.40\ \mathrm{eV}^{2}$.

(1) The errors $y-\hat{y}$ of model A are $(-0.3,\ 0.1,\ -0.5,\ 0.3)$. MAE $=(0.3+0.1+0.5+0.3)/4=0.30$ eV. The sum of squares is $0.09+0.01+0.25+0.09=0.44$, and RMSE $=\sqrt{0.44/4}=\sqrt{0.11}=0.332$ eV. $R^{2}=1-0.44/0.40=-0.10$.

(2) The errors of model B are $(-0.1,\ 0.1,\ -0.1,\ 0.2)$. MAE $=0.5/4=0.125$ eV. The sum of squares is $0.01+0.01+0.01+0.04=0.07$, and RMSE $=\sqrt{0.0175}=0.132$ eV. $R^{2}=1-0.07/0.40=0.825$.

(3) The denominator $\sum_n(y_n-\bar{y})^{2}$ of $R^{2}$ is "the sum of squared errors when all points are predicted by the test data's own mean, 3.2 eV." The prediction 3.3 eV of model A is farther from the data than this mean, so its sum of squares, 0.44, is larger than the denominator 0.40, and $R^{2}\lt0$. The mean of the test data is a value that is not known at the time of prediction, so $R^{2}\lt0$ means "we could have won if we had known the mean of the test data." It tends to occur when the mean of the training data and the mean of the test data are shifted (the distribution has changed).

Exercise 6.7: Surface color and density

Solution

(1) The variance of a sum of independent quantities is the sum of the variances, and the covariance of independent quantities is 0, so

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[x]&=0.8^{2}\times1+0.36=1.00,\\ \Cov[x,y]&=0.8\times1.5\times\Var[z]=1.20,\\ \Var[y]&=1.5^{2}+0.75=3.00 . \end{aligned} $$

The regression slope is $\Cov[x,y]/\Var[x]=1.20$, and the correlation coefficient is $1.20/\sqrt{1.00\times3.00}=0.693$.

(2) We have $y=1.5z+n_y$, and $n_y$ is independent of both $x$ and $z$ ($x$ is determined only by $z$ and $n_x$), so $\Ex[y\mid x,z]=1.5z$. The regression that includes both $x$ and $z$ (least squares over the population) coincides with the conditional expectation, so the coefficient of $x$ is 0 and the coefficient of $z$ is 1.5. The whole correlation between $x$ and $y$ passes through $z$, so once $z$ is fixed, $x$ tells us nothing about $y$.

(3) An example experiment: split samples fired in the same furnace and at the same position (the same $z$) randomly into two groups, polish or paint the surface of only one group to change its lightness, and compare the densities. If there is no difference in density, lightness is not the cause. As another design, assign the position in the furnace to each sample at random, measure the actual temperature with a thermocouple, record $z$, and carry out the regression of (2).

(4) As long as the samples are made in the same furnace, in the same atmosphere, and from the same raw materials, the relation between lightness and density (the correlation through $z$) is preserved, so lightness can be useful as an inexpensive test for estimating density. However, it is useful only as long as "the data arise by the same mechanism." If the relation $z\to x$ changes, for example by changing the furnace, by changing the atmosphere so that the way the color changes is different, or by finishing the surface, the prediction breaks down (the distribution shift of Section 6.9). It cannot be used by manipulating lightness in order to raise the density.

Exercise 6.8: The expectation of the smaller of two candidates

Solution

For any real numbers $a,b$, $\min(a,b)=\dfrac{a+b}{2}-\dfrac{\abs{a-b}}{2}$ (if $a\le b$, the right-hand side $=\frac{a+b}{2}-\frac{b-a}{2}=a$, and similarly for $a\gt b$). By the linearity of expectation,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[\min(a,b)]&=\frac{\Ex[a]+\Ex[b]}{2}-\frac{\Ex\abs{a-b}}{2}\\ &=\mu-\frac{\Ex\abs{a-b}}{2} . \end{aligned} $$

Since $a,b$ are independent Gaussian random variables, $u=a-b$ is also Gaussian, with mean 0 and variance $s^{2}+s^{2}=2s^{2}$. For $u\sim\Normal(0,\sigma_u^{2})$ ($\sigma_u$ is the standard deviation of $u$), using the fact that the integrand is an even function, $\Ex\abs{u}$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex\abs{u}&=2\int_0^{\infty}u\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_u}e^{-u^{2}/(2\sigma_u^{2})}\,\dd u\\ &=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_u}\Bigl[-\sigma_u^{2}e^{-u^{2}/(2\sigma_u^{2})}\Bigr]_0^{\infty}\\ &=\frac{2\sigma_u^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_u}=\sigma_u\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}} . \end{aligned} $$

Substituting $\sigma_u=\sqrt2\,s$ gives $\Ex\abs{a-b}=\sqrt2\,s\sqrt{2/\pi}=2s/\sqrt{\pi}$. Therefore

$$ \Ex[\min(a,b)]=\mu-\frac{s}{\sqrt{\pi}} . $$

The bias $s/\sqrt\pi$ is proportional to the spread $s$ of the estimate of the validation error. The less validation data there is, the larger $s$ is, and the more optimistic the validation error of the selected model becomes. Conversely, if we increase the validation data and make $s$ smaller, the bias due to selection also becomes smaller.

← Back to Chapter 6, “Generalization, Model Selection, and Regularization — Models That Predict Unseen Data”

Chapter 7 Fundamentals of Optimization — Descending the Valley of the Loss

Exercise 7.1: Tracing two-dimensional gradient descent by hand

Solution

(1) From $\det(\bm{H}-\lambda\bm{I})=(3-\lambda)^{2}-1=0$ we get $3-\lambda=\pm1$, so the eigenvalues are $\lambda=2$ and $\lambda=4$. For $\lambda=4$: $(\bm{H}-4\bm{I})\bm{q}=\bm{0}$ reads $-q_1+q_2=0$, so $\bm{q}_2=(1,1)^{\tp}/\sqrt2$. For $\lambda=2$: $q_1+q_2=0$, so $\bm{q}_1=(1,-1)^{\tp}/\sqrt2$. The two eigenvectors are orthogonal.

(2) The condition for convergence is $0\lt\eta\lt2/\lambda_{\max}=2/4=0.5$. We have $\eta_{\mathrm{opt}}=2/(4+2)=1/3$, $\kappa=4/2=2$, and $\rho_{\mathrm{opt}}=(2-1)/(2+1)=1/3$.

(3) Since $\bm{H}\bm{w}_0=(3\cdot2+1\cdot0,\ 1\cdot2+3\cdot0)^{\tp}=(6,2)^{\tp}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{w}_1&=\bm{w}_0-\tfrac13\bm{H}\bm{w}_0\\ &=(2,0)^{\tp}-(2,\tfrac23)^{\tp}=\bigl(0,\ -\tfrac23\bigr)^{\tp}. \end{aligned} $$

Since $\bm{H}\bm{w}_1=(-\tfrac23,\ -2)^{\tp}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{w}_2&=\bm{w}_1-\tfrac13\bm{H}\bm{w}_1\\ &=\bigl(0+\tfrac29,\ -\tfrac23+\tfrac23\bigr)^{\tp}=\bigl(\tfrac29,\ 0\bigr)^{\tp}. \end{aligned} $$

The norms are $\norm{\bm{w}_0}=2$, $\norm{\bm{w}_1}=2/3$, and $\norm{\bm{w}_2}=2/9$, so the norm shrinks by exactly a factor of $1/3$ at each step. The losses are $L(\bm{w}_0)=\tfrac12\bm{w}_0^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{w}_0=\tfrac12(2\cdot6)=6$, $L(\bm{w}_1)=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac23\cdot2=\tfrac23$, and $L(\bm{w}_2)=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac29\cdot\tfrac23=\tfrac{2}{27}\approx0.0741$, so the loss shrinks by a factor of $1/9$ at each step.

(Intuition) We have $\bm{w}_0=(2,0)^{\tp}=\sqrt2\,\bm{q}_1+\sqrt2\,\bm{q}_2$. With $\eta=1/3$, the $\bm{q}_1$ component is multiplied by $1-\tfrac13\cdot2=\tfrac13$ and the $\bm{q}_2$ component by $1-\tfrac13\cdot4=-\tfrac13$. Both factors have absolute value $1/3$, so the norm shrinks by exactly a factor of $1/3$, and only the sign of the $\bm{q}_2$ component flips at every step (this is why $\bm{w}$ moves alternately on the $w_1$ axis and the $w_2$ axis).

Exercise 7.2: The optimal constant learning rate

Solution

(1) For a fixed $\eta\gt0$, $\phi(\lambda)=1-\eta\lambda$ is a linear (decreasing) function of $\lambda$. For every $\lambda$ in the interval $[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]$ we have $\phi(\lambda_{\max})\le\phi(\lambda)\le\phi(\lambda_{\min})$, so $\abs{\phi(\lambda)}\le\max\bigl(\abs{\phi(\lambda_{\min})},\abs{\phi(\lambda_{\max})}\bigr)$. All the eigenvalues lie in this interval, and its two endpoints are themselves eigenvalues, so $\rho(\eta)=\max\bigl(\abs{1-\eta\lambda_{\min}},\abs{1-\eta\lambda_{\max}}\bigr)$.

(2) Let $a(\eta)=\abs{1-\eta\lambda_{\min}}$ and $b(\eta)=\abs{1-\eta\lambda_{\max}}$. For $0\lt\eta\le1/\lambda_{\max}$, both are of the form $1-\eta\lambda$ and $a\ge b$, so $\rho=a=1-\eta\lambda_{\min}$ decreases in $\eta$. For $\eta\ge1/\lambda_{\min}$, $b=\eta\lambda_{\max}-1\ge a=\eta\lambda_{\min}-1$, so $\rho=b$ increases. In between, for $1/\lambda_{\max}\le\eta\le1/\lambda_{\min}$, we have $a=1-\eta\lambda_{\min}$ (decreasing) and $b=\eta\lambda_{\max}-1$ (increasing), so $\rho=\max(a,b)$ is smallest at the point where $a=b$.

$$ \begin{aligned} &1-\eta\lambda_{\min}=\eta\lambda_{\max}-1\\ &\Longrightarrow\quad \eta_{\mathrm{opt}}=\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}} . \end{aligned} $$

Then

$$ \begin{aligned} \rho_{\mathrm{opt}}&=1-\frac{2\lambda_{\min}}{\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}}\\ &=\frac{\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min}}{\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}}=\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa+1} \end{aligned} $$

(in the last step we divided the numerator and denominator by $\lambda_{\min}$). That $\eta_{\mathrm{opt}}$ lies in $[1/\lambda_{\max},1/\lambda_{\min}]$ can be checked from $\lambda_{\min}\le\lambda_{\max}$, which gives $\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}\le2\lambda_{\max}$ and $\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}\ge2\lambda_{\min}$.

(3) After $k$ iterations the error is at most $\rho^{k}$ times the initial error, so from $\rho^{k}=\epsilon$ we get $k=\ln\epsilon/\ln\rho=\ln(1/\epsilon)/\bigl(-\ln\rho\bigr)$. Here $\rho=(\kappa-1)/(\kappa+1)=1-2/(\kappa+1)$, and for $\kappa\gg1$ the quantity $2/(\kappa+1)\approx2/\kappa$ is small, so $\ln(1-x)\approx-x$ gives $-\ln\rho\approx2/\kappa$. Hence $k\approx\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\ln(1/\epsilon)$. For example, for $\kappa=1000$ and $\epsilon=10^{-6}$, this is about $500\times13.8\approx6900$ iterations.

Exercise 7.3: Standardizing the firing temperature and the holding time

Solution

(1) Take the averages over $N=5$ samples. We have $\overline{1}=1$, $\overline{T}=6500/5=1300$, $\overline{t_{\mathrm h}}=(1+4+2+3+2)/5=2.4$, $\overline{T^{2}}=8.55\times10^{6}/5=1.71\times10^{6}$ (the numerator is $(1.21+1.44+1.69+1.96+2.25)\times10^{6}$), $\overline{Tt_{\mathrm h}}=15700/5=3140$ (the numerator is $1100+4800+2600+4200+3000$), and $\overline{t_{\mathrm h}^{2}}=(1+16+4+9+4)/5=6.8$. Therefore

$$ \bm{H}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1300&2.4\\1300&1.71\times10^{6}&3140\\2.4&3140&6.8\end{pmatrix}. $$

(2) $s_T^{2}=1.71\times10^{6}-1300^{2}=20000$, so $s_T=141.42\ ^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$. $s_t^{2}=6.8-2.4^{2}=1.04$, so $s_t=1.0198\ \mathrm{h}$. The covariance is $3140-1300\times2.4=20$, and the correlation coefficient is $r=20/(141.42\times1.0198)=0.1387$. After standardization, $\bm{H}$ takes the form given in the main text, $\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\0&1&r\\0&r&1\end{pmatrix}$, with eigenvalues $1,\ 1+r=1.1387,\ 1-r=0.8613$. The condition number is $1.1387/0.8613=1.322$.

(3) The upper limit of the learning rate is $2/(1.710\times10^{6})=1.17\times10^{-6}$, and the condition number is $1.710\times10^{6}/1.15\times10^{-2}\approx1.48\times10^{8}$. By Exercise 7.2 (3), the number of iterations with the optimal constant learning rate is about $\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\ln10^{6}=0.74\times10^{8}\times13.8\approx1.0\times10^{9}$. After standardization, $\rho=(1.322-1)/(1.322+1)=0.1387$ (which is $=r$), so $\ln10^{-6}/\ln0.1387=13.8/1.975\approx7.0$, and seven iterations suffice.

(4) It does not change. Expressing the time in minutes multiplies the values of $t_{\mathrm h}$ by 60, but the mean and the standard deviation are also multiplied by 60, so the standardized value $(t_{\mathrm h}-m)/s$ is the same. The correlation coefficient also does not depend on the units. Standardization removes the choice of unit and origin of each feature, so the standardized $\bm{H}$ does not depend on how the units are chosen. (The condition number before standardization does change when the unit is changed to minutes.)

Exercise 7.4: Variance of the minibatch gradient under sampling without replacement

Solution

(1) Even under sampling without replacement, by symmetry the index $i$ that enters a given position in the minibatch (say, the first) is any element of $\{1,\dots,N\}$ with probability $1/N$. Hence $\Ex[\bm{d}_i]=\frac1N\sum_n\bm{d}_n=\bm{0}$. Since $\bm{g}_{\mathcal{B}}-\nabla L=\frac1B\sum_{b=1}^{B}\bm{d}_{i_b}$, the linearity of expectation gives $\Ex[\bm{g}_{\mathcal{B}}]=\nabla L$ (unbiased).

(2) The pair of indices $(i,j)$ entering two different positions is equally likely, with probability $1/\{N(N-1)\}$, to be any of the $N(N-1)$ ordered pairs of distinct indices. On the other hand, since $\sum_n\bm{d}_n=\bm{0}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} 0=\Bigl\|\sum_n\bm{d}_n\Bigr\|^{2}&=\sum_n\norm{\bm{d}_n}^{2}+\sum_{n\ne n'}\bm{d}_n^{\tp}\bm{d}_{n'}\\ &=Ns^{2}+\sum_{n\ne n'}\bm{d}_n^{\tp}\bm{d}_{n'} . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\sum_{n\ne n'}\bm{d}_n^{\tp}\bm{d}_{n'}=-Ns^{2}$, and

$$ \Ex[\bm{d}_i^{\tp}\bm{d}_j]=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{n\ne n'}\bm{d}_n^{\tp}\bm{d}_{n'}=-\frac{s^{2}}{N-1}. $$

(3) Expanding as in the proof in the main text, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} &\Ex\Bigl[\norm{\bm{g}_{\mathcal{B}}-\nabla L}^{2}\Bigr]\\ &=\frac1{B^{2}}\Bigl(\sum_{b}\Ex\norm{\bm{d}_{i_b}}^{2}+\sum_{b\ne b'}\Ex[\bm{d}_{i_b}^{\tp}\bm{d}_{i_{b'}}]\Bigr)\\ &=\frac1{B^{2}}\Bigl(Bs^{2}-B(B-1)\frac{s^{2}}{N-1}\Bigr)\\ &=\frac{s^{2}}{B}\Bigl(1-\frac{B-1}{N-1}\Bigr)=\frac{s^{2}}{B}\cdot\frac{N-B}{N-1}. \end{aligned} $$

In Example 7.6, $s^{2}=6.6875$, $N=4$, and $B=2$, so $\frac{6.6875}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{3}=2.229$, which agrees with the variance $13.375/6=2.229$ obtained by enumerating all six cases. This is also consistent with the fact that for $B=N$ we have $(N-B)=0$, so the variance is 0 and the minibatch gradient coincides with the gradient over all the data.

Exercise 7.5: How much does momentum reduce the number of iterations?

Solution

(1) The eigenvalues are $100$ and $1$, so $\kappa=100$. We have $\eta_{\mathrm{opt}}=2/(100+1)=0.0198$ and $\rho_{\mathrm{opt}}=99/101=0.9802$. The number of iterations is $\ln10^{-6}/\ln0.9802$ $=-13.816/(-0.02000)\approx691$.

(2) $\eta=4/(\sqrt{100}+\sqrt1)^{2}=4/121=0.0331$ and $\beta_1=\bigl((10-1)/(10+1)\bigr)^{2}=(9/11)^{2}=0.669$. The contraction factor is $9/11=0.818$, and the number of iterations is $\ln10^{-6}/\ln0.818$ $=-13.816/(-0.2007)\approx69$. This is about 1/10 of that of gradient descent. (With these $\eta,\beta_1$, the eigenvalues of the iteration in each direction are double roots, and the error decreases as $\rho^{k}$ times a linear polynomial in $k$. As a result, the actual number of iterations is larger than this estimate: starting from $\bm{w}_0=(1,1)^{\tp}$, it takes 93 iterations to reduce $\norm{\bm{w}_k}$ by a factor of $10^{-6}$. This is still about 1/7 of that of gradient descent.) Since $\kappa$ has been replaced by $\sqrt{\kappa}$, for $\kappa=100$ the number of iterations is reduced to about one-tenth, since $\sqrt{100}=10$.

(3) For $\kappa=1$, the optimum is $\beta_1=\bigl((1-1)/(1+1)\bigr)^{2}=0$, so not using momentum at all (plain gradient descent) is the best. Since gradient descent with $\eta=1/\lambda$ already reaches the minimum in one step for this problem, carrying over the past velocity only produces overshoot ($w_3=-0.25$ in Example 7.8). Momentum is effective for problems with a large condition number, where the gradient accumulates and accelerates in the gentle direction while the oscillations in the steep direction cancel each other.

Exercise 7.6: Three inequality constraints

Solution

Write the constraints as $g_1=w_1+w_2-2\le0$, $g_2=-w_1\le0$, and $g_3=-w_2\le0$. Then $\nabla g_1=(1,1)^{\tp}$, $\nabla g_2=(-1,0)^{\tp}$, and $\nabla g_3=(0,-1)^{\tp}$.

(1) $\nabla f=(2(w_1-1),\ 2(w_2-2))^{\tp}$. The unconstrained minimum $(1,2)$ satisfies $w_1+w_2=3\gt2$, so it lies outside the hypotenuse, while it satisfies $w_1,w_2\gt0$. So we assume that only $g_1$ is active ($\alpha_2=\alpha_3=0$). From the stationarity conditions $2(w_1-1)+\alpha_1=0$ and $2(w_2-2)+\alpha_1=0$, we get $w_1=1-\alpha_1/2$ and $w_2=2-\alpha_1/2$. Substituting into $g_1=0$ gives $3-\alpha_1=2$, so $\alpha_1=1$. Hence $\bm{w}^{*}=(0.5,\ 1.5)$ and $f^{*}=0.25+0.25=0.5$. Check: 1. $\nabla f+\alpha_1\nabla g_1=(-1,-1)+(1,1)=\bm{0}$. 2. $g_1=0$, $g_2=-0.5$, and $g_3=-1.5$ are all $\le0$. 3. $\alpha=(1,0,0)\ge\bm{0}$. 4. $\alpha_1g_1=0$, $\alpha_2g_2=0\cdot(-0.5)=0$, and $\alpha_3g_3=0$. All the conditions are satisfied.

(2) $\nabla f=(2(w_1+1),\ 2(w_2-3))^{\tp}$. The unconstrained minimum $(-1,3)$ has $w_1\lt0$ and lies on the line $w_1+w_2=2$, outside the upper-left vertex $(0,2)$ of the triangle. First assume that only $g_1$ is active. Then $w_1=-1-\alpha_1/2$ and $w_2=3-\alpha_1/2$, and $g_1=0$ gives $2-\alpha_1=2$, so $\alpha_1=0$ and $\bm{w}=(-1,3)$; but $g_2=1\gt0$, which violates condition 2. Next assume that only $g_2$ is active. Then $w_1=0$, and $2(0+1)-\alpha_2=0$ gives $\alpha_2=2$ and $w_2=3$, but $g_1=1\gt0$, which is a violation. So we assume that both $g_1$ and $g_2$ are active (the vertex $(0,2)$). The stationarity conditions are

$$ \begin{aligned} 2(0+1)+\alpha_1-\alpha_2&=0,\\ 2(2-3)+\alpha_1&=0 . \end{aligned} $$

The second equation gives $\alpha_1=2$, and the first gives $\alpha_2=4$. Hence $\bm{w}^{*}=(0,2)$ and $f^{*}=1+1=2$. Check: 1. Adding $2(1,1)+4(-1,0)=(-2,2)$ to $\nabla f=(2,-2)$ gives $\bm{0}$. 2. $g_1=0$, $g_2=0$, and $g_3=-2$. 3. $\alpha=(2,4,0)\ge\bm{0}$. 4. $\alpha_1g_1=\alpha_2g_2=0$ and $\alpha_3=0$. All the conditions are satisfied. Since $f$ and all the $g_j$ are convex, this is the minimum.

Exercise 7.7: Relaxing a structure with stiff and soft bonds

Solution

Since $\bm{F}=(-k_1u_1,\ -k_2u_2)^{\tp}$, the update is $u_i\leftarrow(1-\eta k_i)u_i$ component by component, which is gradient descent with the Hessian matrix $\diag(k_1,k_2)$.

(1) $\eta\lt2/k_1=2/20=0.1\ \text{Å}^{2}/\mathrm{eV}$ (since $\eta F$ is a length in Å, the unit of $\eta$ is Å divided by eV/Å, that is, $\text{Å}^{2}/\mathrm{eV}$).

(2) $\eta_{\mathrm{opt}}=2/(20+0.5)=0.0976\ \text{Å}^{2}/\mathrm{eV}$. $\kappa=20/0.5=40$, and the contraction factor is $(40-1)/(40+1)=0.951$. Then $\ln10^{-3}/\ln0.951$ $=-6.908/(-0.0500)\approx138$ iterations.

(3) With $\eta=0.05$, $1-0.05\times20=0$, so the stiff direction relaxes in one step, but the soft direction shrinks only by a factor of $1-0.05\times0.5=0.975$ per step, which takes $\ln10^{-3}/\ln0.975\approx273$ iterations. With a learning rate equal to half the upper limit, the number of iterations in the slow direction is about twice as large.

(4) If $\eta_i=1/k_i$, then $1-\eta_ik_i=0$, and both directions reach $u_i=0$ in one iteration. This is $\bm{\Delta}=-\bm{H}^{-1}\nabla E$, obtained by multiplying by the inverse of the Hessian matrix $\diag(k_1,k_2)$, that is, Newton's method (Section 7.9). It cannot be used as it is for real structures, for the following reasons: (i) the coordinates of the normal modes (the eigenvectors of the force-constant matrix) are not known in advance, and in atomic coordinates stiff bonds and soft deformations are mixed; (ii) computing the $3N_{\mathrm a}\times3N_{\mathrm a}$ force-constant matrix requires many energy and force calculations and is expensive; (iii) far from a stable point the energy is not quadratic, and the matrix changes from place to place. Therefore BFGS or L-BFGS is used, which gradually approximates the Hessian matrix (or its inverse) from the changes in the forces.

Exercise 7.8: Newton's method does not depend on scale

Solution

(1) The components of $\bm{w}=\bm{A}\bm{w}'$ are $w_i=\sum_jA_{ij}w'_j$, so $\partial w_i/\partial w'_j=A_{ij}$. By the chain rule,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{L'}{w'_j}&=\sum_i\pdiff{L}{w_i}\pdiff{w_i}{w'_j}\\ &=\sum_iA_{ij}\pdiff{L}{w_i}=\bigl(\bm{A}^{\tp}\nabla_{\bm{w}}L\bigr)_j . \end{aligned} $$

Differentiating once more with respect to $w'_l$, in the same way,

$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial^{2}L'}{\partial w'_j\partial w'_l}&=\sum_i\sum_mA_{ij}\,\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial w_i\partial w_m}\,A_{ml}\\ &=(\bm{A}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{A})_{jl}. \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\bm{H}'=\bm{A}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{A}$.

(2) One step of Newton's method on the $\bm{w}'$ side is $\bm{H}'\bm{\Delta}'=-\nabla_{\bm{w}'}L'$, that is, $\bm{A}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{A}\bm{\Delta}'=-\bm{A}^{\tp}\nabla_{\bm{w}}L$. Since $\bm{A}^{\tp}$ is invertible, multiplying both sides from the left by $(\bm{A}^{\tp})^{-1}$ gives $\bm{H}(\bm{A}\bm{\Delta}')=-\nabla_{\bm{w}}L$. This is the same as the Newton equation $\bm{H}\bm{\Delta}=-\nabla_{\bm{w}}L$ on the $\bm{w}$ side, so if $\bm{H}$ is invertible, $\bm{A}\bm{\Delta}'=\bm{\Delta}$. Therefore $\bm{A}\bm{w}'_{k+1}=\bm{A}\bm{w}'_k+\bm{A}\bm{\Delta}'=\bm{w}_k+\bm{\Delta}=\bm{w}_{k+1}$. Whichever coordinates we compute in, we pass through the same points.

(3) Mapping one step of gradient descent on the $\bm{w}'$ side back to the $\bm{w}$ side gives

$$ \bm{A}\bigl(\bm{w}'_k-\eta\bm{A}^{\tp}\nabla_{\bm{w}}L\bigr)=\bm{w}_k-\eta\,\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\tp}\nabla_{\bm{w}}L $$

which does not coincide with $\bm{w}_k-\eta\nabla_{\bm{w}}L$ unless $\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\tp}=\bm{I}$ (a rotation, for example). That is, the path taken by gradient descent depends on the choice of coordinates (the units of the features). Standardization amounts to a coordinate change of the form $\bm{w}=\bm{A}\bm{w}'$ (dividing a feature by $s_j$ gives $w_j=w'_j/s_j$, so the diagonal entries of $\bm{A}$ are the reciprocals of the standard deviations, $1/s_j$; the part that subtracts the mean mixes the other components into the intercept component), and it makes the shape of the valley, $\bm{A}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{A}$, rounder for gradient descent. In Newton's method this transformation is built in automatically, so the sequence of points does not change whether or not we standardize (though, as far as rounding errors are concerned, standardization is also beneficial for Newton's method). Gradient descent with $\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\tp}$ multiplied in, of the form $\bm{w}_k-\eta\bm{P}\nabla L$ ($\bm{P}$ positive definite), is called preconditioned gradient descent; $\bm{P}=\bm{H}^{-1}$ gives Newton's method, and taking $\bm{P}$ to be diagonal is close to the idea behind RMSProp and Adam.

Chapter 8 Linear Regression Models — Basis Functions and Least Squares

Exercise 8.1: The formulas for fitting a straight line (derivation)

Solution

For the basis $(1,x)$ the normal equations are

$$ \begin{aligned} Nw_0+w_1\sum_n x_n&=\sum_n y_n,\\ w_0\sum_n x_n+w_1\sum_n x_n^{2}&=\sum_n x_ny_n . \end{aligned} $$

Eliminating $w_0$. Dividing the first equation by $N$ gives $w_0+w_1\bar{x}=\bar{y}$, that is, $w_0=\bar{y}-w_1\bar{x}$. This says that “the line $w_0+w_1x$ takes the value $\bar{y}$ at $x=\bar{x}$,” in other words, that the line passes through the point $(\bar{x},\bar{y})$.

The expression for $w_1$. Substitute $w_0=\bar{y}-w_1\bar{x}$ into the second equation and use $\sum_nx_n=N\bar{x}$.

$$ \begin{aligned} (\bar{y}-w_1\bar{x})N\bar{x}+w_1\sum_n x_n^{2}&=\sum_n x_ny_n\\ w_1\Bigl(\sum_n x_n^{2}-N\bar{x}^{2}\Bigr)&=\sum_n x_ny_n-N\bar{x}\bar{y}. \end{aligned} $$

Rewrite the right-hand side and the parenthesis on the left in terms of deviations.

$$ \begin{aligned}&\sum_n(x_n-\bar{x})(y_n-\bar{y})\\&=\sum_nx_ny_n-\bar{y}\sum_nx_n-\bar{x}\sum_ny_n+N\bar{x}\bar{y}\\&=\sum_nx_ny_n-N\bar{x}\bar{y}\end{aligned} $$

Hence the right-hand side is $S_{xy}$. Similarly (replacing $y$ by $x$), the parenthesis on the left is $S_{xx}$. Therefore $w_1=S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ (Eq. (8.7) of Chapter 8).

Properties of the residuals. For the residuals $r_n=y_n-w_0-w_1x_n$, the first normal equation is equivalent to $\sum_n r_n=0$ and the second to $\sum_n x_nr_n=0$ (they are merely the rewritten forms of $\sum_n y_n-Nw_0-w_1\sum_nx_n=0$ and $\sum_nx_ny_n-w_0\sum_nx_n-w_1\sum_nx_n^{2}=0$, respectively). This is nothing other than the statement that “the residuals are orthogonal to the two columns $(1,\dots,1)^{\tp}$ and $(x_1,\dots,x_N)^{\tp}$ of the design matrix.”

Exercise 8.2: The hat matrix and the bias of $\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{ML}}$ (derivation)

Solution

(1) $\bm{A}\equiv\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}$ is symmetric and nonsingular, so its inverse matrix is also symmetric.

  • $\bm{H}^{\tp}=(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp})^{\tp}(\bm{A}^{-1})^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{\Phi}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{H}$.
  • $\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{\Phi}\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi})\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}$ $=\bm{\Phi}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{H}$.
  • $\tr\bm{H}=\tr\bigl(\bm{\Phi}\,\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bigr)$ $=\tr\bigl(\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}\bigr)=\tr\bm{I}_M=M$.

Similarly, $\bm{I}-\bm{H}$ is also symmetric, $(\bm{I}-\bm{H})^{2}=\bm{I}-2\bm{H}+\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{I}-\bm{H}$, and $\tr(\bm{I}-\bm{H})=N-M$.

(2) The least-squares predicted value is $\hat{\bm{y}}=\bm{H}\bm{y}$, so the residual is $\bm{r}=(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{y}$. From $\bm{H}\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{A}=\bm{\Phi}$ we get $(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\Phi}\bm{w}_{\mathrm{true}}=\bm{0}$, so $\bm{r}=(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}$. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned}\norm{\bm{r}}^{2}&=\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}(\bm{I}-\bm{H})^{\tp}(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}\\&=\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}(\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}.\end{aligned} $$

A scalar equals its own trace, and the trace may be cycled in the order of a product, so

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex\bigl[\norm{\bm{r}}^{2}\bigr]&=\Ex\bigl[\tr\bigl((\bm{I}-\bm{H})\bm{\varepsilon}\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}\bigr)\bigr]\\&=\tr\bigl((\bm{I}-\bm{H})\Ex[\bm{\varepsilon}\bm{\varepsilon}^{\tp}]\bigr)\\ &=\sigma^{2}\tr(\bm{I}-\bm{H})=\sigma^{2}(N-M). \end{aligned} $$

Since $\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{ML}}=\norm{\bm{r}}^{2}/N$, we have $\Ex[\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{ML}}]=\frac{N-M}{N}\sigma^{2}$. The quantity $\norm{\bm{r}}^{2}/(N-M)$ is an unbiased estimate. The same property was also used in Exercise 6.1 of Chapter 6.

(3) In Example 8.2, $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}3&3\\3&5\end{pmatrix}$, and its inverse matrix is $\frac16\begin{pmatrix}5&-3\\-3&3\end{pmatrix}$. Since the $n$th row is $(1,x_n)$, $H_{nm}=\frac16(5-3x_n-3x_m+3x_nx_m)$. Substituting $x=0,1,2$ gives

$$ \bm{H}=\frac16\begin{pmatrix}5&2&-1\\2&2&2\\-1&2&5\end{pmatrix}. $$

It is symmetric, and its trace is $(5+2+5)/6=2=M$. The (1,1) component of $\bm{H}^{2}$ is $(25+4+1)/36=30/36=5/6$, which agrees with $H_{11}$ (the other components work out the same way). $\bm{H}\bm{y}=\frac16(5+2-4,\ 2+2+8,$ $-1+2+20)^{\tp}$ $=(0.5,\ 2.0,\ 3.5)^{\tp}$ agrees with $\hat{\bm{y}}$ in Example 8.2.

Exercise 8.3: The bowing parameter when the end members are known (a materials topic)

Solution

(1) Rearranging Eq. (8.23) of Chapter 8 gives $E_g-[(1-x)E_{\mathrm{A}}+xE_{\mathrm{B}}]=b\cdot\{-x(1-x)\}$. The target value is $z_n=E_{g,n}-[(1-x_n)1.20+x_n2.40]$, the basis function is $\psi(x)=-x(1-x)$, and the model is a linear model with a single coefficient $b$, namely $z=b\,\psi(x)$ (no constant term).

(2) The linear part $1.20+1.20x_n$ takes the values $1.44,\ 1.68,\ 1.80,\ 1.92,\ 2.16$. The targets are $z_n=-0.11,\ -0.21,\ -0.19,\ -0.22,\ -0.11$. The basis values are $\psi_n=-0.16,\ -0.24,\ -0.25,$ $-0.24,\ -0.16$. From the normal equation for a single coefficient, $b\sum_n\psi_n^{2}=\sum_n\psi_nz_n$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \sum_n\psi_nz_n&=0.0176+0.0504+0.0475\\&\quad+0.0528+0.0176=0.1859,\\ \sum_n\psi_n^{2}&=0.0256+0.0576+0.0625\\&\quad+0.0576+0.0256=0.2289,\\ b&=0.1859/0.2289=0.812\ \mathrm{eV}. \end{aligned} $$

(3) For a flat prior distribution ($\sigma_w\to\infty$), $S_N=\sigma^{2}/\sum_n\psi_n^{2}=0.03^{2}/0.2289$, and the standard deviation is $0.03/\sqrt{0.2289}=0.03/0.4784=0.063$ eV. Thus $b=0.81\pm0.06$ eV.

(4) At $x=0.3$, the linear part is $1.20+1.20\times0.3=1.56$ and $\psi=-0.3\times0.7=-0.21$. The predictive mean is $1.56+0.812\times(-0.21)=1.389$ eV. The standard deviation due to lack of knowledge is $\abs{\psi}\sqrt{S_N}=0.21\times0.063=0.013$ eV (the standard deviation of an observed value is $\sqrt{0.013^{2}+0.03^{2}}=0.033$ eV). Because we treated the end members as known and reduced the number of coefficients to one, the uncertainty is much smaller than in the three-coefficient fit of Chapter 8, Section 8.10 (standard deviation of $b$ equal to 0.14 eV, 12 data points). Note, however, that if the values of the end members contain errors, $b$ is biased by that amount.

Exercise 8.4: An Arrhenius plot at four temperatures (a materials topic)

Solution

(1) $u=1000/T=2.0000,\ 1.6667,\ 1.4286,\ 1.2500$ (1000 times K$^{-1}$), $\bar{u}=1.5863$, $\bar{l}=-6.00$ (with $l=\ln\sigma_{\mathrm{ion}}$). The deviations are $u_n-\bar{u}=0.4137,\ 0.0804,$ $-0.1577,\ -0.3363$ and $l_n-\bar{l}=-2.14,\ -0.37,\ 0.82,\ 1.69$.

$$ \begin{aligned} S_{uu}&=0.1711+0.0065+0.0249+0.1131\\&=0.3156,\\ S_{ul}&=-0.8853-0.0297-0.1294-0.5684\\&=-1.6127,\\ w_1&=S_{ul}/S_{uu}=-5.110,\\ w_0&=\bar{l}-w_1\bar{u}=2.107. \end{aligned} $$

$E_a=-1000\,k_{\mathrm B}w_1$ $=1000\times8.617\times10^{-5}\times5.110$ $=0.440$ eV.

(2) $\sigma/\sqrt{S_{uu}}=0.10/\sqrt{0.3156}=0.178$. The standard deviation of $E_a$ is $1000\,k_{\mathrm B}\times0.178=0.0862\times0.178=0.015$ eV. Thus $E_a=0.440\pm0.015$ eV.

(3) $u_*=1000/900=1.1111$. The predictive mean is $2.107-5.110\times1.1111=-3.572$ ($\sigma_{\mathrm{ion}}\approx e^{-3.572}=0.028$ S/cm). The variance due to lack of knowledge is

$$ \begin{aligned}&0.10^{2}\bigl[1/4+(1.1111-1.5863)^{2}/0.3156\bigr]\\&=0.01\times(0.25+0.716)=0.00966,\end{aligned} $$

so its standard deviation is 0.098. The standard deviation of an observed value is $\sqrt{0.00966+0.01}=0.140$. Since 900 K lies outside the measurement range (500–800 K), the contribution from lack of knowledge has grown to the same order as the noise.

This expression is obtained by using the centered basis $(1,\ u-\bar{u})$, for which $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}=\diag(N,\ S_{uu})$ (the cross term vanishes, $\sum_n(u_n-\bar{u})=0$) and, for a flat prior distribution, $\bm{S}_N=\sigma^{2}\diag(1/N,\ 1/S_{uu})$, and then sandwiching this between $\bm{\phi}_*=(1,\ u_*-\bar{u})^{\tp}$.

(4) If the error $\delta$ is constant in absolute value, the error in $\ln\sigma_{\mathrm{ion}}$ is approximately $\delta/\sigma_{\mathrm{ion},n}$, which is larger for the low-temperature points (where the conductivity is small). Weight each point by its variance $\sigma_n^{2}=(\delta/\sigma_{\mathrm{ion},n})^{2}$ and minimize $\sum_n r_n^{2}/\sigma_n^{2}$ (weighted least squares). In this example the conductivity at 500 K is about $e^{3.8}\approx45$ times smaller than at 800 K, so the error in the logarithm is about 45 times larger and the weight is about $45^{2}\approx2000$ times smaller; the 500 K point then hardly affects the slope. Alternatively, do not take the logarithm and fit $\sigma_{\mathrm{ion}}=\sigma_0e^{-E_a/(k_{\mathrm B}T)}$ directly by nonlinear least squares (the methods of Chapter 7).

Exercise 8.5: The ridge coefficients shrink as $\lambda$ increases (derivation)

Solution

(1) From Eq. (8.14) of Chapter 8, $\hat{\bm{w}}_\lambda=\sum_ic_i(\lambda)\bm{v}_i$ with $c_i(\lambda)=\dfrac{s_i}{s_i^{2}+\lambda}\bm{u}_i^{\tp}\bm{y}$. Since $\bm{v}_i^{\tp}\bm{v}_j=\delta_{ij}$,

$$ \norm{\hat{\bm{w}}_\lambda}^{2}=\sum_i c_i(\lambda)^{2}=\sum_i\frac{s_i^{2}}{(s_i^{2}+\lambda)^{2}}(\bm{u}_i^{\tp}\bm{y})^{2}. $$

Differentiating with respect to $\lambda$, each term becomes $-\dfrac{2s_i^{2}}{(s_i^{2}+\lambda)^{3}}(\bm{u}_i^{\tp}\bm{y})^{2}\le0$. Therefore $\norm{\hat{\bm{w}}_\lambda}^{2}$ decreases monotonically for $\lambda\ge0$ (strictly if there is a term with $\bm{u}_i^{\tp}\bm{y}\ne0$). It approaches 0 as $\lambda\to\infty$.

(2) Since $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I}=\bm{V}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}+\lambda\bm{I})\bm{V}^{\tp}$, its inverse matrix is $\bm{V}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{V}^{\tp}$. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} &\bm{\Phi}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\\&=\bm{U}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\tp}\bm{V}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{V}^{\tp}\bm{V}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{U}^{\tp}\\ &=\bm{U}\,\bm{\Sigma}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}\,\bm{U}^{\tp}. \end{aligned} $$

Taking the trace and using $\tr(\bm{U}\bm{D}\bm{U}^{\tp})=\tr(\bm{D}\bm{U}^{\tp}\bm{U})=\tr\bm{D}$, we obtain $\tr\bm{D}=\sum_is_i^{2}/(s_i^{2}+\lambda)=\mathrm{df}(\lambda)$. At $\lambda=0$ it agrees with the trace $M$ of the hat matrix (Exercise 8.2).

(3) In Example 8.6, $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}=\diag(4,\ 0.2)$ and $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{y}=(6.0,\ 0.34)^{\tp}$. For $\lambda=1$, $w_0=6.0/5=1.2$ and $w_1=0.34/1.2=0.283$. The effective degrees of freedom are $4/5+0.2/1.2=0.800+0.167=0.967$. $\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}$ decreases monotonically: $1.5^{2}+1.7^{2}=5.14$ for $\lambda=0$, $1.4286^{2}+0.85^{2}=2.76$ for $\lambda=0.2$, and $1.2^{2}+0.283^{2}=1.52$ for $\lambda=1$.

Exercise 8.6: Sequential updating and batch updating (derivation and computation)

Solution

(1) First update with $\data_1$. From the prior distribution $\Normal(\bm{0},\sigma_w^{2}\bm{I})$, Eq. (8.20) of Chapter 8 gives

$$ \begin{aligned}\bm{S}_1^{-1}&=\sigma_w^{-2}\bm{I}+\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_1,\\ \bm{S}_1^{-1}\bm{m}_1&=\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{y}_1 .\end{aligned} $$

Taking this as the prior distribution ($\bm{m}_0\to\bm{m}_1$, $\bm{S}_0\to\bm{S}_1$) and updating with $\data_2$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{S}_2^{-1}&=\bm{S}_1^{-1}+\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_2\\&=\sigma_w^{-2}\bm{I}+\sigma^{-2}(\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_1+\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_2),\\ \bm{S}_2^{-1}\bm{m}_2&=\bm{S}_1^{-1}\bm{m}_1+\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{y}_2\\&=\sigma^{-2}(\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{y}_1+\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{y}_2). \end{aligned} $$

The design matrix of the full data is $\bm{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Phi}_1\\ \bm{\Phi}_2\end{pmatrix}$ and the target is $\bm{y}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{y}_1\\ \bm{y}_2\end{pmatrix}$, so $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_1+\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}_2$ and $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{y}=\bm{\Phi}_1^{\tp}\bm{y}_1+\bm{\Phi}_2^{\tp}\bm{y}_2$. Hence $\bm{S}_2,\bm{m}_2$ agree with $\bm{S}_N,\bm{m}_N$ obtained in one batch (Eq. (8.18)). The key point is that the precision matrix and “precision × mean” are sums over the data.

(2) The posterior distribution in Example 8.7 has $m_1=0.90625$ and $S_1^{-1}=16$. For the fourth point $x=4$, $y=4.1$ ($\sigma=1$),

$$ \begin{aligned}S_2^{-1}&=16+\frac{4^{2}}{1}=32,\\ m_2&=\frac{16\times0.90625+4\times4.1}{32}\\&=\frac{14.5+16.4}{32}=0.9656 .\end{aligned} $$

In batch form, $\sum x_n^{2}=1+4+9+16=30$, $\sum x_ny_n=14.5+16.4=30.9$, $S^{-1}=1/0.5+30=32$, and $m=30.9/32=0.9656$, which agrees. The posterior standard deviation is $1/\sqrt{32}=0.177$ (it has shrunk from 0.25 with three points).

Exercise 8.7: The predictive variance of a line determined by two points (computation)

Solution

(1) $\bm{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$ and $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix}$. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned}\bm{S}_N^{-1}&=\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sigma_w^{2}}+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\bm{I}=\frac{\sigma^{2}+2\sigma_w^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sigma_w^{2}}\bm{I},\\ \bm{S}_N&=\frac{\sigma^{2}\sigma_w^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+2\sigma_w^{2}}\bm{I}.\end{aligned} $$

(2) Since $\bm{\phi}_*=(1,x_*)^{\tp}$,

$$ \bm{\phi}_*^{\tp}\bm{S}_N\bm{\phi}_*=\frac{\sigma^{2}\sigma_w^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+2\sigma_w^{2}}\,(1+x_*^{2}). $$

It is smallest at $x_*=0$ (the midpoint of the two points) and grows in proportion to $x_*^{2}$.

(3) For $\sigma_w=1$ and $\sigma=0.5$, the coefficient is $0.25/(0.25+2)=1/9$. For $x_*=0,1,2,3$ the values are $1/9=0.111$, $2/9=0.222$, $5/9=0.556$, $10/9=1.111$. At $x_*=3$ it exceeds four times the noise variance $\sigma^{2}=0.25$.

$\bm{y}$ does not enter $\bm{S}_N$ (Eq. (8.18) of Chapter 8). Therefore the variance due to lack of knowledge is determined only by “where we measured” (the design matrix), not by the measured values. For this reason, before carrying out any measurement, we can compute at which inputs measuring would reduce the predictive uncertainty the most. This is the foundation of the idea of experimental design (Chapter 13). Note, however, that this holds when $\sigma^{2}$ and $\sigma_w^{2}$ are fixed; if they are chosen by the marginal likelihood (the Advanced Topic box in Chapter 8, Section 8.7), the variance also depends on $\bm{y}$.

Exercise 8.8: Extrapolation with Gaussian basis functions (discussion and a materials topic)

Solution

(1) $\phi_j(s)=\exp[-(s-c_j)^{2}/(2\times0.25^{2})]$ $=\exp[-(s-c_j)^{2}/0.125]$.

  • $s=1.2$: for $c=1$, $\exp(-0.04/0.125)=\exp(-0.32)=0.726$; for $c=0.75$, $\exp(-0.2025/0.125)=\exp(-1.62)=0.198$; for $c=0.5$, $\exp(-3.92)=0.020$. $\bm{\phi}^{\tp}\bm{\phi}\approx0.527+0.039+0.0004=0.567$. The variance from the prior distribution alone is $100\times0.567=56.7$ (standard deviation 7.5 %).
  • $s=1.4$: for $c=1$, $\exp(-0.16/0.125)=\exp(-1.28)=0.278$; for $c=0.75$, $\exp(-3.38)=0.034$. $\bm{\phi}^{\tp}\bm{\phi}\approx0.0773+0.0012=0.0785$, and the variance is $7.85$ (standard deviation 2.8 %).
  • $s=2.0$: for $c=1$, $\exp(-1/0.125)=\exp(-8)=3.4\times10^{-4}$, and the others are even smaller. $\bm{\phi}^{\tp}\bm{\phi}\approx1.1\times10^{-7}$, and the variance is $1.1\times10^{-5}$ (standard deviation 0.003 %).

(At $s=0$, $1+2\times0.6065^{2}+2\times0.1353^{2}+\dots=1.77$ and the variance is 177, so the prior variance is larger inside the range of the data.)

(2) At 1700 °C ($s=2.0$), regardless of whether there are data, the variance due to lack of knowledge becomes almost 0, and the predictive mean is $\bm{m}_N^{\tp}\bm{\phi}\approx0$, that is, it returns to the baseline value of 88 %. The band on the screen is only the band of the observed value, ±2σ = ±2 %, but this does not mean that “the relative density at 1700 °C is well known to be 88 ± 2 %.” It is a value that comes out mechanically from the model assumption that the basis functions vanish at this temperature, and it says nothing about the reliability of the extrapolation. For a real sintered body, neither the data nor the basis functions tell us whether densification continues at still higher temperatures or whether the density decreases because of grain growth or decomposition.

(3) Examples: (a) Add a constant and linear basis (the basis $1,s$) or polynomial basis functions so that the model can express that “some form continues” outside the data (the band of a polynomial widens outside). (b) Place the centers of the basis functions out to the range where we want to predict (for example $[-2,2]$), or increase the width $r$. (c) Use the Gaussian process (Chapter 12), the limit of an infinite number of basis functions, so that far from the data the variance returns to the prior variance $\sigma_f^{2}$. (d) Use a functional form based on the physics of sintering (for example a rate equation for densification) as the basis. In every case, the band remains the width under the model's assumptions, so predictions outside the range should be checked by confirmation experiments.
