Chapter 9 Linear Classification Models — Decision Boundaries and Probabilities

Exercise 9.1: Distance to the boundary

Solution

(1) Since $f(\bm{x})=3x_1-4x_2+5$, the decision boundary is $3x_1-4x_2+5=0$, that is, the line $x_2=\tfrac34x_1+\tfrac54$.

(2) $\norm{\bm{w}}=\sqrt{3^{2}+(-4)^{2}}=5$. The signed distance is $r=f(\bm{x})/\norm{\bm{w}}$ (Eq. (9.2) of Chapter 9).

  • Point $(1,2)$: $f=3-8+5=0$. Since $r=0$, this point lies on the boundary. By the convention that $f\ge0$ means class 1, it is predicted to be class 1, but it is a point that cannot be said to belong to either class.
  • Point $(-1,1)$: $f=-3-4+5=-2$. Since $r=-2/5=-0.4$, it is class 0.
  • Origin: $f=b=5$. Since $r=5/5=1$, it is class 1.

(3) The required point is the foot of the perpendicular dropped from the origin to the boundary. The origin is at distance 1 on the side in the direction of $\bm{w}$, so we need only move from it by 1 in the direction of $-\bm{w}$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{x}_{\perp}&=\bm{0}-1\times\frac{\bm{w}}{\norm{\bm{w}}}\\ &=-\frac{(3,-4)}{5}=(-0.6,\ 0.8). \end{aligned} $$

Check: $3\times(-0.6)-4\times0.8+5$ $=-1.8-3.2+5=0$, so the point lies on the boundary, and its distance from the origin is $\sqrt{0.36+0.64}=1$.

Exercise 9.2: Sigmoid and tanh

Solution

(1) Let $u=a/2$. Then $\tanh u=\dfrac{e^{u}-e^{-u}}{e^{u}+e^{-u}}$. Multiplying the numerator and denominator by $e^{-u}$ gives $\tanh u=\dfrac{1-e^{-2u}}{1+e^{-2u}}=\dfrac{1-e^{-a}}{1+e^{-a}}$. Hence

$$ \begin{aligned} &\tfrac12\{1+\tanh(a/2)\}\\ &=\frac12\cdot\frac{(1+e^{-a})+(1-e^{-a})}{1+e^{-a}}\\ &=\frac{1}{1+e^{-a}}=\sigma(a). \end{aligned} $$

The sigmoid is tanh stretched horizontally by a factor of 2, with the range of values moved from $(-1,1)$ to $(0,1)$.

(2) Let $p=\sigma(a)$. Then $1-p=e^{-a}/(1+e^{-a})$, so $p/(1-p)=1/e^{-a}=e^{a}$, and taking the logarithm of both sides gives $a=\ln\{p/(1-p)\}=\ell(p)$. Conversely, if $\ell(p)=a$, solving $p/(1-p)=e^{a}$ gives $p=\sigma(a)$. Hence $\ell$ and $\sigma$ are inverse functions of each other. The derivative is

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{\ell}{p}&=\diff{}{p}\bigl\{\ln p-\ln(1-p)\bigr\}\\ &=\frac1p+\frac{1}{1-p}=\frac{1}{p(1-p)} . \end{aligned} $$

This also agrees with the derivative of the inverse function, $1/\sigma'(a)=1/\{\sigma(1-\sigma)\}$.

(3) We use Eq. (9.9) of Chapter 9, $\sigma'=\sigma(1-\sigma)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{}{a}\ln\sigma(a)&=\frac{\sigma'(a)}{\sigma(a)}=1-\sigma(a),\\ \frac{\dd^{2}}{\dd a^{2}}\ln\sigma(a)&=-\sigma'(a)\\ &=-\sigma(a)\bigl(1-\sigma(a)\bigr). \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\dfrac{\dd^{2}}{\dd a^{2}}\{-\ln\sigma(a)\}=\sigma(a)(1-\sigma(a))\gt0$, and $-\ln\sigma(a)$ is a convex function. Likewise $-\ln(1-\sigma(a))=-\ln\sigma(-a)$ is convex, so the cross-entropy loss for a single point is convex in $a$. Since $a$ is a linear (first-degree) expression in $(\bm{w},b)$, the loss, being the composition of a convex function with a linear expression, is also convex in $(\bm{w},b)$.

Exercise 9.3: Conditions for the Hessian matrix to be positive definite

Solution

(1) Let $s_n=\hat{y}_n(1-\hat{y}_n)$. Then $0\lt s_n\le1/4$ (since $0\lt\hat{y}_n\lt1$). For any $\bm{v}\in\R^{M+1}$,

$$ \bm{v}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{v}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}s_n(\bm{v}^{\tp}\tilde{\bm{\phi}}_n)^{2}\ge0 . $$

Suppose equality holds. Since $s_n\gt0$, every term is 0; that is, $\bm{v}^{\tp}\tilde{\bm{\phi}}_n=0$ for all $n$, which in matrix form reads $\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}\bm{v}=\bm{0}$. If the rank of $\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}$ is $M+1$ (the columns are linearly independent), the only solution of $\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}\bm{v}=\bm{0}$ is $\bm{v}=\bm{0}$. Therefore, if $\bm{v}\ne\bm{0}$, then $\bm{v}^{\tp}\bm{H}\bm{v}\gt0$, and $\bm{H}$ is positive definite.

(2) The Hessian matrix of the regularization term $\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}$ with respect to $\bm{w}$ is $\lambda\bm{I}_M$. Writing the block for the $\bm{w}$ part as $\bm{H}_{ww}+\lambda\bm{I}_M$, for any $\bm{u}\in\R^{M}$ with $\bm{u}\ne\bm{0}$ we have

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{u}^{\tp}(\bm{H}_{ww}+\lambda\bm{I}_M)\bm{u}&=\bm{u}^{\tp}\bm{H}_{ww}\bm{u}+\lambda\norm{\bm{u}}^{2}\\ &\ge\lambda\norm{\bm{u}}^{2}\gt0 \end{aligned} $$

($\bm{H}_{ww}$ is positive semidefinite for the same reason as in (1)). It is positive definite regardless of the rank of the data.

(3) A positive definite Hessian matrix guarantees that “the loss is strictly convex” and implies that a minimum point, if it exists, is unique. However, it does not guarantee that “a minimum point exists.” For example, $g(w)=e^{-w}$ is strictly convex because $g''=e^{-w}\gt0$, but it only approaches 0 as $w\to\infty$ and has no minimum. The loss for linearly separable data is the same: moving in a certain direction keeps lowering the loss, and the curvature $s_n$ in that direction approaches 0 ($\hat{y}_n\to0$ or 1). The Hessian is positive definite at every finite point, but the minimum point lies infinitely far away. When regularization is added, the lower bound $\lambda$ in (2) keeps the curvature from approaching 0, and in addition the loss grows without bound as $\norm{\bm{w}}\to\infty$, so the minimum point comes to lie at a finite position.

Exercise 9.4: One step of Newton's method

Solution

The data are $x=(-1.5,-0.5,0.5,1.5)$ and $y=(0,1,0,1)$. Let $\tilde{\bm{\phi}}_n=(x_n,1)^{\tp}$ and $\tilde{\bm{w}}=(w,b)^{\tp}$.

(1) At $w=b=0$, $\hat{y}_n=0.5$ and $s_n=\hat{y}_n(1-\hat{y}_n)=0.25$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{H}&=0.25\sum_n\begin{pmatrix}x_n^{2}&x_n\\x_n&1\end{pmatrix}\\ &=0.25\begin{pmatrix}5&0\\0&4\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1.25&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned} $$

($\sum x_n^{2}=2.25+0.25+0.25+2.25=5$, $\sum x_n=0$.) By Example 9.4 (2), the gradient is $\nabla L=(-1.0,\ 0)^{\tp}$.

(2) Solving $\bm{H}\bm{d}=\nabla L$ gives $\bm{d}=(-1.0/1.25,\ 0/1)^{\tp}=(-0.8,\ 0)^{\tp}$. The update $\tilde{\bm{w}}\leftarrow\tilde{\bm{w}}-\bm{d}$ gives $(w,b)=(0.8,\ 0)$. Then $a_n=0.8x_n=(-1.2,\ -0.4,\ 0.4,\ 1.2)$ and $\hat{y}_n=(0.2315,\ 0.4013,\ 0.5987,\ 0.7685)$, and the loss is

$$ \begin{aligned} L&=-2\ln0.7685-2\ln0.4013\\ &=2\times0.2633+2\times0.9130\\ &\approx2.3526 . \end{aligned} $$

Gradient descent ($\eta=0.5$) gave $w=0.5$ and $L=2.4256$ after one step, and $w=0.700$ and $L=2.3669$ after two steps. So one step of Newton's method ($L=2.3526$) gets closer to the minimum $L^{*}\approx2.3475$ than two steps of gradient descent do. This is because Newton's method uses the curvature $\bm{H}$ to decide the size of each step direction by direction.

(3) Second step: at $(w,b)=(0.8,0)$, $s_n=(0.1779,\ 0.2403,\ 0.2403,\ 0.1779)$, $\bm{H}=\begin{pmatrix}0.9207&0\\0&0.8363\end{pmatrix}$, $\partial L/\partial w=-0.0957$, and $\partial L/\partial b=0$. Then $w\leftarrow0.8+0.0957/0.9207=0.9040$. At the third step $w=0.90818$, which agrees with the minimum point $w^{*}=0.90818$ to more than three digits (the 0.904 of the second step differs in the third digit). Near the minimum point, the error shrinks by roughly squaring at each step (quadratic convergence).

Exercise 9.5: A two-class softmax is logistic regression

Solution

(1) Dividing the numerator and denominator of $\hat{y}_1=\dfrac{e^{a_1}}{e^{a_1}+e^{a_2}}$ by $e^{a_1}$ gives $\hat{y}_1=\dfrac{1}{1+e^{-(a_1-a_2)}}=\sigma(a_1-a_2)$. Here

$$ \begin{aligned} a_1-a_2&=(\bm{w}_1-\bm{w}_2)^{\tp}\bm{\phi}+(b_1-b_2)\\ &=\bm{w}^{\tp}\bm{\phi}+b \end{aligned} $$

so $\hat{y}_1$ equals the output of logistic regression with $\bm{w}=\bm{w}_1-\bm{w}_2$ and $b=b_1-b_2$. $\hat{y}_2=1-\hat{y}_1$ agrees as well.

(2) By Eq. (9.19) of Chapter 9, $\nabla_{\bm{w}_k}L=\sum_n(\hat{y}_{nk}-y_{nk})\bm{\phi}_n$. The sum over $k=1,2$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} &\sum_n\bigl\{(\hat{y}_{n1}+\hat{y}_{n2})-(y_{n1}+y_{n2})\bigr\}\bm{\phi}_n\\ &=\sum_n(1-1)\bm{\phi}_n=\bm{0} \end{aligned} $$

(the probabilities sum to 1, and so does the one-hot vector). Meaning: even if the same vector $\bm{c}$ is added to both $\bm{w}_1$ and $\bm{w}_2$, $a_1-a_2$ does not change, and neither does the loss. The component of the gradient in the direction $(\bm{c},\bm{c})$, along which the loss does not change, is 0, and this appears as “the sum is 0.” For this reason the solution of $K$-class softmax regression is not uniquely determined, and it is fixed either by setting the weights of one class to $\bm{0}$ or by adding regularization.

(3) Let $\hat{y}_k=e^{a_k}/Z$ and $Z=\sum_je^{a_j}$. Then $\partial Z/\partial a_j=e^{a_j}$. By the quotient rule,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{\hat{y}_k}{a_j}&=\frac{\delta_{kj}e^{a_k}Z-e^{a_k}e^{a_j}}{Z^{2}}\\ &=\frac{e^{a_k}}{Z}\Bigl(\delta_{kj}-\frac{e^{a_j}}{Z}\Bigr)\\ &=\hat{y}_k(\delta_{kj}-\hat{y}_j). \end{aligned} $$

For $K=2$ and $k=j=1$ this becomes $\hat{y}_1(1-\hat{y}_1)$, which agrees with the derivative of the sigmoid.

Exercise 9.6: Inspection threshold for sintered parts

Solution

There are $5000\times0.02=100$ defective parts and 4900 good parts.

(1) $t=1.0$: recall $=1-\Phi(1-2)=\Phi(1)=0.8413$ and false positive rate $=1-\Phi(1)=0.1587$. TP $=100\times0.8413=84.1$, FN $=15.9$, FP $=4900\times0.1587=777.4$, TN $=4122.6$. Precision $=84.1/(84.1+777.4)=0.0977$, F1 $=2\times0.0977\times0.8413$ $/(0.0977+0.8413)=0.175$, accuracy $=(84.1+4122.6)/5000=0.841$.
$t=2.0$: recall $=1-\Phi(0)=0.5$ and false positive rate $=1-\Phi(2)=0.02275$. TP $=50$, FN $=50$, FP $=111.5$, TN $=4788.5$. Precision $=50/161.5=0.310$, F1 $=2\times0.310\times0.5/0.810=0.382$, accuracy $=4838.5/5000=0.968$.

(2) Expected cost $=20\times\mathrm{FN}+1\times\mathrm{FP}$. For $t=1.0$: $20\times15.9+777.4=1094.7$. For $t=2.0$: $20\times50+111.5=1111.5$. For “everything is good”: FN $=100$ and FP $=0$, giving $2000$. Although $t=2.0$ is better than $t=1.0$ in accuracy and F1, it is slightly worse in cost. The conclusion changes depending on which metric is used to choose.

(3) The class-conditional densities of the score are Gaussian distributions with variance 1 and means 0 and 2, so by the same calculation as Example 9.2 of Chapter 9 the log-odds are $a(s)=2s-2+\ln(0.02/0.98)$ $=2s-2-3.892$. The probability at the threshold is $p^{*}=1/(1+20)=1/21$, and in log-odds this is $\ln\{(1/21)/(20/21)\}=-\ln20=-2.996$. Solving $a(s)=-2.996$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} 2s&=2+3.892-2.996=2.896,\\ t^{*}&=1.448 . \end{aligned} $$

Then recall $=\Phi(0.552)=0.710$, false positive rate $=1-\Phi(1.448)=0.0738$, FN $=29.0$, FP $=361.6$, and the expected cost is $20\times29.0+361.6=942.6$. This is smaller than that for either of $t=1.0$ and $2.0$, and the same $t\approx1.448$ is obtained even if we vary $t$ finely and minimize the cost numerically.

Exercise 9.7: Expressing the range of the tolerance factor with a discriminant function

Solution

(1) $a(t)=-200t^{2}+376.8t-174.735$. By the quadratic formula, the solutions of the quadratic equation $a(t)=0$ are

$$ \begin{aligned} t&=\frac{376.8\pm\sqrt{376.8^{2}-4\times200\times174.735}}{2\times200}\\ &=\frac{376.8\pm\sqrt{141978.24-139788}}{400}\\ &=\frac{376.8\pm46.8}{400}, \end{aligned} $$

$t_1=0.825$ and $t_2=1.059$. Since the coefficient of $t^{2}$ is negative, $a(t)\gt0$ ($\hat{y}\gt0.5$) holds for $0.825\lt t\lt1.059$. We can write $a(t)=-200(t-0.825)(t-1.059)$.

(2) $t=0.94$: $a=-200\times0.115\times(-0.119)=2.737$ and $\hat{y}=\sigma(2.737)=0.939$. $t=0.80$: $a=-200\times(-0.025)\times(-0.259)=-1.295$ and $\hat{y}=0.215$. $t=1.10$: $a=-200\times0.275\times0.041=-2.255$ and $\hat{y}=0.095$.

(3) Since $a=wt+b$ is a linear function of $t$, if $w\ne0$ the sign changes at only one point, $t=-b/w$, and the region where $a\gt0$ is a half-line on one side, either “$t\gt-b/w$” or “$t\lt-b/w$.” If $w=0$, the sign is the same everywhere. In either case, $a\gt0$ cannot hold on only a bounded interval $(t_1,t_2)$. In the plane of the features $(t,t^{2})$, $a=w_1t+w_2t^{2}+b=0$ is a line, and the data points $(t,t^{2})$ lie on a parabola. That line cuts the parabola at two points, so on the $t$ axis it becomes a test for an interval.

(4) Since $\tau\lt4.18$ is class 1 (perovskite), we can write $f(\tau)=4.18-\tau\gt0$. Then $w=-1$ and $b=4.18$ (a positive multiple of these gives the same boundary). The Platt scaling that the paper used to obtain probabilities from $\tau$ amounts to fitting $A\gt0$ and $B$ in $1/\{1+\exp(A\tau+B)\}$ (the form in Section 9.8), and the boundary is $\tau=-B/A$.

Exercise 9.8: Choosing between generative and discriminative

Solution

(1) Generative classification with a shared covariance: the means take $2\times20=40$ parameters, the covariance takes $20\times21/2=210$, and the prior probability takes 1, for a total of 251. With a separate covariance for each class, the total is $40+2\times210+1=461$. Logistic regression: $\bm{w}$ has 20 parameters and $b$ has 1, for a total of 21. A generative model estimates even “the shape of the distribution of the inputs,” which is not used directly for classification, so it has far more parameters. With little data, the estimate of the covariance matrix becomes unstable (if $N_k\lt D$, the sample covariance matrix is singular and has no inverse matrix).

(2) The generative model is easier to handle. If $p(\bm{x}\mid C_k)$ is a multivariate Gaussian distribution, the marginal distribution obtained by integrating out the missing components is also Gaussian, and it is obtained simply by extracting the submatrices of the mean and covariance for the observed components (Chapter 3, Section 3.6). Therefore the posterior probability $p(C_k\mid\bm{x}_{\text{observed}})$ can be computed from the observed descriptors alone. Logistic regression presupposes an $\bm{x}$ with all components present, so one must either impute the missing values (which itself requires assumptions) or discard that sample.

(3) Representing a variable that takes only 0 or 1 with a Gaussian distribution is an incorrect assumption about the shape of the distribution (it places density on values that are never taken, and treats the relationship between the two values as a continuous quantity). That error enters $\bm{w}$ through $\bm{\Sigma}$ and $\bm{\mu}_k$. Logistic regression assumes nothing about the distribution of the inputs and makes only the assumption that “the log-odds are a linear expression in the features,” so binary descriptors can be put into the features as they are (the coefficient can be read as “how much the log-odds increase when that element is contained”). Because it makes fewer assumptions, if there is enough data it tends to give a boundary with less bias.

← Back to Chapter 9, “Linear Classification Models — Decision Boundaries and Probabilities”

Chapter 10 Kernel Methods — Learning from Similarity

Exercise 10.1: Feature map of the nonhomogeneous polynomial kernel (computation)

Solution

(1) Let $t\equiv\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'=x_1x_1'+x_2x_2'$. Then $(t+c)^{2}=t^{2}+2ct+c^{2}$. We expand each term.

$$ \begin{aligned} t^{2}&=x_1^{2}x_1'^{2}+x_2^{2}x_2'^{2}\\ &\quad+(\sqrt2x_1x_2)(\sqrt2x_1'x_2')\\ 2ct&=(\sqrt{2c}\,x_1)(\sqrt{2c}\,x_1')\\ &\quad+(\sqrt{2c}\,x_2)(\sqrt{2c}\,x_2')\\ c^{2}&=c\cdot c \end{aligned} $$

Hence $(\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'+c)^{2}=\bm{\phi}(\bm{x})^{\tp}\bm{\phi}(\bm{x}')$, where

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{\phi}(\bm{x})=\bigl(&c,\ \sqrt{2c}\,x_1,\ \sqrt{2c}\,x_2,\\ &x_1^{2},\ x_2^{2},\ \sqrt2x_1x_2\bigr)^{\tp} \end{aligned} $$

(2) The contributions to the dot product are $c^{2}$ from the constant term, $2c\,t$ from the first-degree term, and $t^{2}$ from the second-degree term. As $c$ grows, the first-degree term (ratio $2c$) and the constant term (ratio $c^{2}$) carry more weight relative to the second-degree term. As $c\to0$ we return to the homogeneous kernel $(\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}')^{2}$.

(3) We have $t=1\times3+2\times(-1)=1$ and $c=2$, so $(1+2)^{2}=9$. Using $\sqrt{2c}=2$, the feature vectors are

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{\phi}(\bm{x})&=(2,\ 2,\ 4,\ 1,\ 4,\ 2\sqrt2)^{\tp}\\ \bm{\phi}(\bm{x}')&=(2,\ 6,\ -2,\ 9,\ 1,\ -3\sqrt2)^{\tp} \end{aligned} $$

Their dot product is $4+12-8+9+4-12=9$, which agrees.

(4) By $\binom{D+d}{d}$, for $D=5$ and $d=3$ we get $\binom{8}{3}=56$, and for $D=20$ and $d=3$ we get $\binom{23}{3}=1771$. Computing the kernel needs only a dot product ($D$ multiplications) and a cube.

Exercise 10.2: An identity linking the primal and dual problems (derivation)

Solution

(1) For any $\bm{v}\in\R^{M}$ with $\bm{v}\ne\bm{0}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{v}^{\tp}(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I}_M)\bm{v}&=\norm{\bm{\Phi}\bm{v}}^{2}+\lambda\norm{\bm{v}}^{2}\\ &\ge\lambda\norm{\bm{v}}^{2}\gt0 \end{aligned} $$

so the matrix is positive definite; hence all its eigenvalues are positive and it is invertible. Similarly, for $\bm{u}\in\R^{N}$ with $\bm{u}\ne\bm{0}$,

$$ \bm{u}^{\tp}(\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}+\lambda\bm{I}_N)\bm{u}=\norm{\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{u}}^{2}+\lambda\norm{\bm{u}}^{2}\gt0 $$

(2) Expanding the matrix product gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{\Phi}^{\tp}(\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}+\lambda\bm{I}_N)&=\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}+\lambda\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\\ &=(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I}_M)\bm{\Phi}^{\tp} \end{aligned} $$

Multiplying both sides by $(\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I}_M)^{-1}$ from the left and by $(\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}+\lambda\bm{I}_N)^{-1}$ from the right (both exist by (1)), we obtain

$$ (\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}+\lambda\bm{I}_M)^{-1}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}=\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}(\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}+\lambda\bm{I}_N)^{-1} $$

Multiplying by $\bm{y}$ from the right, the solution $\bm{w}$ of the primal problem coincides with $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{a}$, built from the solution of the dual problem.

(3) We compare the number of operations, counting multiplications and additions together. For the primal problem, factorizing the $M\times M=10^{4}\times10^{4}$ matrix takes about $M^{3}/3=3.3\times10^{11}$ operations; for the dual problem, factorizing the $50\times50$ matrix takes about $50^{3}/3=4.2\times10^{4}$ operations (counting multiplications alone, both are half of this). The cost of forming the matrices is likewise $NM^{2}=5\times10^{9}$ operations for $\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}\bm{\Phi}$ and $N^{2}M=2.5\times10^{7}$ operations for $\bm{K}=\bm{\Phi}\bm{\Phi}^{\tp}$; in both respects the dual problem is far cheaper.

Exercise 10.3: Building up kernels (derivation)

Solution

(1) Let $\bm{K}_1$ and $\bm{K}_2$ be the Gram matrices of the two kernels on the same set of points. The Gram matrix of the product kernel is the componentwise product $\bm{K}_1\odot\bm{K}_2$ (that is, $(\bm{K}_1\odot\bm{K}_2)_{nm}=(K_1)_{nm}(K_2)_{nm}$). Since $\bm{K}_2$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite, we can write $\bm{K}_2=\sum_i\mu_i\bm{u}_i\bm{u}_i^{\tp}$ with $\mu_i\ge0$ (Section A.3 of Appendix A). In components, $(K_2)_{nm}=\sum_i\mu_iu_{in}u_{im}$. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} &\bm{c}^{\tp}(\bm{K}_1\odot\bm{K}_2)\bm{c}\\ &=\sum_{n,m}c_nc_m(K_1)_{nm}\sum_i\mu_iu_{in}u_{im}\\ &=\sum_i\mu_i\sum_{n,m}(c_nu_{in})(K_1)_{nm}(c_mu_{im})\\ &=\sum_i\mu_i\,\bm{b}_i^{\tp}\bm{K}_1\bm{b}_i\ \ge\ 0 \end{aligned} $$

Here $\bm{b}_i=\bm{c}\odot\bm{u}_i$ (with components $c_nu_{in}$). Since $\mu_i\ge0$ and $\bm{b}_i^{\tp}\bm{K}_1\bm{b}_i\ge0$, each term is nonnegative. Symmetry is also preserved ($(K_1)_{nm}(K_2)_{nm}=(K_1)_{mn}(K_2)_{mn}$).

(2) Since $\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'/\ell^{2}$ is the dot product of the features $\bm{x}/\ell$, it is a positive definite kernel. The coefficients of $\exp(t)=\sum_{j\ge0}t^{j}/j!$ are all positive, so by (6) of Theorem 10.2 in the main text, $\exp(\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'/\ell^{2})$ is also a positive definite kernel.

(3) From $\norm{\bm{x}-\bm{x}'}^{2}=\norm{\bm{x}}^{2}-2\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'+\norm{\bm{x}'}^{2}$ we get

$$ \begin{aligned} &\sigma_f^{2}\exp\!\left(-\frac{\norm{\bm{x}-\bm{x}'}^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right)\\ &=\sigma_f^{2}\,g(\bm{x})\exp\!\left(\frac{\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}'}{\ell^{2}}\right)g(\bm{x}') \end{aligned} $$

where $g(\bm{x})=e^{-\norm{\bm{x}}^{2}/(2\ell^{2})}$. Applying (4) of Theorem 10.2 (weighting by a function) and (2) (multiplication by the positive constant $\sigma_f^{2}$) to the kernel in (2), we see that the RBF kernel is positive definite.

(4) From $e^{-1}=0.36788$, $e^{-3}=0.04979$, and $e^{-2}=0.13534$,

$$ \bm{K}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0.36788&0.04979\\0.36788&1&0.13534\\0.04979&0.13534&1\end{pmatrix} $$

The eigenvalues are $0.6218$, $0.9680$, and $1.4101$, all positive (their sum agrees with $\tr\bm{K}=3$). The Laplace kernel is also a positive definite kernel (the case $\nu=1/2$ of the Matérn family).

Exercise 10.4: Kernel ridge regression on three points (computation)

Solution

(1) Let $c_1=e^{-1/2}=0.60653$ (distance 1) and $c_2=e^{-2}=0.13534$ (distance 2). Then

$$ \bm{K}+0.5\bm{I}=\begin{pmatrix}1.5&c_1&c_2\\c_1&1.5&c_1\\c_2&c_1&1.5\end{pmatrix} $$

The data are symmetric under $x\to-x$ ($y(-1)=y(1)$), and this matrix is also unchanged when the first and third indices are swapped. Since the solution is unique, the swapped solution is the same solution, so $a_1=a_3$. Substituting $\bm{a}=(p,q,p)^{\tp}$, the first and third rows give the same equation, and we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} (1.5+c_2)\,p+c_1q&=1\\ 2c_1\,p+1.5\,q&=0 \end{aligned} $$

From the second equation, $q=-\dfrac{2c_1}{1.5}p=-0.80871\,p$. Substituting into the first,

$$ \begin{aligned} &(1.63534-0.60653\times0.80871)\,p\\ &=1.14483\,p=1 \end{aligned} $$

Hence $p=0.87349$ and $q=-0.70640$.

(2) The prediction is $f(x)=p\,k(-1,x)+q\,k(0,x)+p\,k(1,x)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} f(0)&=2c_1p+q\\ &=1.05961-0.70640=0.3532\\ f(1)&=c_2p+c_1q+p\\ &=0.11822-0.42845+0.87349\\ &=0.5633\\ f(3)&=e^{-8}p+e^{-4.5}q+e^{-2}p\\ &=0.00029-0.00785+0.11822\\ &=0.1107 \end{aligned} $$

By symmetry, $f(-1)$ is the same as $f(1)$.

(3) The value $\lambda=0.5$ is large compared with the diagonal entries 1 of the Gram matrix and shrinks the fine components strongly, so the predictions at the training points are pulled from the observed values (0 and 1) toward the mean ($0.353$ and $0.563$). The point $x=3$ is $2\ell$ away even from the nearest point $x=1$, and the kernel values are small, so the prediction approaches 0. Since we solved without subtracting the mean, the value it returns to is 0.

Exercise 10.5: Interpolating the band gap of a mixed crystal with kernel ridge regression (a materials topic)

Solution

(1) $E_g(0)=1.20$, $E_g(0.5)=0.60+1.20$ $-0.80\times0.25=1.60$, and $E_g(1)=2.40$ eV. The mean is $\bar{y}=(1.20+1.60+2.40)/3=1.7333$ eV. The target values with the mean subtracted are $(-0.5333,\ -0.1333,\ 0.6667)$ eV.

(2) The spacing 0.5 between points equals $\ell$, so the entries of $\bm{K}$ are $e^{-1/2}=0.60653$ (neighbors) and $e^{-2}=0.13534$ (the two ends), the same pattern as in Exercise 10.4. Solving $(\bm{K}+10^{-3}\bm{I})\bm{a}=\bm{y}-\bar{y}\bm{1}$ gives $\bm{a}=(-0.3257,\ -0.5785,\ 1.0605)$ eV.

At $x=0.25$, $\bm{k}_*=(e^{-0.125},\ e^{-0.125},\ e^{-1.125})^{\tp}$ $=(0.8825,\ 0.8825,\ 0.3247)^{\tp}$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} f(0.25)&=1.7333\\ &\quad+0.8825\times(-0.9042)\\ &\quad+0.3247\times1.0605\\ &=1.7333-0.7980+0.3443\\ &=1.280\ \mathrm{eV} \end{aligned} $$

The true value is $E_g(0.25)=0.90+0.60$ $-0.80\times0.1875=1.350$ eV. Similarly, at $x=0.75$ we get $f=2.053$ eV, while the true value is $E_g(0.75)=0.30+1.80-0.15$ $=1.950$ eV.

Because $\lambda$ is small, the fit passes almost exactly through the observed values at the training points ($f(0)=1.2003$, $f(1)=2.3989$).

(3) Linear interpolation gives $1.40$ eV at $x=0.25$ and $2.00$ eV at $x=0.75$, with an error of $0.05$ eV in both cases. The errors of kernel ridge regression are $-0.07$ eV and $+0.10$ eV, so in this example linear interpolation is closer. The true function is a downward-convex parabola (bowing), but the RBF kernel ($\ell=0.5$) makes the assumption that “between the points the function is a superposition of bumps that return toward the mean of 1.733 eV,” so three points alone cannot reproduce the shape of the parabola. A linear regression that includes the basis function $x(1-x)$, as in Chapter 8, can determine the true function exactly from three points. When a physically reasonable functional form is known, it is better to use it as the mean function or basis and let the kernel account only for the deviation from it.

(4) The prediction at $x=1.3$ is $2.447$ eV, a value on the way back toward the mean of 1.733 eV as we move away from the end point. In the first place, the composition $x$ is a quantity between 0 and 1, so $x=1.3$ has no physical meaning, and neither the extrapolated formula value of $3.07$ eV nor the model's prediction is a “value of a material.” The correct answer is: do not predict outside the domain.

Exercise 10.6: Similarity of atomic configurations and invariance (a materials topic, discussion)

Solution

(1) Under the 90° rotation $(x,y)\mapsto(-y,x)$, atom 1 moves to $(0,0)$, atom 2 to $(0,1.0)$, and atom 3 to $(-1.2,0)$. Swapping the labels of atoms 2 and 3 gives Q: atom 1 at $(0,0)$, atom 2 at $(-1.2,0)$, atom 3 at $(0,1.0)$.

(2) The vectors listing the coordinates are P $=(0,0,\ 1.0,0,\ 0,1.2)$ and Q $=(0,0,\ -1.2,0,\ 0,1.0)$. The sum of squared differences is $(1.0+1.2)^{2}+(1.2-1.0)^{2}$ $=4.84+0.04=4.88$ Å$^{2}$. The similarity is $\exp\bigl(-4.88/(2\times0.25)\bigr)$ $=e^{-9.76}=5.8\times10^{-5}$, so although the two arrangements are physically the same, they are judged to be “not similar at all.”

(3) The interatomic distances of P are $\abs{12}=1.0$, $\abs{13}=1.2$, and $\abs{23}=\sqrt{1.0^{2}+1.2^{2}}=1.562$ Å. For Q as well, the rotation does not change the distances and the relabeling only changes their order, so sorting them in increasing order gives the same $(1.0,\ 1.2,\ 1.562)$. The similarity is $e^{0}=1$.

(4) The distances of P′ are $(1.0,\ 1.3,\ \sqrt{1+1.69}=1.640)$. The sum of squared differences from P is $0+0.01+0.0061=0.0161$ Å$^{2}$, and the similarity is $\exp(-0.0161/0.5)=0.968$. If we move the atoms slightly, the similarity drops only slightly.

(5) Examples of weaknesses: ① when there are more atoms, the set of pairwise distances alone does not determine the arrangement uniquely (different arrangements can have the same set of distances), so different structures may be judged to be “the same.” ② Where the order of two distances is exchanged, the components of the sorted result change with a kink, so the derivative of the similarity (the quantity corresponding to the force) becomes discontinuous. ③ When the number of nearby atoms changes, the length of the descriptor changes, and descriptors cannot be compared as they are. SOAP is a method that integrates the overlap of atomic densities over rotations; it avoids these problems while maintaining invariance and smoothness (Section 10.10 of the main text).

Exercise 10.7: The Nadaraya–Watson limits and attention (computation, discussion)

Solution

(1) The distances from $x_*=1.5$ to the three points are 1.5, 0.5, and 0.5. As $\ell\to0$, the ratio of window values $e^{-(d_n^{2}-d_m^{2})/(2\ell^{2})}$ tends to 0 for every point other than the one with the shortest distance. The points at the shortest distance are the two points $x=1$ and $x=2$, which are equally distant, so the weights are $(0,\ \tfrac12,\ \tfrac12)$ and the prediction is $(3+2)/2=2.5$. As $\ell\to\infty$, all the window values tend to 1, the weights are $(\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13)$, and the prediction is the mean $(1+3+2)/3=2.0$.

(2) For $\ell=1$ and $x_*=4$, the exponents are $-(4-0)^{2}/2=-8$, $-(4-1)^{2}/2=-4.5$, and $-(4-2)^{2}/2=-2$. To avoid overflow, we subtract the maximum $-2$ before exponentiating, which gives $(e^{-6},\ e^{-2.5},\ 1)=(0.00248,\ 0.08208,\ 1)$, with sum $1.08456$. The weights are $(0.0023,\ 0.0757,\ 0.9220)$, and the prediction is $0.0023\times1+0.0757\times3$ $+0.9220\times2=2.073$. Outside the data, the prediction approaches the value at the nearest point ($x=2$, value 2).

(3) With $s_n=x_*x_n-x_n^{2}/2$ ($\ell=1$), we get $(0,\ 3.5,\ 6)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} -\frac{(x_*-x_n)^{2}}{2}&=-\frac{x_*^{2}}{2}+x_*x_n-\frac{x_n^{2}}{2}\\ &=-8+s_n \end{aligned} $$

so the two differ only by the constant $-8$, which is common to all $n$. The softmax does not change when the same constant is added to all the scores, so the weights coincide. Indeed, subtracting the maximum 6 from $s_n$ gives $(-6,\ -2.5,\ 0)$, the same numbers as in (2).

(4) The scale $1/\ell^{2}$ of the score corresponds to $1/\sqrt{d_k}$ in attention. Increasing the scale corresponds to decreasing $\ell$, and the weights concentrate on the key with the highest score (the limit $\ell\to0$ selects only the nearest point). Decreasing the scale makes the weights approach uniform, and the prediction approaches the simple average of the values.

Exercise 10.8: A periodic kernel for crystal orientation (a materials topic, derivation)

Solution

(1) Let $\Delta=\theta-\theta'$ and $\omega=2\pi/p$. Since $\bm{u}$ is a point on the unit circle, $\norm{\bm{u}}=\norm{\bm{u}'}=1$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} &\norm{\bm{u}-\bm{u}'}^{2}\\ &=2-2\bigl(\cos\omega\theta\cos\omega\theta'+\sin\omega\theta\sin\omega\theta'\bigr)\\ &=2-2\cos(\omega\Delta)=4\sin^{2}(\omega\Delta/2) \end{aligned} $$

(the last step uses the half-angle formula $1-\cos t=2\sin^{2}(t/2)$). Since $\omega\Delta/2=\pi\Delta/p$, the RBF kernel in the space of $\bm{u}$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} \exp\!\left(-\frac{\norm{\bm{u}-\bm{u}'}^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right)&\\ =\exp\!\left(-\frac{2\sin^{2}(\pi\Delta/p)}{\ell^{2}}\right)& \end{aligned} $$

The RBF kernel is positive definite, and positive definiteness is preserved when the input is transformed by a map $\bm{u}(\theta)$ ((5) of Theorem 10.2 in the main text), so this kernel is positive definite.

(2) For $\ell=1$ and $p=90°$:

  • $\Delta=15°$: $\sin^{2}(30°)=0.25$, $k=e^{-0.5}=0.607$
  • $\Delta=45°$: $\sin^{2}(90°)=1$, $k=e^{-2}=0.135$
  • $\Delta=90°$: $\sin^{2}(180°)=0$, $k=1$

(3) If we use the RBF kernel ($\ell=20°$) directly on $\theta$, then $k=\exp(-\Delta^{2}/800)$, which is $0.755$ for $\Delta=15°$, $0.080$ for $45°$, and $4.0\times10^{-5}$ for $90°$. The directions 0° and 90°, which should take the same value by symmetry, are regarded as “unrelated.” The periodic kernel treats 0° and 90° as the same point, so, for example, measurements over 0–45° can also predict the values over 45–90°, and symmetry compensates for the small amount of data. Conversely, if the sample orientation has errors or the crystal symmetry is lower than assumed (twinning, strain caused by stress, and so on), the assumed period does not hold and the predictions are wrong. Building symmetry into the kernel also means making an assumption about the symmetry.

← Back to Chapter 10, “Kernel Methods — Learning from Similarity”

Chapter 11 Support Vector Machines — Maximizing the Margin

Exercise 11.1: Checking whether a given line is the maximum-margin line

Solution

(1) The line $x_1+x_2-2=0$ has $\bm{w}=(1,1)^{\tp}$, $b=-2$, and $\norm{\bm{w}}=\sqrt2$. At each point, $y_nf(\bm{x}_n)$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{x}_1&:\ (+1)(2+2-2)=2,\\ \bm{x}_2&:\ (+1)(4+3-2)=5,\\ \bm{x}_3&:\ (-1)(0+0-2)=2,\\ \bm{x}_4&:\ (-1)(1-2-2)=3 . \end{aligned} $$

The signed distances $r_n=y_nf/\norm{\bm{w}}$ are $2/\sqrt2=1.414$, $5/\sqrt2=3.536$, $1.414$, and $3/\sqrt2=2.121$, all positive (every point is on the correct side). The margin is the smallest of these, $\sqrt2\approx1.414$. Since $\min_ny_nf=2$, multiplying $(\bm{w},b)$ by $1/2$ gives the canonical form $\bm{w}=(0.5,\ 0.5)^{\tp}$, $b=-1$ (the boundary is the same line). Then $y_nf=1,\ 2.5,\ 1,\ 1.5$.

(2) The points on the margin ($y_nf=1$) are $\bm{x}_1$ and $\bm{x}_3$, so we assume $\mathcal{S}=\{1,3\}$ and $\alpha_2=\alpha_4=0$. The stationarity condition $\bm{w}=\sum_n\alpha_ny_n\bm{x}_n$ reads

$$ \begin{aligned} (0.5,\ 0.5)^{\tp}&=\alpha_1(+1)(2,2)^{\tp}+\alpha_3(-1)(0,0)^{\tp}\\ &=(2\alpha_1,\ 2\alpha_1)^{\tp} \end{aligned} $$

so $\alpha_1=\tfrac14$. From $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=\alpha_1-\alpha_3=0$ we get $\alpha_3=\tfrac14$. We now check the KKT conditions. Stationarity: satisfied above. Primal feasibility: $y_nf=1,\ 2.5,\ 1,\ 1.5\ge1$. Dual feasibility: $\bm{\alpha}=(\tfrac14,\ 0,\ \tfrac14,\ 0)\ge\bm{0}$. Complementarity: $\alpha_2=\alpha_4=0$, and $y_nf-1=0$ at $\bm{x}_1,\bm{x}_3$. Since the primal problem is convex, this point, which satisfies the KKT conditions, is the optimal solution. That is, the given line is the maximum-margin line (geometrically, it is the perpendicular bisector of the segment joining $\bm{x}_1$ and $\bm{x}_3$).

(3) The width is $2/\norm{\bm{w}}=2/\sqrt{0.5}=2\sqrt2\approx2.83$ (equal to the distance $\sqrt{8}$ between $\bm{x}_1$ and $\bm{x}_3$). The dual value $D=\sum_n\alpha_n-\tfrac12\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}=\tfrac12-\tfrac14=\tfrac14$ equals the primal value $\tfrac12\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}=\tfrac14$.

Exercise 11.2: Changing the penalty for each class

Solution

(1) Write the penalty of point $n$ as $C_n\equiv C_{y_n}$ (that is, $C_{+}$ if $y_n=+1$ and $C_{-}$ if $-1$). The objective function is then $\tfrac12\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}+\sum_nC_n\xi_n$. Introducing $\alpha_n\ge0$ for the constraint $1-\xi_n-y_nf(\bm{x}_n)\le0$ and $\mu_n\ge0$ for $-\xi_n\le0$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} \Lambda={}&\frac12\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}+\sum_nC_n\xi_n-\sum_n\mu_n\xi_n\\ &-\sum_n\alpha_n\bigl\{y_n(\bm{w}^{\tp}\bm{\phi}_n+b)-1+\xi_n\bigr\}. \end{aligned} $$

The stationarity conditions for $\bm{w}$ and $b$ are the same as Eqs. (11.6) and (11.7) of Chapter 11: $\bm{w}=\sum_n\alpha_ny_n\bm{\phi}_n$ and $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=0$. The stationarity condition for $\xi_n$ is $\partial\Lambda/\partial\xi_n=C_n-\alpha_n-\mu_n=0$, that is, $\mu_n=C_n-\alpha_n$. The terms containing $\xi_n$ vanish because $\sum_n(C_n-\alpha_n-\mu_n)\xi_n=0$, and the rest is the same calculation as in the hard-margin case, giving

$$ D(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_n\alpha_n-\frac12\sum_{n,m}\alpha_n\alpha_my_ny_mk(\bm{x}_n,\bm{x}_m) $$

From $\mu_n\ge0$ we obtain $\alpha_n\le C_n$. Therefore the constraints are $0\le\alpha_n\le C_{y_n}$ and $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=0$.

(2) Take $C_{+}\gt C_{-}$. If we impose a large penalty on the slack of points with $y=+1$ (defective items), the boundary is less willing to place a defective item inside the margin or on the wrong side, so misses (judging a defective item to be good) decrease. In return, false alarms, in which a good item is judged defective, may increase. A class that makes up only 2 % contributes almost nothing to the total penalty when the same $C$ is used, so the boundary tends to fit the majority class. A commonly used rule of thumb is to make the ratio of the penalties the inverse ratio of the class sizes ($C_{+}/C_{-}\approx98/2$), but in the end we decide by looking at the costs of misses and false alarms and at the recall and precision on the validation data (Chapter 9, Section 9.7).

Exercise 11.3: A one-dimensional SVM and adding a point

Solution

(1) The closest points of different classes are $x=-2$ ($-1$) and $x=0$ ($+1$), and the boundary is their midpoint $x=-1$. Let $f(x)=w(x+1)$. The canonical-form condition $f(0)=1$ gives $w=1$ and $b=1$. For the other points, $y_nf=-(-3+1)=2$ and $2+1=3$, both at least 1. Taking $\mathcal{S}=\{-2,0\}$, the stationarity condition $w=\sum_n\alpha_ny_nx_n$ reads

$$ 1=\alpha_{-2}(-1)(-2)+\alpha_{0}(+1)(0)=2\alpha_{-2} $$

so $\alpha_{-2}=\tfrac12$. From $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=-\alpha_{-2}+\alpha_0=0$ we get $\alpha_0=\tfrac12$. Thus $\bm{\alpha}=(0,\ \tfrac12,\ \tfrac12,\ 0)$ (the points in the order $-3,-2,0,2$). Since the point $x=0$ has $x_n=0$, we have $\alpha_ny_nx_n=0$, so it does not contribute to $w$; however, to satisfy the equality constraint $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=0$ (the stationarity condition for $b$), $\alpha_0=\alpha_{-2}$ is required. It is this point that determines $b$. The width is $2/w=2$.

(2) For the new point $x=-1.2$ ($y=-1$), $y f=-(-1.2+1)=0.2\lt1$, which violates the constraint, so the solution changes. The new closest pair is $x=-1.2$ and $x=0$, the boundary is their midpoint $x=-0.6$, and the width is $1.2$. Imposing $f(0)=1$ on $f(x)=w(x+0.6)$ gives $w=1/0.6=5/3$ and $b=5/3\times0.6=1$. Check: $f(-1.2)=\tfrac53(-0.6)=-1$, $f(-2)=\tfrac53(-1.4)=-2.33$, $f(-3)=-4$, and $f(2)=\tfrac53(2.6)=4.33$, so every point has $y_nf\ge1$. The stationarity condition $\tfrac53=\alpha_{-1.2}(-1)(-1.2)+\alpha_0\cdot0=1.2\,\alpha_{-1.2}$ gives $\alpha_{-1.2}=\tfrac{25}{18}\approx1.389$, $\alpha_0=\tfrac{25}{18}$, and all the others are 0. The point $x=-2$, which was a support vector originally, now has $\alpha=0$.

Exercise 11.4: A two-point SVM with an RBF kernel and γ

Solution

(1) We have $k_{11}=k_{22}=1$ and $k_{12}=q=e^{-\gamma d^{2}}$. From $\sum_n\alpha_ny_n=0$, $\alpha_1=\alpha_2=\alpha$.

$$ \begin{aligned} D(\alpha)&=2\alpha-\frac12\alpha^{2}(1+1-2q)\\ &=2\alpha-\alpha^{2}(1-q). \end{aligned} $$

From $\mathrm{d}D/\mathrm{d}\alpha=2-2\alpha(1-q)=0$ we get $\alpha=1/(1-q)$. Then $b=y_2-\alpha y_1k_{12}-\alpha y_2k_{22}$ $=1+\alpha q-\alpha$ $=1-\alpha(1-q)=0$. Since $\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}=\alpha^{2}(2-2q)=2\alpha$, the width is $2/\norm{\bm{w}}=\sqrt{2/\alpha}=\sqrt{2(1-q)}$.

(2) For $d=1$: with $\gamma=0.05$, $q=e^{-0.05}=0.9512$, $\alpha=1/0.04877=20.50$, and the width is $\sqrt{2\times0.04877}=0.312$. With $\gamma=1$, $q=0.3679$, $\alpha=1.582$, and the width is $1.124$. With $\gamma=20$, $q=e^{-20}=2.06\times10^{-9}$, $\alpha=1.000$, and the width is $\sqrt2=1.414$.

(3) As $\gamma\to\infty$, $q\to0$, the feature vectors of the two points become orthogonal (dissimilar), $\alpha\to1$, and the width $\to\sqrt2$. The score equals $\pm1$ only very close to each point and returns to 0 a little away from the two points (the limit of surrounding each point with a narrow peak). As $\gamma\to0$, $q\to1$ and the feature vectors of the two points become almost identical, the width $\sqrt{2(1-q)}\approx d\sqrt{2\gamma}\to0$, and $\alpha\approx1/(\gamma d^{2})\to\infty$. Because two points that cannot be distinguished in the feature space are forcibly separated, $\alpha$ diverges. With a soft margin, the multiplier is capped by $\alpha\le C$, and both points have $\alpha=C$ and lie inside the margin.

Exercise 11.5: Reading the glass-formation boundary in the original units

Solution

(1) Setting $f=0$ at $\delta=8$: $1.087\,u_1=5.718-0.2691\times8$ $=5.718-2.153=3.565$, so $u_1=3.565/1.087=3.280$. Then $R=10^{3.280}\approx1.9\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{K\,s^{-1}}$. If the material is cooled faster than this, the model judges that it “forms a glass.”

(2) The values $f=\pm1$ correspond to positions shifted by $\pm1/1.087=\pm0.920$ in $u_1$, so the margin band is about $2\times0.92=1.84$ decades in $\log_{10}R$ (at $\delta=8\ \%$, $R\approx2.3\times10^{2}$–$1.6\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{K\,s^{-1}}$). For cooling rates in this range the judgment is uncertain.

(3) On the boundary, $1.087\,\Delta u_1+0.2691\,\Delta u_2=0$. If $\Delta u_2=1$, then $\Delta u_1=-0.2691/1.087=-0.248$. When the atomic-radius difference is larger by 1 %, the cooling rate on the boundary falls by about 0.25 decades (to about 1/1.8). For the rule that generated the data, $(u_1-3.5)+0.30(u_2-9.0)=0$, the value is 0.30 decades, so as an estimate from 30 points it is close but not identical.

(4) It must not be used. These data are synthetic data for teaching purposes, not measured values of real alloys. The coefficients merely reflect the rule and the noise that we built in under the assumption that “ease of glass formation is determined by a linear function of two descriptors.” In real systems, other factors such as the heat of mixing, the number of components, and the liquidus temperature matter, and the boundary is not necessarily a straight line. Moreover, there is no guarantee for extrapolation outside the range of the data ($\log_{10}R\in[1,6]$, $\delta\in[2,16]\ \%$). We take away only the way of thinking (make samples near the boundary, and read the coefficients in the original units).

Exercise 11.6: Classifying phases by the tolerance factor

Solution

(1) The closest points of different classes are $t=0.78$ ($-1$) and $t=0.86$ ($+1$). The threshold is their midpoint $t=0.82$. Imposing the canonical-form condition $f(0.86)=1$ on $f(t)=w(t-0.82)$ gives $w=1/0.04=25$ and $b=-25\times0.82=-20.5$. The width is $2/w=0.08$ (the distance between the two points). For the other points, $y_nf$ is $-25(0.72-0.82)=2.5$ at $t=0.72$, $2.25$ at $0.91$, $3.25$ at $0.95$, and $4.25$ at $0.99$, all at least 1. From the stationarity condition $w=\alpha_{0.78}(-1)(0.78)+\alpha_{0.86}(+1)(0.86)$ and $\alpha_{0.78}=\alpha_{0.86}\equiv\alpha$ (the equality constraint), we get $25=\alpha(0.86-0.78)=0.08\alpha$, so $\alpha=312.5$. The other points have $\alpha=0$. The reason $\alpha$ is large is that the range of $t$ is narrow and $\norm{\bm{w}}$ is large; if we standardize the input, $\alpha$ becomes a value of about 1.

(2) If we add a sample at $t=1.06$ that “did not form,” the $+1$ samples ($0.86\le t\le0.99$) are sandwiched on both sides by $-1$ samples. A one-dimensional linear discriminant function $f=wt+b$ is monotone, so the region $f\gt0$ is a half-line $t\gt-b/w$ (or $t\lt-b/w$), and it cannot make the $-1$ samples on both sides negative at the same time. Therefore they cannot be separated by a linear boundary. Since “only the inside of the right range is $+1$,” it suffices to add $t^{2}$ to the features, $\bm{\phi}(t)=(t,\ t^{2})$ ($f=w_2t^{2}+w_1t+b$ is an upward-convex parabola for $w_2\lt0$, positive between the two thresholds), that is, to use the quadratic polynomial kernel $(tt'+1)^{2}$ or the RBF kernel. The stability of an actual phase is not determined by the tolerance factor alone, so we should also consider increasing the number of descriptors.

Exercise 11.7: Which added sample moves the boundary?

Solution

The original solution is $\bm{w}=(1,1)^{\tp}$, $b=-1$, and $\bm{\alpha}=(1,\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$.

(1) At $(3,3)$ ($y=+1$), $yf=3+3-1=5\ge1$. If we assign $\alpha=0$ to the new point, the sum in the stationarity condition does not change, the constraint of the new point is satisfied, and complementarity also holds, since $\alpha\cdot(5-1)=0$. All the KKT conditions are satisfied, so the solution does not change.

(2) At $(0.4,0.4)$ ($y=+1$), $yf=0.8-1=-0.2\lt1$ (misclassified), which violates the constraint, so the solution changes. The closest points of different classes are $(0,0)$ and $(0.4,0.4)$ (distance $0.4\sqrt2=0.566$), so we can expect the boundary to be their perpendicular bisector $x_1+x_2=0.4$. Let $\bm{w}=c(1,1)^{\tp}$. From $f(0,0)=b=-1$ and $f(0.4,0.4)=0.8c-1=1$, we get $c=2.5$. Thus $\bm{w}=(2.5,\ 2.5)^{\tp}$ and $b=-1$. For the other points, $f(2,0)=f(0,2)=5-1=4\ge1$. The stationarity condition $\bm{w}=\alpha_4(0.4,0.4)^{\tp}$ gives $\alpha_4=6.25$, and the equality constraint gives $\alpha_1=6.25$ and $\alpha_2=\alpha_3=0$. The width shrinks to $2/\norm{\bm{w}}=2/(2.5\sqrt2)=0.566$. The original support vectors $(2,0)$ and $(0,2)$ no longer take part in determining the boundary.

(3) Adding a sample under conditions where it is correctly classified outside the margin (conditions where $yf\ge1$ is expected) does not move the boundary and carries little information. What can move the boundary is a sample with $yf\lt1$, that is, a condition inside the margin. The actual label is not known until we make the sample, so it is efficient to make the next sample at a condition where the predicted score $\abs{f}$ is smaller than 1 (the point P of Example 11.9). However, as in (2), a single point can move the boundary a great deal, so we check that the label of that point is not a mistake and, if necessary, suppress the influence of outliers with a soft margin (finite $C$).

Exercise 11.8: Platt scaling

Solution

(1) $p=0.5$ occurs when $\exp(Af+B)=1$, that is, $Af+B=0$. Then $f=-B/A=-(-0.22)/(-2.18)=-0.101$. The score boundary $f=0$ and the point with probability 0.5 do not coincide (at $f=0$, $p=0.555$).

(2) For $f=-0.5$: $Af+B=1.09-0.22=0.87$ and $p=1/(1+e^{0.87})=1/(1+2.387)=0.295$. For $f=2$: $Af+B=-4.36-0.22=-4.58$ and $p=1/(1+e^{-4.58})$ $=1/(1+0.0103)=0.990$.

(3) Differentiating $p(f)=1/\{1+\exp(Af+B)\}$ gives

$$ \frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}f}=\frac{-A\exp(Af+B)}{\{1+\exp(Af+B)\}^{2}} $$

which is always positive if $A\lt0$. Since $p$ is a strictly increasing function of $f$, the ranking by score and the ranking by probability are the same. Even so, converting to probabilities is meaningful because (i) the threshold can be chosen from costs (for example, if the cost of a miss is 9 times that of a false alarm, judge “defective” when $p\gt0.1$), (ii) the probabilities can be combined with those of other models and with prior probabilities, and (iii) the result can be reported as a meaningful number, such as “$+1$ with probability 80 %.”

(4) The training points are points that the SVM learned to “push out of the margin,” and their scores come out systematically larger (overconfident) than the scores of new data. In particular, points with $\alpha=0$ gather at $\abs{f}\ge1$. If $A,B$ are determined from the scores of the training data, $\abs{A}$ becomes large and the probabilities are pushed too close to 0 and 1 (in the example of Chapter 11, $A=-3.08$ and $B=0.80$, determined from the 60 training points, estimated the probability at $f=-1$ as 0.020, far smaller than the 0.123 determined from the calibration data). For calibration we use data not used in training (validation data, or the scores of the held-out points of each cross-validation split).

Chapter 12 Gaussian Process Regression — Placing a Distribution on Functions

Exercise 12.1: Writing the prediction from a single observation in general

Solution

(1) Since there is one observation, $\bm{K}_y=\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}$ (a $1\times1$ matrix), $\bm{k}_*=k(x_1,x_*)=\sigma_f^{2}e^{-r^{2}/(2\ell^{2})}$, and $k_{**}=\sigma_f^{2}$. Substituting into the mean and variance expressions of Formula 12.1 in Chapter 12 gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \mu_*(x_*)&=m+\frac{\sigma_f^{2}e^{-r^{2}/(2\ell^{2})}}{\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}}\,(y_1-m),\\ \sigma_*^{2}(x_*)&=\sigma_f^{2}-\frac{\sigma_f^{4}e^{-r^{2}/\ell^{2}}}{\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}}. \end{aligned} $$

The exponent in the variance expression is $-r^{2}/\ell^{2}$ because the exponent doubles in $k(x_1,x_*)^{2}$.

(2) We have $y_1-m=-3.40+4.47=1.07$, $\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}=0.64+0.0025=0.6425$, $\sigma_f^{2}/(\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2})=0.99611$, and $\sigma_f^{4}/(\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2})=0.4096/0.6425=0.63751$.

  • $x_*=7.0$ ($r=0$): $\mu_*=-4.47+0.99611\times1.07$ $=-4.47+1.06584$ $=-3.4042$. $\sigma_*^{2}=0.64-0.63751=0.00249$, $\sigma_*=0.0499$.
  • $x_*=8.0$ ($r=1$): from $e^{-1/2}=0.60653$, $\mu_*=-4.47+0.99611\times0.60653\times1.07$ $=-4.47+0.64646$ $=-3.8235$. From $e^{-1}=0.36788$, $\sigma_*^{2}=0.64-0.63751\times0.36788=0.40547$, $\sigma_*=0.6368$.
  • $x_*=9.0$ ($r=2$): from $e^{-2}=0.13534$, $\mu_*=-4.47+0.99611\times0.13534\times1.07$ $=-4.3258$. From $e^{-4}=0.018316$, $\sigma_*^{2}=0.64-0.63751\times0.018316=0.62832$, $\sigma_*=0.7927$.

(3) As $\sigma\to0$, at $x_*=x_1$ we get $\mu_*=m+(y_1-m)=y_1$ and $\sigma_*^{2}=\sigma_f^{2}-\sigma_f^{2}=0$, so the predictive mean passes exactly through the observation point (interpolation). If $\sigma\gt0$, then even at the observation point $\mu_*$ is shrunk from $y_1$ toward $m$ by the fraction $\sigma^{2}/(\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2})$. As $r\to\infty$ the exponential factor goes to 0, so $\mu_*\to m$ and $\sigma_*^{2}\to\sigma_f^{2}$: the prediction returns to the prior distribution.

Exercise 12.2: The linear kernel and Bayesian linear regression

Solution

(1) With $f(x)=wx$, $\Ex[w]=0$, and $\Ex[w^{2}]=\sigma_w^{2}$, we have $k(x,x')=\Cov[wx,wx']=xx'\,\Ex[w^{2}]=\sigma_w^{2}xx'$.

(2) The Gram matrix is $\bm{K}=\sigma_w^{2}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\tp}$, with $\bm{K}_y=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}+\sigma_w^{2}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\tp}$ and $\bm{k}_*=\sigma_w^{2}x_*\bm{x}$. Let $S\equiv\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}=\sum_nx_n^{2}$. Apply the Sherman–Morrison formula with $\bm{A}=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}$ and $\bm{u}=\sigma_w\bm{x}$. Then $\bm{A}^{-1}=\bm{I}/\sigma^{2}$ and $\bm{u}^{\tp}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{u}=\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}$, so

$$ \bm{K}_y^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{I}-\frac{\sigma_w^{2}/\sigma^{4}}{1+\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}}\,\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\tp}. $$

Substituting this into $\mu_*=\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{y}$ and using $\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{x}=S$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \mu_*&=\sigma_w^{2}x_*\left[\frac{\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{y}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_w^{2}/\sigma^{4}}{1+\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}}\,S\,\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{y}\right]\\ &=\frac{\sigma_w^{2}x_*\,\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{y}}{\sigma^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}}\right]\\ &=\frac{\sigma_w^{2}x_*\,\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{y}}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\sigma_w^{2}S/\sigma^{2}}\\ &=\frac{\sigma_w^{2}x_*\,\bm{x}^{\tp}\bm{y}}{\sigma^{2}+\sigma_w^{2}S}=x_*\,\frac{\sum_nx_ny_n}{\sum_nx_n^{2}+\sigma^{2}/\sigma_w^{2}}. \end{aligned} $$

In the last step we divided the numerator and the denominator by $\sigma_w^{2}$.

(3) Differentiate the one-weight ridge regression $L(w)=\tfrac12\sum_n(y_n-wx_n)^{2}+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}w^{2}$ with respect to $w$ and set the result to 0: $-\sum_nx_n(y_n-wx_n)+\lambda w=0$, hence $w=\dfrac{\sum_nx_ny_n}{\sum_nx_n^{2}+\lambda}$. If we take $\lambda=\sigma^{2}/\sigma_w^{2}$, then $\mu_*$ in (2) is this $w$ multiplied by $x_*$. The same calculation gives the variance $\sigma_*^{2}=x_*^{2}\,\sigma^{2}/(S+\sigma^{2}/\sigma_w^{2})$, which agrees with the posterior variance of the weight in the Bayesian linear regression of Chapter 8, $(S/\sigma^{2}+1/\sigma_w^{2})^{-1}$, multiplied by $x_*^{2}$. This is the simplest example of the fact that computations in weight space and in function space give the same prediction.

Exercise 12.3: Adding an observation does not increase the predictive variance

Solution

(1) Upper bound: since $\bm{K}_y$ is positive definite, $\bm{K}_y^{-1}$ is also positive definite (its eigenvalues are the reciprocals of positive numbers), so $\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*\ge0$ for any vector $\bm{k}_*$. Hence $\sigma_*^{2}=k_{**}-\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*\le k_{**}$.

Lower bound: compute the variance of the random variable $e=f_*-\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{y}$. Using $\Var[f_*]=k_{**}$, $\Cov[\bm{y},f_*]=\bm{k}_*$, and $\Cov[\bm{y}]=\bm{K}_y$, and letting $\bm{b}=\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[e]&=\Var[f_*]-2\bm{b}^{\tp}\Cov[\bm{y},f_*]\\ &\quad+\bm{b}^{\tp}\Cov[\bm{y}]\bm{b}\\ &=k_{**}-2\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*\\ &\quad+\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{K}_y\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*\\ &=k_{**}-\bm{k}_*^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{k}_*=\sigma_*^{2}. \end{aligned} $$

A variance is at least 0, so $\sigma_*^{2}\ge0$.

(2) Conditioning on $N+1$ points is the same as first conditioning on $N$ points and then conditioning on $y_{N+1}$ with the resulting posterior distribution as the new prior distribution (sequential updating with Bayes's theorem; Chapter 4, Section 4.4). In the posterior distribution after $N$ points, $(f_*,f_{N+1})$ follows a Gaussian distribution with variances $\sigma_{*,N}^{2}$ and $s^{2}$ and covariance $c$. Since the noise $\varepsilon_{N+1}$ in $y_{N+1}=f_{N+1}+\varepsilon_{N+1}$ is independent of the earlier data and of $f$, the covariance matrix of $(f_*,y_{N+1})$ after $N$ points is

$$ \begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{*,N}^{2}&c\\ c&s^{2}+\sigma^{2}\end{pmatrix} $$

Applying the formula for the conditional distribution in Chapter 3 (the scalar case) gives

$$ \sigma_{*,N+1}^{2}=\sigma_{*,N}^{2}-\frac{c\cdot c}{s^{2}+\sigma^{2}}=\sigma_{*,N}^{2}-\frac{c^{2}}{s^{2}+\sigma^{2}}. $$

(3) The subtracted quantity $c^{2}/(s^{2}+\sigma^{2})$ is at least 0, so adding an observation does not increase the predictive variance. The reduction is larger the larger the posterior covariance $c$ between the value at the new point and the value we want to predict, and if $c=0$ (a distant point, or a point already well known from the $N$ points), the variance does not decrease. Moreover, the observed value $y_{N+1}$ does not appear in this expression, so once we decide where to measure, we can know how much the variance will decrease before measuring. This is the basis, in the next chapter, for asking “where should we measure to reduce the uncertainty the most?”

Exercise 12.4: Computing the prediction and the marginal likelihood with the Cholesky decomposition

Solution

Since $k(0,2)=e^{-2}=0.13534$, we have $\bm{K}_y=\begin{pmatrix}1.01&0.13534\\0.13534&1.01\end{pmatrix}$.

(1) $L_{11}=\sqrt{1.01}=1.00499$, $L_{21}=0.13534/1.00499=0.13466$, $L_{22}=\sqrt{1.01-0.13466^{2}}$ $=\sqrt{1.01-0.01813}$ $=0.99592$.

(2) Forward substitution $\bm{L}\bm{u}=\bm{y}$:

$$ \begin{aligned} u_1&=1.0/1.00499=0.99504,\\ u_2&=\frac{0.2-0.13466\times0.99504}{0.99592}\\ &=0.06627. \end{aligned} $$

Back substitution $\bm{L}^{\tp}\bm{a}=\bm{u}$:

$$ \begin{aligned} a_2&=0.06627/0.99592=0.06655,\\ a_1&=\frac{0.99504-0.13466\times0.06655}{1.00499}\\ &=0.98118. \end{aligned} $$

(3) $\bm{k}_*=(e^{-1/2},e^{-1/2})^{\tp}=(0.60653,\,0.60653)^{\tp}$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mu_*&=0.60653\times(0.98118+0.06655)\\ &=0.6355,\\ v_1&=0.60653/1.00499=0.60352,\\ v_2&=\frac{0.60653-0.13466\times0.60352}{0.99592}\\ &=0.52741,\\ \bm{v}^{\tp}\bm{v}&=0.36424+0.27816=0.64240,\\ \sigma_*^{2}&=1-0.64240=0.35760,\\ \sigma_*&=0.5980. \end{aligned} $$

Even exactly midway between the two observation points, the distance to each observation point is 1 ($=\ell$), so $\sigma_*$ decreases only from the prior value 1 to about 0.6.

(4) The data-fit term is $-\tfrac12\bm{y}^{\tp}\bm{a}=-\tfrac12\bm{u}^{\tp}\bm{u}$ $=-\tfrac12(0.99010+0.00439)$ $=-0.49725$. The complexity-penalty term is $-\sum_i\ln L_{ii}$ $=-(\ln1.00499+\ln0.99592)$ $=-(0.00498-0.00409)$ $=-0.00089$. The constant term is $-\tfrac22\ln2\pi=-1.83788$. Adding them up, $\ln p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{\theta})=-2.3360$.

Exercise 12.5: How to report a prediction of ionic conductivity

Solution

(1) From Table 12.3 of Chapter 12, at $x_*=7.0$ we have $\mu_*=-3.5124$ and $\sqrt{\sigma_*^{2}+\sigma^{2}}=0.2940$. The range into which the next observed value falls with about 95% probability is

$$ -3.5124\pm2\times0.2940=[-4.100,\ -2.925]. $$

The band for the latent function is $-3.5124\pm2\times0.2897=[-4.092,\ -2.933]$, and the difference between the two bands is only 0.008 at each end. This is because, away from the observation points, $\sigma_*=0.29$ is much larger than $\sigma=0.05$, so almost all of the uncertainty comes from lack of knowledge.

(2) Examples of information to add: ① The uncertainty. State that $\sigma_*=0.29$ (in $\log_{10}$), that the approximately 95% band of the latent function is $10^{-4.09}$ to $10^{-2.93}\ \mathrm{S\,cm^{-1}}$, which corresponds to 0.26 to 3.8 times the central value in terms of the conductivity ratio, and whether it is the band of the latent function or the band of the observed value. ② That 7.0 mol% itself was not measured, and that the prediction interpolates between the observations at 6.4 mol% and 8.2 mol%. ③ The dependence on the hyperparameters. With $\ell=1$ mol% the result is $-3.51\pm0.29$, but with $\ell=1.585$ mol%, which maximizes the marginal likelihood, it is $-3.47\pm0.08$, so both the predicted value and the width of the band change (Table 12.1). It is also good to state which kernel (RBF), noise assumption, and mean function were used, and that there are only 8 data points.

(3) It is better to measure 7.0 mol%. The predictive variance is determined only by the arrangement of the inputs, not by the values of the data (Section 12.4.4), so we can compute the effect before measuring. If a ninth point is added, with $\ell=1$, $\sigma_f=0.8$, and $\sigma=0.05$, then at $x_*=7.0$ the value of $\sigma_*$ decreases only from 0.290 to 0.288 when the point is added at 6.4 mol%, but it decreases to 0.049 when the point is added at 7.0 mol%. Since 6.4 mol% has already been measured ($\sigma_*=0.050$), measuring it once more reduces $\sigma_*$ only to about 0.035, and we learn almost nothing about the height near the top of the peak (near 7.0 mol%). In terms of the formula in Exercise 12.3 (2), this is because the posterior covariance $c$ between the new observed value at 6.4 mol% and $f(7.0)$ is small.

Exercise 12.6: Two inputs, firing temperature and dopant amount

Solution

(1) If we keep the inputs as $(T,x)$ and use $\ell=1$, the correlation coefficient between two points with $\Delta T=50$ (°C) and $\Delta x=0$ is $\exp(-50^{2}/2)=e^{-1250}$, which is effectively 0 (about $10^{-543}$). That is, samples whose temperatures differ by 50 °C are regarded as “completely unrelated,” and a measurement at one temperature gives no information about the prediction at a neighboring temperature. At points far from every observation point the prediction returns to the mean function, so there is almost no interpolation in the temperature direction. On the other hand, if the dopant amount differs by only 0.5 mol%, the correlation is as high as $e^{-0.125}=0.88$. The magnitudes of the numbers are determined merely by the units and do not express physical “closeness.”

(2) With the ARD kernel,

$$ \begin{aligned} \rho&=\exp\!\left[-\frac12\left(\frac{50}{100}\right)^{2}-\frac12\left(\frac{0.5}{1}\right)^{2}\right]\\ &=\exp(-0.25)=0.779 . \end{aligned} $$

The difference in each direction is “half a length scale,” so each weakens the correlation to the same degree.

(3) The distance in the kernel is measured by differences in the numerical values of the inputs, so if inputs with different units or orders of magnitude are simply placed side by side, the input with the larger magnitude (the temperature in °C) dominates the distance. Just as we standardized the inputs for gradient descent in Chapter 7, Section 7.3, it is good either to standardize each input to mean 0 and standard deviation 1 and then use an isotropic kernel, or to use an ARD kernel with a length scale $\ell_i$ (with units) for each input and choose the $\ell_i$ by the marginal likelihood. With ARD, an input whose chosen $\ell_i$ is large can be read as “having little effect on the prediction.” However, the mean and standard deviation used for standardization must be computed from the training data only (data leakage, Chapter 6).

Exercise 12.7: When the same point is measured twice

Solution

(1) Since the two inputs are the same, $\bm{K}=\sigma_f^{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$, whose two rows are equal, so $\det\bm{K}=\sigma_f^{4}(1-1)=0$ (the eigenvalues are $0$ and $2\sigma_f^{2}=1.28$). If $\sigma=0$, then $\bm{K}_y=\bm{K}$ is also singular, and neither the inverse matrix nor the Cholesky decomposition exists. In terms of meaning, the noiseless model assumes that “$f(7.0)$ is a single value and the observations reproduce it exactly,” so it cannot simultaneously explain two different observed values, $-3.40$ and $-3.30$. The data and the model contradict each other.

(2) If $\sigma=0.05$, then $\bm{K}_y=\sigma^{2}\bm{I}+\sigma_f^{2}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\tp}$. Since $\bm{K}_y\bm{1}=(\sigma^{2}+2\sigma_f^{2})\bm{1}$, we have $\bm{K}_y^{-1}\bm{1}=\bm{1}/(\sigma^{2}+2\sigma_f^{2})$. Because $\bm{k}_*=\sigma_f^{2}\bm{1}$, using the symmetry of $\bm{K}_y^{-1}$ we get

$$ \begin{aligned} \mu_*&=m+\sigma_f^{2}\bm{1}^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}(\bm{y}-m\bm{1})\\ &=m+\frac{\sigma_f^{2}\sum_n(y_n-m)}{2\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}}\\ &=m+\frac{2\sigma_f^{2}}{2\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}}(\bar{y}-m), \end{aligned} $$

where $\bar{y}=(y_1+y_2)/2=-3.35$. Substituting the numbers, $\mu_*=-4.47+\dfrac{1.28}{1.2825}\times1.12$ $=-4.47+1.11782$ $=-3.3522$. The variance has the same form as in Example 12.7: $\sigma_*^{2}=\dfrac{\sigma_f^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2}}=\dfrac{0.0016}{1.2825}=0.00125$ ($\sigma_*=0.035$). The predictive mean is the average of the two observed values, $-3.35$, shrunk toward the mean function $m$ by only $\sigma^{2}/(2\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma^{2})=0.2$%, so it is almost exactly that average.

(3) If we add jitter $\epsilon$, then computationally this amounts to replacing $\sigma^{2}$ by $\epsilon$ in (2), and the computation goes through. However, if $\epsilon$ is small, then $\sigma_*^{2}\approx\epsilon/2$ is also nearly 0, and we reach the overconfident conclusion that “$f(7.0)$ is almost certainly $-3.35$.” In reality the two measured values differ by as much as 0.1, so we should consider that there is measurement scatter (about $0.1/\sqrt2\approx0.07$ in standard deviation), and its magnitude must be put into the model as the noise variance. Jitter is a remedy for numerical computation and cannot replace an estimate of the noise.
