Chapter 13 Bayesian Optimization and Experimental Design — Where to Measure Next

Exercise 13.1: Comparing acquisition functions on three candidates

Solution

Since $f_{\mathrm{best}}=-4.1059$ and $\xi=0$, we have $d=\mu_*-f_{\mathrm{best}}=\mu_*+4.1059$ and $z=d/\sigma_*$. PI $=\Phi(z)$ and EI $=d\,\Phi(z)+\sigma_*\Normal(z\mid0,1)$ (Eqs. (13.6)–(13.8) of Chapter 13).

For $x=2.3$ mol%: $d=-4.1015+4.1059=0.0044$ and $z=0.0044/0.0708=0.0621$. $\Phi(0.0621)=0.5248$ and $\Normal(0.0621\mid0,1)=0.3982$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{EI}&=0.0044\times0.5248+0.0708\times0.3982\\ &=0.0023+0.0282=0.0305 . \end{aligned} $$

For $x=7.0$ mol%: $d=-4.6462+4.1059=-0.5403$ and $z=-0.5403/0.7831=-0.690$. $\Phi(-0.690)=0.2451$ and $\Normal(0.690\mid0,1)=0.3144$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{EI}&=-0.5403\times0.2451+0.7831\times0.3144\\ &=-0.1324+0.2462=0.1138 . \end{aligned} $$

For $x=10.0$ mol%: $d=-4.7301+4.1059=-0.6242$ and $z=-0.6242/0.6368=-0.980$. $\Phi(-0.980)=0.1635$ and $\Normal(0.980\mid0,1)=0.2468$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{EI}&=-0.6242\times0.1635+0.6368\times0.2468\\ &=-0.1021+0.1572=0.0551 . \end{aligned} $$

UCB $=\mu_*+\kappa\sigma_*$:

$x$ / mol%PIEIUCB ($\kappa=1$)UCB ($\kappa=2$)
2.30.5250.0305$-4.031$$-3.960$
7.00.2450.1138$-3.863$$-3.080$
10.00.1640.0551$-4.093$$-3.457$

(2) PI selects 2.3 mol% (right next to the best point, 2.4 mol%), EI selects 7.0 mol%, and UCB selects 7.0 mol% for both $\kappa=1$ and $\kappa=2$. This agrees with Figure 13.4 of Chapter 13, where the maximum of PI lies at 2.300 mol% and the maxima of EI and UCB lie near 7 mol% (at 6.800 and 6.875). The maximum on the grid is not exactly 7.0 because here we compared only three candidates. At 10.0 mol% as well, which is at the edge of the range and far from the observations so that $\sigma_*$ is large, the EI is larger than at 2.3 mol%.

Exercise 13.2: Deriving the EI for minimization from the integral

Solution

Suppose $\sigma_*\gt0$. Since $I=\max(f_{\mathrm{best}}-f-\xi,0)$ is positive only when $f\lt f_{\mathrm{best}}-\xi$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[I]=\int_{-\infty}^{f_{\mathrm{best}}-\xi}&\bigl(f_{\mathrm{best}}-f-\xi\bigr)\\ &\times\Normal(f\mid\mu_*,\sigma_*^{2})\,\dd f . \end{aligned} $$

Let $u=(\mu_*-f)/\sigma_*$. Then $f=\mu_*-\sigma_*u$ and $\dd f=-\sigma_*\,\dd u$, and as $f$ runs from $-\infty$ to $f_{\mathrm{best}}-\xi$, $u$ runs from $+\infty$ to $(\mu_*-f_{\mathrm{best}}+\xi)/\sigma_*=-d/\sigma_*=-z$ (where $d=f_{\mathrm{best}}-\mu_*-\xi$). Since the Gaussian density is symmetric in $u$, $\Normal(f\mid\mu_*,\sigma_*^{2})\,\abs{\dd f}=\Normal(u\mid0,1)\,\dd u$, and by reversing the orientation we can take the range of integration to be $[-z,\infty)$. The improvement in the integrand is

$$ \begin{aligned} f_{\mathrm{best}}-f-\xi&=f_{\mathrm{best}}-\mu_*+\sigma_*u-\xi\\ &=d+\sigma_*u . \end{aligned} $$

Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[I]&=\int_{-z}^{\infty}(d+\sigma_*u)\,\Normal(u\mid0,1)\,\dd u\\ &=d\int_{-z}^{\infty}\Normal(u\mid0,1)\,\dd u\\ &\quad+\sigma_*\int_{-z}^{\infty}u\,\Normal(u\mid0,1)\,\dd u . \end{aligned} $$

The integral in the first term is $1-\Phi(-z)=\Phi(z)$. For the second term, from $\diff{}{u}\Normal(u\mid0,1)=-u\,\Normal(u\mid0,1)$ we get

$$ \begin{aligned} &\int_{-z}^{\infty}u\,\Normal(u\mid0,1)\,\dd u\\ &=\Bigl[-\Normal(u\mid0,1)\Bigr]_{-z}^{\infty}\\ &=\Normal(-z\mid0,1)=\Normal(z\mid0,1) . \end{aligned} $$

Hence $\Ex[I]=d\,\Phi(z)+\sigma_*\Normal(z\mid0,1)$, with $d=f_{\mathrm{best}}-\mu_*-\xi$ and $z=d/\sigma_*$. This has the same form as the formula for maximization; only the direction of the improvement is reversed (Table 13.1 of Chapter 13).

Exercise 13.3: EI increases in both the mean and the width

Solution

Regard $\mathrm{EI}=d\,\Phi(z)+\sigma_*\Normal(z\mid0,1)$ (with $d=\mu_*-f_{\mathrm{best}}-\xi$ and $z=d/\sigma_*$) as a function of $\mu_*$ and $\sigma_*$. Below we abbreviate $\Normal(z\mid0,1)$ to $\Normal(z)$. The relations we use are $\diff{\Phi(z)}{z}=\Normal(z)$, $\diff{\Normal(z)}{z}=-z\,\Normal(z)$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} &\pdiff{d}{\mu_*}=1,\qquad \pdiff{z}{\mu_*}=\frac{1}{\sigma_*},\\ &\pdiff{z}{\sigma_*}=-\frac{d}{\sigma_*^{2}}=-\frac{z}{\sigma_*} . \end{aligned} $$

Derivative with respect to $\mu_*$: by the product rule and the chain rule,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{\mathrm{EI}}{\mu_*}&=\Phi(z)+d\,\Normal(z)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_*}\\ &\quad+\sigma_*\cdot\bigl(-z\,\Normal(z)\bigr)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_*}\\ &=\Phi(z)+z\,\Normal(z)-z\,\Normal(z)=\Phi(z) . \end{aligned} $$

(We used $d/\sigma_*=z$.)

Derivative with respect to $\sigma_*$: since $d$ does not depend on $\sigma_*$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{\mathrm{EI}}{\sigma_*}&=d\,\Normal(z)\cdot\Bigl(-\frac{z}{\sigma_*}\Bigr)+\Normal(z)\\ &\quad+\sigma_*\cdot\bigl(-z\,\Normal(z)\bigr)\cdot\Bigl(-\frac{z}{\sigma_*}\Bigr)\\ &=-z^{2}\Normal(z)+\Normal(z)+z^{2}\Normal(z)\\ &=\Normal(z) . \end{aligned} $$

Since $\Phi(z)\gt0$ and $\Normal(z)\gt0$, EI is a strictly increasing function of both $\mu_*$ and $\sigma_*$. The higher the predicted mean (exploitation) and the wider the predictive width (exploration), the larger the EI. This is why the contour lines in Figure 13.5 of Chapter 13 increase in value toward the right and toward the top. The ratio of the two partial derivatives, $\Normal(z)/\Phi(z)$, is larger the smaller $z$ is (that is, the more the mean falls short), and at such points increasing the width raises EI more.

Exercise 13.4: The next point for the firing temperature

Solution

(1) From $s=(T-1300)/200$: at 1250 °C, $s=-0.25$; at 1380 °C, $s=0.40$; at 1460 °C, $s=0.80$.

(2) $f_{\mathrm{best}}=92.0$ %, $d=\mu_*-92.0$, and $z=d/\sigma_*$.

  • 1250 °C: $d=-7.0$ and $z=-7.0/0.6=-11.7$. Both $\Phi(-11.7)$ and $\Normal(11.7\mid0,1)$ are at most $10^{-30}$, so PI and EI are effectively 0. UCB $=85.0+1.2=86.2$ %.
  • 1380 °C: $d=-0.2$ and $z=-0.4$. $\mathrm{PI}=\Phi(-0.4)=0.345$. $\mathrm{EI}=-0.2\times0.3446$ $+0.5\times0.3683$ $=-0.0689+0.1842$ $=0.115$ %. UCB $=91.8+1.0=92.8$ %.
  • 1460 °C: $d=-1.0$ and $z=-0.667$. $\mathrm{PI}=\Phi(-0.667)=0.253$. $\mathrm{EI}=-1.0\times0.2525$ $+1.5\times0.3194$ $=-0.2525+0.4792$ $=0.227$ %. UCB $=91.0+3.0=94.0$ %.

PI selects 1380 °C, and EI and UCB select 1460 °C. PI favors points whose mean is close to $f_{\mathrm{best}}$, whereas EI and UCB evaluate the possible upside of 1460 °C, where the width is large.

(3) Solving $g'(s)=8-10s+6\cos3s=0$ numerically gives $s\approx0.623$, that is, $T=1300+200\times0.623\approx1425$ °C, with $g\approx92.96$ % (this is a maximum because $g''(s)=-10-18\sin3s\lt0$). The true values are $g(1380\ ^{\circ}\mathrm{C})=92.26$ % and $g(1460\ ^{\circ}\mathrm{C})=92.55$ %, and the true maximum lies between the two candidates. Each candidate is only 0.4–0.7 % below the true maximum, a difference smaller than the noise standard deviation of 1.0 %. At this stage, a single observation cannot decide which of the two candidates is better; it makes sense to measure between the candidates (1400–1450 °C) and to repeat the same condition and average (Section 13.7 of Chapter 13). The choice of acquisition function is a judgment made on the model at that point in time, and it is not a guarantee of hitting the location of the true maximum.

Exercise 13.5: Random search and the guarantee of a grid

Solution

(1) The probability of landing in $[6.75,7.25]$ in a single trial is $0.5/10=0.05$. The probability of missing in all $n$ trials is $0.95^{n}$, so $1-0.95^{n}\ge0.9$, that is, $0.95^{n}\le0.1$. Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives $n\ln0.95\le\ln0.1$. Dividing by $\ln0.95\lt0$ reverses the inequality, and

$$ n\ge\frac{\ln0.1}{\ln0.95}=\frac{-2.3026}{-0.051293}\approx44.9 . $$

The minimum is $n=45$ trials ($1-0.95^{45}=0.9006$ and $1-0.95^{44}=0.8953$).

(2) For $n=20$, the grid points are $x_k=10(k-\frac12)/20=0.5k-0.25$ ($k=1,\dots,20$), that is, $0.25,0.75,\dots,9.75$, with spacing 0.5 mol%. For $k=14$ this gives $6.75$ and for $k=15$ it gives $7.25$, and both lie 0.25 mol% from the peak at 7.0. Hence the grid with $n=20$ is certain to satisfy the condition (in fact, wherever the peak lies in $[0.25,9.75]$, the distance to the nearest grid point is at most 0.25).

(3) In two dimensions, the probability that random search lands in the square in a single trial is $0.05\times0.05=0.0025$. For $1-0.9975^{n}\ge0.9$ we need $n\ge\ln0.1/\ln0.9975\approx919.9$, that is, 920 trials. The grid has 20 points in each direction, so $20^{2}=400$ trials. As the dimension increases, the number of trials needed grows exponentially for both (in $D$ dimensions the grid needs $20^{D}$). The grid is certain but spends the budget with “the same fineness everywhere,” whereas random search guarantees only in a probabilistic sense. Bayesian optimization is a method that tries to greatly reduce this number by using the assumption that the function is smooth and that only a few directions matter.

Exercise 13.6: Repeated measurements and the recommended point

Solution

(1) The maximum of the six observed values is $-3.3635$ at $x=7.125$ mol%, so the recommended point is 7.125 mol%.

(2) The means are

$$ \begin{aligned} \bar{y}(6.900)&=\tfrac13(-3.3908-3.4539-3.4994)\\ &=-3.4480,\\ \bar{y}(7.125)&=\tfrac13(-3.4051-3.4229-3.3635)\\ &=-3.3972 . \end{aligned} $$

The standard deviation of each mean is $0.05/\sqrt3=0.0289$, and the standard deviation of the difference of the means is $\sqrt{0.0289^{2}+0.0289^{2}}$ $=\sqrt{2/3}\times0.05=0.0408$. The difference is $-3.3972-(-3.4480)=0.0509$, which is a mere $0.0509/0.0408\approx1.25$ times the standard deviation of the difference.

(3) The true value $f(6.900)=-3.4066$ is higher than $f(7.125)=-3.4103$ by 0.0037, so both the maximum of the observed values and the means of three repetitions chose the wrong point. The true difference of 0.0037 is one fourteenth of the noise 0.05, and it cannot possibly be distinguished with means of three repetitions each (following the idea of Example 13.8 of Chapter 13, to make the probability of mistaking the two below 5 % we need $n\gt(1.645\times0.0707/0.0037)^{2}\approx988$ at each point, that is, about 990 repetitions). What we can say is the following. (i) If we recommend the maximum of the observed values, the point with the luckiest noise tends to be chosen. It is better to recommend by the mean or the posterior mean. (ii) Two conditions whose true difference is far smaller than the measurement noise should be regarded as equivalent in practice, and it is better not to spend the budget on telling them apart. Both are within 0.01 of the true maximum, $-3.400$. (iii) The recommended condition should be reported together with a check of reproducibility by remaking the samples.

Exercise 13.7: UCB is a quantile of the predictive distribution

Solution

(1) $u=(f_*-\mu_*)/\sigma_*$ follows the standard Gaussian distribution. Since $\sigma_*\gt0$,

$$ \begin{aligned} f_*\le\mu_*+\kappa\sigma_*&\iff\frac{f_*-\mu_*}{\sigma_*}\le\kappa\\ &\iff u\le\kappa , \end{aligned} $$

and therefore $P(f_*\le\mu_*+\kappa\sigma_*)=P(u\le\kappa)=\Phi(\kappa)$.

(2) For $\kappa=1$: $\Phi(1)=0.841$. For $\kappa=2$: $\Phi(2)=0.977$. UCB ($\kappa=2$) is an optimistic estimate in the sense that “the probability that the true value exceeds it is only about 2.3 %.”

(3) The value satisfying $\Phi(\kappa)=0.95$ is $\kappa=1.645$. The upper 5 % point is $\mu_*+1.645\sigma_*$.

(4) Increasing $\kappa$ means comparing at a higher quantile (a more optimistic estimate). In the quantile location $\mu_*+\kappa\sigma_*$, the contribution of $\sigma_*$ grows in proportion to $\kappa$, so points with a somewhat lower mean but a large width rise to the top. That is, the search leans toward exploration. $\kappa=0$ means comparing at the center of the distribution (mean = median), which gives pure exploitation.

Exercise 13.8: Planning a two-week search with four furnaces

Solution

The following is one reasonable example of a plan (there is not just one answer).

(1) Initial points: since the input is two-dimensional, place eight points (two days' worth; several times the input dimension) in an arrangement that fills the range 1100–1450 °C × 1–10 h without bias (a Latin hypercube, or a slightly perturbed grid). If there are results for similar materials from the past, include one or two of those conditions or add them as initial data. Including one set of repetitions of the same condition gives a rough estimate of the noise magnitude $\sigma$.

(2) Kernel and scales: use an ARD kernel, which has a length scale for each input (Example 13.12 of Chapter 13). Either give the temperature its own $\ell_T$ in °C as it is, or normalize both inputs to $[0,1]$. Since the effect of holding time is often logarithmic, taking $\ln t$ as the input is also one option. Re-select the hyperparameters ($\ell_T$, $\ell_t$, $\sigma_f$, $\sigma$) every day by the marginal likelihood. The objective is the relative density in % (the range is 0–100 %, so when it is close to 100 % also consider a transformation).

(3) Four conditions per day: choose one maximum point of EI, tentatively assume that it was “observed at the value of the predicted mean,” update the model, and choose the next maximum point of EI; repeat this four times. Alternatively, draw four functions from the posterior distribution and choose the maximum point of each (Thompson sampling). Check that the four points are not too close to one another.

(4) Constraints: exclude conditions above 1450 °C from the search range (a known constraint). Record conditions where the sample cracks and cannot be measured as “failures,” predict whether cracking occurs with a separate classification model (the logistic regression of Chapter 9, or classification with a Gaussian process), and choose by the value of EI multiplied by the “probability of not cracking.” If the density of cracked samples is discarded as missing values, a dangerous region will be chosen over and over.

(5) When to stop, and the recommendation: stop when all 40 runs have been used, or when the maximum of EI stays below about 1/10 of the measurement noise (for example, the standard deviation of repeated relative-density measurements) for many days and the recommended point no longer changes. The recommended point is not the maximum of the observed values but the already-observed condition at which the predicted mean of the surrogate model is largest; on the final day, repeat that condition and the runner-up condition 2–4 times each to check reproducibility and the difference. In the report, write the search range, the initial arrangement, the acquisition function, and how the hyperparameters were chosen, and state clearly that nothing can be said about the outside of the range.

← Back to Chapter 13, “Bayesian Optimization and Experimental Design — Where to Measure Next”

Chapter 14 Representations in Neural Networks — Learning the Basis Functions

Exercise 14.1: Forward propagation in a 2–2–1 network

Solution

(1) The activations of the first layer are $\bm{a}^{(1)}=\bm{W}^{(1)}\bm{x}+\bm{b}^{(1)}$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} a^{(1)}_1&=1\times0.5+1\times1.0+0=1.5,\\ a^{(1)}_2&=1\times0.5+(-1)\times1.0-0.5=-1.0 . \end{aligned} $$

Passing these through tanh gives $\bm{z}^{(1)}=(\tanh1.5,\ \tanh(-1.0))$ $=(0.905148,\ -0.761594)$. The activation of the output layer is

$$ \begin{aligned} a^{(2)}&=2\times0.905148\\ &\quad+(-1)\times(-0.761594)+(-1)\\ &=1.810296+0.761594-1=1.571890, \end{aligned} $$

and $\hat{y}=\sigma(1.571890)=1/(1+e^{-1.571890})$ $=1/(1+0.207652)=0.828053$.

(2) Since $y=1$, $\ell=-\ln\hat{y}=-\ln0.828053=0.188678$. Since the output layer pairs a sigmoid with the cross-entropy, the error at the output layer is $\delta^{(2)}=\hat{y}-y=0.828053-1=-0.171947$. Because it is negative, learning proceeds in the direction that increases $a^{(2)}$ (that is, brings $\hat{y}$ closer to 1).

(3) With ReLU, $\bm{z}^{(1)}=(\max(0,1.5),\ \max(0,-1.0))=(1.5,\ 0)$. Then $a^{(2)}=2\times1.5+(-1)\times0-1=2$ and $\hat{y}=\sigma(2)=0.880797$.

(4) The first layer has $2\times(2+1)=6$ parameters and the second layer has $1\times(2+1)=3$, for a total of 9.

Exercise 14.2: The relation between tanh and sigmoid

Solution

(1) Since $\sigma(2a)=\dfrac{1}{1+e^{-2a}}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} 2\sigma(2a)-1&=\frac{2-(1+e^{-2a})}{1+e^{-2a}}=\frac{1-e^{-2a}}{1+e^{-2a}}\\ &=\frac{e^{a}-e^{-a}}{e^{a}+e^{-a}}=\tanh a . \end{aligned} $$

In the third equality we multiplied the numerator and the denominator by $e^{a}$.

(2) Applying (1) to $a_j=\bm{w}_j^{\tp}\bm{x}+b_j$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} f(\bm{x})&=\sum_jW^{(2)}_j\bigl\{2\sigma(2\bm{w}_j^{\tp}\bm{x}+2b_j)-1\bigr\}+b^{(2)}\\ &=\sum_j\bigl(2W^{(2)}_j\bigr)\,\sigma\bigl((2\bm{w}_j)^{\tp}\bm{x}+2b_j\bigr)\\ &\quad+\Bigl(b^{(2)}-\sum_jW^{(2)}_j\Bigr). \end{aligned} $$

Therefore, in a network with sigmoid hidden layers, if we replace the weights and biases by

$$ \begin{aligned} &\bm{w}_j\to2\bm{w}_j,\quad b_j\to2b_j,\quad W^{(2)}_j\to2W^{(2)}_j,\\ &b^{(2)}\to b^{(2)}-\sum_jW^{(2)}_j \end{aligned} $$

then exactly the same function is represented. The reverse direction (from sigmoid to tanh) can be converted in the same way. As a numerical example, with the weights of Exercise 14.1 and $\bm{x}=(0.3,-0.7)^{\tp}$, both the tanh version and the converted sigmoid version give $f=-2.22202$ (here $f$ is $a^{(2)}$, before passing through the sigmoid).

(3) The set of representable functions does not change. However, how easy the network is to train can differ. tanh has slope 1 at the origin and its outputs are distributed around 0, so the initial gradients are large and the inputs to the next layer are not biased. Sigmoid has slope 1/4 at the origin and its outputs are always positive, so with the same initialization and the same learning rate, learning tends to be slow. Even for the same set of functions, a different way of parametrizing it (the scale) changes the behavior of gradient methods.

Exercise 14.3: What goes wrong when squared error is used on a sigmoid output

Solution

(1) By the chain rule and $\sigma'(a)=\hat{y}(1-\hat{y})$,

$$ \pdiff{\ell_{\mathrm{SE}}}{a}=\pdiff{\ell_{\mathrm{SE}}}{\hat{y}}\,\diff{\hat{y}}{a}=(\hat{y}-y)\,\hat{y}(1-\hat{y}). $$

(2) $\hat{y}=\sigma(-5)=1/(1+e^{5})$ $=1/149.413159=0.0066929$. With the cross-entropy, $\delta=\hat{y}-y=-0.993307$. With squared error, $(-0.993307)\times0.0066929\times(1-0.0066929)$ $=(-0.993307)\times0.0066481$ $=-0.0066036$. The ratio of the magnitudes is about 150 times, and the squared-error gradient is far smaller.

(3) With squared error, the more badly the prediction misses so that the sigmoid is saturated ($\hat{y}$ close to 0 or 1), the closer $\hat{y}(1-\hat{y})$ gets to 0, and the gradient vanishes. The learning signal from exactly the data that most need correcting, the “confidently wrong” ones, becomes weak, and learning makes little progress. With the cross-entropy, the steep growth of $-\ln\hat{y}$ cancels the saturation of the sigmoid, and the error becomes exactly the size of the miss, $\hat{y}-y$. Moreover, squared error does not arise from the likelihood of the Bernoulli distribution, so it also has no justification as an estimate of a probability.

Exercise 14.4: One hidden unit cannot separate XOR

Solution

(1) Let $u=\bm{w}^{\tp}\bm{x}+b$. Since $\sigma$ is monotonically increasing and $\sigma(0)=1/2$, we have $\hat{y}\gt1/2\iff vh(u)+c\gt0$.

  • If $v=0$: the condition is $c\gt0$, which does not depend on $\bm{x}$. It holds for all $\bm{x}$ or for none (the empty set).
  • If $v\gt0$: the condition is $h(u)\gt-c/v$. Since $h$ is monotone nondecreasing, the set $S=\{u:h(u)\gt-c/v\}$ has the property that “if some $u$ lies in $S$, then every larger $u$ does too.” Hence $S$ is one of $\R$ itself, the empty set, $\{u\gt t\}$, or $\{u\ge t\}$ (with $t=\inf S$).
  • If $v\lt0$: the condition is $h(u)\lt-c/v$, and in the same way it is one of $\R$ itself, the empty set, $\{u\lt t\}$, or $\{u\le t\}$.

In every case, the set of $\bm{x}$ with $\hat{y}\gt1/2$ is either a half-plane bounded by the line $\bm{w}^{\tp}\bm{x}+b=t$ (with or without the boundary), or the whole plane, or the empty set.

(2) Suppose the four points were separated correctly. Then the two points $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ with $y=1$ lie inside the half-plane, and the two points $(0,0)$ and $(1,1)$ with $y=0$ lie outside. The whole plane and the empty set do not split the data into two classes, so we consider only the half-plane case. In the case “$u\gt t$” (the others are similar), $u$ is a linear function of $\bm{x}$ plus a constant, so $u(1,0)+u(0,1)=u(0,0)+u(1,1)$ holds (both sides equal $w_1+w_2+2b$). The two terms on the left are greater than $t$ and the two terms on the right are at most $t$, so left side $\gt2t\ge$ right side, a contradiction. For “$u\ge t$,” “$u\lt t$,” and “$u\le t$,” only the direction of the inequalities and whether equality is allowed change, and the same contradiction arises. Therefore no weights exist that correctly separate the four XOR points.

(3) The XOR data in Simulator 14.1 consist of four clusters, and a single straight line can correctly separate at most three of them (about 75 % of the whole). With $H=1$, by (1) the decision boundary is a single straight line; together with the fact that the clusters are spread out by noise, the accuracy is bounded above by roughly 75 % (since noise makes some points in the tails of the clusters spill over to the other side of the boundary, with a finite amount of data it fluctuates around 75 %), and in practice it stayed at 73 %. This differs from the case in which the solution is not found by training even with more hidden units ($H=2$, default seed): with $H=1$, the expressive power itself is insufficient.

Exercise 14.5: Representing the band gap of a mixed crystal with ReLU

Solution

(1) $E_g(0)=1.20$ eV, $E_g(1)=2.40$ eV, and $E_g(0.5)=0.5\times1.20+0.5\times2.40$ $-0.80\times0.25$ $=0.60+1.20-0.20=1.60$ eV.

(2) The slopes are $m_1=(1.60-1.20)/0.5=0.8$ eV on $[0,0.5]$ and $m_2=(2.40-1.60)/0.5=1.6$ eV on $[0.5,1]$. For $0\le x$ we have $\relu(x)=x$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} f(x)={}&1.20+0.8\,\relu(x)\\ &+0.8\,\relu(x-0.5)\quad(\mathrm{eV}), \end{aligned} $$

that is, $c=1.20$ eV, $v_1=m_1=0.8$ eV, and $v_2=m_2-m_1=0.8$ eV.

(3) $E_g(x)=1.20+1.20x-0.80x+0.80x^{2}$ $=1.20+0.40x+0.80x^{2}$, so $E_g''(x)=1.6$ eV (constant). The error of the piecewise linear function is largest at the midpoint of each interval, and its magnitude is $E_g''h_s^{2}/8=1.6\times0.5^{2}/8=0.05$ eV (since $g''$ is constant within each interval, this matches the upper limit in Eq. (14.18) of Chapter 14 with equality). It occurs at $x=0.25$ and $x=0.75$. For example, at $x=0.25$, $E_g=1.20+0.10+0.05=1.35$ eV and $f=1.20+0.8\times0.25=1.40$ eV, a difference of 0.05 eV. This is larger than the noise standard deviation of 0.03 eV, so a three-point piecewise linear function cannot fully capture the structure of the data.

(4) From $1.6\,h_s^{2}/8\le0.01$ we get $h_s^{2}\le0.05$, so $h_s\le0.2236$. Since $h_s=1/K$, $K\ge4.47$; that is, $K=5$ intervals are needed (for $K=4$ the maximum error is 0.0125 eV, and for $K=5$ it is 0.008 eV). Constructing it as in (2), there are $K=5$ hidden units. (However, for $x\ge0$ the first unit $\relu(x)$ is just $x$ itself, so if there is a direct linear term from the input to the output, 4 units suffice.)

(5) The true function of data set B is exactly a linear combination of $1,\ x,\ x(1-x)$ (the Vegard's law line plus the bowing term), so linear regression with the basis functions of Chapter 8 can represent it without error using 3 parameters. The solution is uniquely determined by the normal equations, the coefficients (such as the bowing parameter $-0.80$ eV) have physical meaning, and Bayesian linear regression can also give the uncertainty. A network does not know the form, so it needs correspondingly more units and data, and its learning also depends on the initial values. If the form of the function is known from physics, it is better to use that form as the basis.

Exercise 14.6: Properties of a sum of per-atom energies

Solution

(1) In the dimer each atom has one partner, so $G_1=G_2=\exp\{-(2.5/2.5)^{2}\}$ $=e^{-1}=0.367879$. Then $2G-1=-0.264241$, $\tanh(-0.264241)=-0.258258$, and $E_i=0.5\times(-0.258258)-3.0=-3.129129$ eV. Hence $E=2\times(-3.129129)=-6.258258$ eV.

(2) The coordinates of the four atoms are $0,\ 2.5,\ 20,\ 22.5$ Å. For example, the descriptor of the atom at $x=2.5$ receives, in addition to the $e^{-1}$ from its dimer partner, the contributions $\exp\{-(17.5/2.5)^{2}\}=e^{-49}\approx5.2\times10^{-22}$ and $\exp\{-(20/2.5)^{2}\}=e^{-64}\approx1.6\times10^{-28}$ from the two distant atoms. Even for the atom at $x=0$, only $e^{-64}$ and $e^{-81}$ are added. The slope of $E_i$ with respect to $G$ is $\diff{E_i}{G}=0.5\times2\times(1-\tanh^{2})$ $=1-0.258258^{2}=0.933303$ eV, so the deviation of the four-atom sum from twice the result of (1) is $0.933303\times2$ $\times(e^{-49}+e^{-64}+e^{-64}+e^{-81})$ $\approx9.8\times10^{-22}$ eV. This is far smaller than the rounding size (about $10^{-15}$ eV) when an energy of about 12.5 eV is represented in double-precision floating point, so numerically it cannot be distinguished from $E=-12.516516$ eV $=2\times(-6.258258)$ eV. In a model that includes a cutoff function $f_{\mathrm c}$, the contribution of atoms beyond the cutoff radius is exactly 0, and the energy is exactly doubled (extensivity).

(3) There are $10!=3628800$ ways of numbering the atoms, so for the same structure (if all atoms are of the same element) $3628800$ different input vectors arise. In addition, translations and rotations also change the input. The problems are the following two: (i) the same structure can yield different energies (invariance under permutation, translation, and rotation is not guaranteed, and it is also impractical to show all the orderings in the training data); (ii) the dimension of the input is determined by the number of atoms, so the same network cannot be used for structures with different numbers of atoms (surfaces, cells containing defects, cells of different sizes), and extensivity does not hold either. The construction by per-atom descriptors and a sum solves these problems by design.

Exercise 14.7: Symmetries of the weight space

Solution

(1) There are $3!=6$ permutations and $2^{3}=8$ choices of whether or not to flip the sign of each unit, giving $6\times8=48$ (including the original weights).

(2) Flipping the sign of all the incoming weights, the bias, and the outgoing weight of hidden unit 1 gives $(0.7,\,0.2,\,1.5)\to(-0.7,\,-0.2,\,-1.5)$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} \tilde{f}(s)={}&-1.5\tanh(-0.7s-0.2)\\ &-0.8\tanh(-1.3s+0.4)+0.1 . \end{aligned} $$

At $s=0.37$: $0.7\times0.37+0.2=0.459$, $\tanh0.459=0.429269$, $-1.3\times0.37+0.4=-0.081$, and $\tanh(-0.081)=-0.080823$. The original expression is $1.5\times0.429269-0.8\times(-0.080823)+0.1$ $=0.643903+0.064659+0.1$ $=0.808562$. For the flipped expression, $-1.5\times\tanh(-0.459)$ $=-1.5\times(-0.429269)=0.643903$, so it also gives $0.808562$.

(3) For $a=1$, $\relu(-1)=0$ and $-\relu(1)=-1$, so $\relu(-a)\ne-\relu(a)$. Therefore, replacing $v\,\relu(wa+b)$ by $(-v)\,\relu(-wa-b)$ generally changes the value (example: $v=1,\ w=1,\ b=0$, input 1, giving 1 and 0). Instead, for $c\gt0$ we have $\relu(ca)=c\,\relu(a)$. Using $c=4$ for $f(s)=2\relu(s-0.5)$ gives

$$ f(s)=\frac{2}{4}\,\relu\bigl(4s-2\bigr)=0.5\,\relu(4s-2). $$

Checking: at $s=0.2$ both are 0; at $s=0.9$ they are $2\times0.4=0.8$ and $0.5\times1.6=0.8$; at $s=1.4$ they are $2\times0.9=1.8$ and $0.5\times3.6=1.8$. Since $c$ may be any positive number, there is a continuum of weights representing the same function. For this reason, in a ReLU network, comparing the magnitudes of the weights themselves (for example, only $\norm{\bm{W}^{(1)}}$) is of limited meaning, and weight decay also affects the balance of the weight magnitudes across layers.

Exercise 14.8: Linear networks and narrow layers

Solution

(1) By Eq. (14.8) of Chapter 14 (composition of linear layers), $\bm{W}=\bm{W}^{(2)}\bm{W}^{(1)}$ and $\bm{b}=\bm{W}^{(2)}\bm{b}^{(1)}+b^{(2)}$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{W}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}\\ &=\bigl(1\cdot1+(-1)\cdot0+2\cdot(-1),\\ &\qquad 1\cdot2+(-1)\cdot1+2\cdot1\bigr)\\ &=\begin{pmatrix}-1&3\end{pmatrix}, \end{aligned} $$ $$ \bm{b}=1\cdot1+(-1)\cdot0+2\cdot(-1)+0.5=-0.5 . $$

Hence $\hat{y}=-x_1+3x_2-0.5$. For example, for $\bm{x}=(0.3,-1.2)^{\tp}$, both the step-by-step computation through the two layers and the single-layer formula give $-4.4$.

(2) Since $\bm{W}^{(1)}\in\R^{1\times3}$ (a row vector $\bm{p}^{\tp}$) and $\bm{W}^{(2)}\in\R^{3\times1}$ (a column vector $\bm{q}$), $\bm{W}^{(2)}\bm{W}^{(1)}=\bm{q}\bm{p}^{\tp}$. For any $\bm{x}$, $\bm{q}\bm{p}^{\tp}\bm{x}=(\bm{p}^{\tp}\bm{x})\,\bm{q}$ is a constant multiple of $\bm{q}$, so the image (column space) is a subspace of dimension at most 1 spanned by $\bm{q}$. Hence the rank is at most 1. In general, $\rank(\bm{A}\bm{B})\le\min(\rank\bm{A},\rank\bm{B})$, and if the width of an intermediate layer is $r$, the rank of the whole is at most $r$.

(3) If a network that passes the input through a narrow layer (of width $r$) and returns it to the original dimension is trained so that its output reproduces its input, it becomes a method of representing the data compressed into $r$ dimensions. This is called an autoencoder. When the activation function is the identity map and squared error is used, the $r$-dimensional subspace obtained by training coincides with the space spanned by the top $r$ principal components of principal component analysis (PCA) (Chapter 18, Section 18.8). If nonlinear activation functions are introduced, curved low-dimensional structures can also be represented.

← Back to Chapter 14, “Representations in Neural Networks — Learning the Basis Functions”

Chapter 15 Backpropagation and Learning — Distributing Gradients with the Chain Rule

Exercise 15.1: A 1–1–1 ReLU network

Solution

(1) Forward propagation: $a^{(1)}=W^{(1)}x+b^{(1)}=0.8\times1.5-0.2=1.0$. Since $a^{(1)}\gt0$, $z^{(1)}=\relu(1.0)=1.0$. Then $\hat{y}=a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}z^{(1)}+b^{(2)}$ $=1.5\times1.0+0.1=1.6$. The loss is $\ell=\tfrac12(1.6-2.0)^{2}=0.08$.

Backward propagation: because the output is the identity and the loss is half the squared error, $\delta^{(2)}=\hat{y}-y=-0.4$. The derivative of the ReLU is 1 since $a^{(1)}=1.0\gt0$, so $\delta^{(1)}=1\times W^{(2)}\delta^{(2)}=1.5\times(-0.4)=-0.6$. The gradients are

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{\ell}{W^{(2)}}&=\delta^{(2)}z^{(1)}=-0.4,\\ \pdiff{\ell}{b^{(2)}}&=\delta^{(2)}=-0.4,\\ \pdiff{\ell}{W^{(1)}}&=\delta^{(1)}x=-0.6\times1.5=-0.9,\\ \pdiff{\ell}{b^{(1)}}&=\delta^{(1)}=-0.6 . \end{aligned} $$

(2) Updating with $\eta=0.1$ gives $W^{(1)}=0.8+0.09=0.89$, $b^{(1)}=-0.2+0.06=-0.14$, $W^{(2)}=1.5+0.04=1.54$, and $b^{(2)}=0.1+0.04=0.14$. After the update, $a^{(1)}=0.89\times1.5-0.14=1.195$, $z^{(1)}=1.195$, $\hat{y}=1.54\times1.195+0.14=1.9803$, and $\ell=\tfrac12(1.9803-2.0)^{2}$ $=\tfrac12\times0.000388=1.94\times10^{-4}$. The loss dropped from 0.08 to about 1/400 of its value.

(3) For $x=-1.0$, $a^{(1)}=0.8\times(-1.0)-0.2=-1.0\lt0$, so $z^{(1)}=0$, $\hat{y}=b^{(2)}=0.1$, and $\ell=\tfrac12(0.1-2.0)^{2}=1.805$. Here $\delta^{(2)}=-1.9$, but the derivative of the ReLU is 0, so $\delta^{(1)}=0$. Therefore $\partial\ell/\partial W^{(1)}=\partial\ell/\partial b^{(1)}=0$. Also, since $z^{(1)}=0$, $\partial\ell/\partial W^{(2)}=\delta^{(2)}z^{(1)}=0$. The only parameter that moves is $b^{(2)}$, with $\partial\ell/\partial b^{(2)}=-1.9$; after the update $b^{(2)}=0.1+0.19=0.29$ (the loss becomes 1.462). For data on which the hidden unit is not active, the weights of the hidden layer learn nothing.

Exercise 15.2: Combining a sigmoid output with the squared error

Solution

(1) By the chain rule, $\delta=\pdiff{\ell}{\hat{y}}\diff{\hat{y}}{a}$. Since $\pdiff{\ell}{\hat{y}}=\hat{y}-y$ and $\diff{\hat{y}}{a}=\sigma'(a)=\sigma(a)(1-\sigma(a))=\hat{y}(1-\hat{y})$, we obtain $\delta=(\hat{y}-y)\hat{y}(1-\hat{y})$.

(2) For $y=1$ and $\hat{y}=0.01$, the squared error gives $\delta=(0.01-1)\times0.01\times0.99=-0.009801$. The cross-entropy gives $\delta=0.01-1=-0.99$. The $\delta$ of the squared error is about 1/100 as large. (For $\hat{y}=0.5$ the values are $-0.125$ and $-0.5$, and for $\hat{y}=0.99$ they are $-0.000099$ and $-0.01$.)

(3) With the squared error the factor $\hat{y}(1-\hat{y})$ is multiplied in. So when the sigmoid saturates and $\hat{y}$ is close to 0 or 1, $\delta$ is small even if the prediction is badly wrong, all of the gradients in Eqs. (15.6)–(15.8) of Chapter 15 become small, and learning hardly progresses. With the cross-entropy, the derivative $1/\hat{y}$ of $\ln\hat{y}$ cancels this factor, and $\delta=\hat{y}-y$ is proportional to how far the prediction misses. Moreover, the cross-entropy is the negative log-likelihood of the Bernoulli distribution, so it is also consistent with maximum likelihood estimation (Chapter 5, Section 5.5; Chapter 14, Section 14.6).

Exercise 15.3: The second update

Solution

The parameters after the update are $\bm{W}^{(1)}=((0.709829,-0.195085),$ $(0.657292,0.128646))$, $\bm{b}^{(1)}=(0.309829,-0.242708)$, $\bm{W}^{(2)}=(1.10770,-0.830494)$, and $b^{(2)}=0.455266$, and the forward-propagation values are $\bm{a}^{(1)}=(0.922116,\,0.478907)$, $\bm{z}^{(1)}=(0.726897,\,0.445368)$, $a^{(2)}=0.890573$, $\hat{y}=0.709008$, and $L=-\ln0.709008=0.343888$.

(1) $\delta^{(2)}=\hat{y}-y=0.709008-1=-0.290992$. Also $h'(a^{(1)}_1)=1-0.726897^{2}=0.471621$ and $h'(a^{(1)}_2)=1-0.445368^{2}=0.801648$. The vector $\bm{W}^{(2)\tp}\delta^{(2)}$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{W}^{(2)\tp}\delta^{(2)}&=\bigl(1.10770\times(-0.290992),\\ &\qquad -0.830494\times(-0.290992)\bigr)\\ &=(-0.322331,\ 0.241667). \end{aligned} $$

Multiplying component by component gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{\delta}^{(1)}&=\bigl(0.471621\times(-0.322331),\\ &\qquad 0.801648\times0.241667\bigr)\\ &=(-0.152018,\ 0.193732). \end{aligned} $$

(2) For the output layer,

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{L}{\bm{W}^{(2)}}&=\delta^{(2)}\bm{z}^{(1)\tp}\\ &=(-0.290992\times0.726897,\\ &\qquad -0.290992\times0.445368)\\ &=(-0.211521,\ -0.129598), \end{aligned} $$

and $\partial L/\partial b^{(2)}=-0.290992$. For the hidden layer, $\partial L/\partial\bm{W}^{(1)}=\bm{\delta}^{(1)}\bm{x}^{\tp}$ $=((-0.152018,\ -0.0760089),$ $(0.193732,\ 0.0968658))$ and $\partial L/\partial\bm{b}^{(1)}=(-0.152018,\ 0.193732)$. The sum of squares of the nine components is $\norm{\bm{g}}^{2}=0.282656$.

(3) Updating with $\eta=0.5$ gives $\bm{W}^{(1)}=((0.785838,-0.157081),$ $(0.560426,0.0802131))$, $\bm{b}^{(1)}=(0.385838,-0.339574)$, $\bm{W}^{(2)}=(1.21346,-0.765695)$, and $b^{(2)}=0.600762$. Forward propagation gives $\bm{a}^{(1)}=(1.093136,\,0.260959)$, $\bm{z}^{(1)}=(0.798020,\,0.255192)$,

$$ \begin{aligned} a^{(2)}&=1.21346\times0.798020\\ &\quad-0.765695\times0.255192+0.600762\\ &=1.373726, \end{aligned} $$

$\hat{y}=\sigma(1.373726)=0.797981$, and $L=-\ln0.797981=0.225670$.

The second decrease is $0.343888-0.225670=0.118218$, which is smaller than the first one, 0.370550. This is because $\hat{y}$ approached 1, so $\abs{\delta^{(2)}}$ fell from 0.510533 to 0.290992, and the sum of squares of the gradient also fell from 1.00151 to 0.282656. The difference from the first-order approximation of the decrease, $\eta\norm{\bm{g}}^{2}=0.141328$, is smaller than for the first update: the smaller the gradient, the better the first-order approximation holds.

Exercise 15.4: The optimal step size for finite differences

Solution

(1) Differentiate $E(\epsilon)=\dfrac{\epsilon^{2}}{6}\abs{L'''}+\dfrac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}}{\epsilon}$ with respect to $\epsilon$ and set the result equal to 0.

$$ \diff{E}{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon}{3}\abs{L'''}-\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}}{\epsilon^{2}}=0 $$

gives

$$ \epsilon^{*}=\Bigl(\frac{3\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}}{\abs{L'''}}\Bigr)^{1/3}. $$

Using $(\epsilon^{*})^{3}\abs{L'''}=3\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}$, we have $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}/\epsilon^{*}=(\epsilon^{*})^{2}\abs{L'''}/3$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} E(\epsilon^{*})&=\frac{(\epsilon^{*})^{2}\abs{L'''}}{6}+\frac{(\epsilon^{*})^{2}\abs{L'''}}{3}\\ &=\frac{(\epsilon^{*})^{2}\abs{L'''}}{2}\\ &=\frac12\abs{L'''}^{1/3}\bigl(3\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}\bigr)^{2/3}, \end{aligned} $$

that is, the minimum error is proportional to $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}^{2/3}$.

(2) For the forward difference, we minimize the sum $E_{\mathrm{f}}(\epsilon)$ of the truncation error $\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\abs{L''}$ and the rounding error $2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}/\epsilon$. From $\diff{E_{\mathrm{f}}}{\epsilon}=\tfrac12\abs{L''}-2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}/\epsilon^{2}=0$ we get $\epsilon^{2}=4\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}/\abs{L''}$, that is, $\epsilon^{*}=2\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}/\abs{L''}}$. The error at this step size is $2\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}\abs{L}\abs{L''}}$, which is proportional to $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}^{1/2}$ (larger than the $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}^{2/3}$ of the central difference).

(3) The optimal step sizes for the central difference and the forward difference are

$$ \begin{aligned} \epsilon^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}&=\Bigl(\frac{3\times1.11\times10^{-16}\times0.714438}{0.0334}\Bigr)^{1/3}\\ &=(7.1\times10^{-15})^{1/3}=1.9\times10^{-5},\\ \epsilon^{*}_{\mathrm{fwd}}&=2\sqrt{\frac{1.11\times10^{-16}\times0.714438}{0.5230}}\\ &=2\sqrt{1.52\times10^{-16}}=2.5\times10^{-8} \end{aligned} $$

In Figure 15.5 of Chapter 15, the bottom of the valley for the central difference is near $\epsilon=10^{-5}$ and that for the forward difference is near $10^{-8}$–$3\times10^{-8}$, which agree to within an order of magnitude. The figure shows the largest error over the nine parameters, so the coefficients differ slightly from the estimate for a single parameter.

Exercise 15.5: He initialization

Solution

(1) Suppose the density $p(a)$ of $a$ satisfies $p(-a)=p(a)$. Since $\relu(a)^{2}$ equals $a^{2}$ for $a\gt0$ and 0 for $a\le0$, $\Ex[\relu(a)^{2}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}a^{2}p(a)\,\dd a$. By symmetry, $\int_{-\infty}^{0}a^{2}p(a)\,\dd a=\int_{0}^{\infty}a^{2}p(a)\,\dd a$, so this is exactly half of $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}a^{2}p(a)\,\dd a$. The mean of a symmetric distribution is 0, so $\int a^{2}p(a)\,\dd a=\Ex[a^{2}]=\Var[a]$. Hence $\Ex[\relu(a)^{2}]=\tfrac12\Var[a]$.

(2) From the derivation in Section 15.6.2, $\Var[a^{(l)}_j]=D_{l-1}\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}^{2}\Ex[(z^{(l-1)})^{2}]$. If $z^{(l-1)}=\relu(a^{(l-1)})$ and $a^{(l-1)}$ is symmetric about 0, then by (1) $\Ex[(z^{(l-1)})^{2}]=\tfrac12\Var[a^{(l-1)}]$. Therefore $\Var[a^{(l)}]=\tfrac12D_{l-1}\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}^{2}\Var[a^{(l-1)}]$, and the condition for this to equal $\Var[a^{(l-1)}]$ is $\tfrac12D_{l-1}\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}^{2}=1$, that is, $\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}^{2}=2/D_{l-1}$. ($a^{(l-1)}$ is symmetric because the weights are drawn independently from a symmetric distribution with mean 0, so the signs of the terms of $a^{(l-1)}=\sum_iW_{ji}z_i$ are interchanged with equal probability.)

(3) He: $\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=\sqrt{2/64}=0.1768$. Xavier (64 inputs and 64 outputs): $\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}^{2}=2/(64+64)=1/64$, so the factor per layer is $\tfrac12\times64\times\tfrac{1}{64}=\tfrac12$. After 10 layers the variance is multiplied by $(1/2)^{10}=1/1024\approx9.8\times10^{-4}$, so the signal becomes three orders of magnitude smaller (the same tendency as the dashed line in Figure 15.7(b) of Chapter 15).

Exercise 15.6: Per-atom energies and forces

Solution

The interatomic distances are $\abs{x_1-x_2}=1.0$, $\abs{x_2-x_3}=1.2$, and $\abs{x_1-x_3}=2.2$ Å, so $e^{-1.0}=0.367879$, $e^{-1.44}=0.236928$, and $e^{-4.84}=0.007907$.

(1) $G_1=0.367879+0.007907=0.375787$, $G_2=0.367879+0.236928=0.604807$, and $G_3=0.007907+0.236928=0.244835$. The per-atom energies are $\varepsilon(G_i)=-\tanh G_i$, that is, $(-0.359043,\ -0.540461,\ -0.240057)$ eV, and the total is $E=-1.139561$ eV.

(2) By the chain rule (Theorem 15.1), we take the sum over all the $G_i$ that $x_2$ affects.

$$ \begin{aligned} F_2&=-\pdiff{E}{x_2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon'(G_i)\,\pdiff{G_i}{x_2},\\ \varepsilon'(G)&=v\bigl(1-\tanh^{2}G\bigr). \end{aligned} $$

We have $\varepsilon'(G_1)=-0.871088$, $\varepsilon'(G_2)=-0.707901$, and $\varepsilon'(G_3)=-0.942373$ eV. Using $\pdiff{}{x_2}e^{-(x_i-x_2)^{2}}=2(x_i-x_2)e^{-(x_i-x_2)^{2}}$ ($i\ne2$), the partial derivatives of $G_i$ are

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{G_1}{x_2}&=2(0-1.0)\times0.367879\\ &=-0.735759,\\ \pdiff{G_3}{x_2}&=2(2.2-1.0)\times0.236928\\ &=0.568627,\\ \pdiff{G_2}{x_2}&=-2(1.0-0)\times0.367879\\ &\quad-2(1.0-2.2)\times0.236928\\ &=-0.735759+0.568627\\ &=-0.167132 \end{aligned} $$

(in units of Å$^{-1}$). The contributions $-\varepsilon'(G_i)\,\partial G_i/\partial x_2$ from the energy of each atom are $-0.640911$ from atom 1, $-0.118313$ from atom 2, and $+0.535858$ eV/Å from atom 3, so

$$ \begin{aligned} F_2&=-0.640911-0.118313+0.535858\\ &=-0.223366\ \mathrm{eV/Å}. \end{aligned} $$

Most of the force acting on atom 2 comes not from the energy of atom 2 itself but from the fact that the energies of the neighboring atoms depend on $x_2$. Similarly, $F_1=1.224848$ and $F_3=-1.001482$ eV/Å (in this model $v\lt0$, so the atoms attract each other, and the atoms at the two ends are pulled inward).

(3) Since $G_i$ is a function only of the differences $x_i-x_j$ between atoms, $E$ does not change when all the atoms are shifted by the same amount $c$: $E(x_1+c,x_2+c,x_3+c)=E(x_1,x_2,x_3)$. Differentiating both sides with respect to $c$ and setting $c=0$ gives $\sum_k\partial E/\partial x_k=0$, that is, $F_1+F_2+F_3=0$ (indeed $1.224848-0.223366-1.001482=0$). From an energy that is unchanged by translation, only internal forces, whose sum is 0, can arise. This is an example of the “invariance” treated in Chapter 16.

Exercise 15.7: Reading the learning curves of the sintering data

Solution

(1) We adopt the weights at epoch 1187, where the validation RMSE is smallest (1.50 %) (early stopping). Before that, both the training and the validation RMSE are decreasing and the training is insufficient; after that, only the training RMSE decreases while the validation RMSE rises (1.65 % at 2000 and 1.94 % at 5000), which is overfitting.

(2) The observations in data set A contain noise (standard deviation 1.0 %), so even if the true function itself were used for prediction, an RMSE of about 1 % should remain on the training data. The value 0.31 % is far smaller than that, which shows that the model is bending to fit the noise of the individual observations rather than the true function (it has memorized even the noise). With only 8 data points for 49 parameters, one can construct any number of curves that pass through the 8 points.

(3) It is not good. If dozens of choices are made on the validation data, the selected combination has also been fitted to the chance fluctuations of the validation data, so the validation RMSE is an optimistic estimate of the error on future data. Report the value obtained by evaluating only once, at the very end, on test data that were never used for any selection (Chapter 6, Section 6.3).

(4) Choose the stopping epoch and $\lambda$ by cross-validation (Chapter 6, Section 6.5), and finally retrain on all the data. Split the data so that samples from the same firing batch are not divided between training and validation (Chapter 6, Section 6.8). Also, with only 8 data points, first try models with few parameters (regression with basis functions in Chapter 8, Gaussian process regression in Chapter 12), and compare them on the validation data to check whether there is any advantage in using a neural network. Predictions outside the temperature range of the training data (1159–1473 °C) are treated as extrapolation for any model.

Exercise 15.8: Weight decay and MAP estimation

Solution

(1) By Bayes's theorem, $p(\bm{w}\mid\data)\propto p(\data\mid\bm{w})\,p(\bm{w})$. Since the observations are independent and Gaussian,

$$ p(\data\mid\bm{w})=\prod_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y_n-f_n)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr). $$

Here $f_n=f(\bm{x}_n;\bm{w})$.

The prior distribution is $p(\bm{w})\propto\exp\bigl(-\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}/(2\sigma_w^{2})\bigr)$. Taking the negative logarithm and collecting the terms that do not depend on $\bm{w}$ into $C$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} -\ln p(\bm{w}\mid\data)=&\ \frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac12(f_n-y_n)^{2}\\ &+\frac{1}{2\sigma_w^{2}}\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}+C . \end{aligned} $$

(2) Multiplying by the positive constant $\sigma^{2}/N$ does not change the $\bm{w}$ that minimizes the expression.

$$ \begin{aligned} &\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\bigl(-\ln p(\bm{w}\mid\data)-C\bigr)\\ &\quad=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac12(f_n-y_n)^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2N\sigma_w^{2}}\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}. \end{aligned} $$

This is the mean loss plus $\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\norm{\bm{w}}^{2}$, with $\lambda=\sigma^{2}/(N\sigma_w^{2})$. Therefore, minimizing the loss with weight decay is the same as MAP estimation under a Gaussian prior distribution. The more data there are (the larger $N$ is), the smaller the $\lambda$ of the mean convention becomes. The $\lambda'=\sigma^{2}/\sigma_w^{2}$ of the sum convention (the same correspondence as for Ridge regression in Chapter 8) does not depend on $N$. Not penalizing the bias corresponds to taking a uniform (very broad) prior distribution for the bias.

(3) In standardized units, the noise standard deviation is $1.0/7.15$ and the variance is $(1/7.15)^{2}=0.01955$. For $N=20$ and $\sigma_w=1$, $\lambda=0.01955/20=9.8\times10^{-4}$, which is nearly equal to the choice $10^{-3}$ among the options of Simulator 15.3. Note that the loss of a minibatch is the mean over the minibatch, so the strength of the penalty in one update is the same as for the mean loss over all the data, and this correspondence can be used as it is in minibatch training as well.

Chapter 16 Deep Learning That Exploits Structure — Images, Graphs, and Symmetry

Exercise 16.1: One-dimensional convolution and the output length

Solution

(1) Since the padding is “valid”, the window must lie entirely inside the input, which gives four outputs, for $t=1,2,3,4$. From $y_t=x_t-x_{t+1}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} y_1&=1-2=-1,\quad y_2=2-0=2,\\ y_3&=0-(-1)=1,\quad y_4=-1-3=-4 . \end{aligned} $$

So $\bm{y}=(-1,2,1,-4)$. This filter takes the difference from the neighbor, so its output is positive where the signal decreases toward the right and negative where it increases. In other words, it detects changes toward the right (steps and edges) together with their sign.

(2) The output length is $\lfloor(T+2P-k)/s\rfloor+1$.

  • (a) $\lfloor(100+4-5)/1\rfloor+1=99+1=100$.
  • (b) $\lfloor(100+4-5)/2\rfloor+1$ $=\lfloor49.5\rfloor+1=50$.
  • (c) $\lfloor(100+0-5)/1\rfloor+1=95+1=96$.

(3) For $s=1$ we have $T_{\mathrm{out}}=T+2P-k+1$, so the condition for $T_{\mathrm{out}}=T$ is $2P=k-1$, that is, $P=(k-1)/2$. For $k=5$ this gives $P=2$, and (a) satisfies exactly this condition. A window of width $k$ sticks out by $(k-1)/2$ entries on each side of its center, so if we pad that many zeros, the window can also be placed at the end positions.

Exercise 16.2: Counting the parameters of a small CNN

Solution

(1) A 3×3 convolution with “same” padding leaves the height and width unchanged, and 2×2 max pooling (stride 2) halves the height and width.

  • Input: 128×128×1 → convolution 1: 128×128×8 → pooling: 64×64×8
  • → convolution 2: 64×64×16 → pooling: 32×32×16
  • → global average: 16 → fully connected: 3 → softmax: 3

(2) We use Eq. (16.5) of Chapter 16, $N_{\mathrm{param}}=C_{\mathrm{out}}(k^{2}C_{\mathrm{in}}+1)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \text{Convolution 1}&:\ 8\times(9\times1+1)=80,\\ \text{Convolution 2}&:\ 16\times(9\times8+1)=1168,\\ \text{Fully connected}&:\ 3\times(16+1)=51 . \end{aligned} $$

Pooling and the global average have no parameters, so the total is $80+1168+51=1299$.

(3) There are $128\times128=16384$ pixels. Connecting them directly to the three classes takes $3\times(16384+1)=49155$ parameters. If we insert 100 hidden units, the first layer has $100\times(16384+1)=1\,638\,500$ parameters and the second layer has $3\times(100+1)=303$, for a total of $1\,638\,803$. The CNN of (2) has about $1/38$ and about $1/1262$ of these numbers of parameters, respectively. Moreover, even if the image size changes, the number of parameters in the convolutional part does not change (and, thanks to the global average, neither does the fully connected part).

Exercise 16.3: A linear map that commutes with the shift is a convolution

Solution

Below, all indices are counted as $0,\dots,T-1$ modulo $T$.

(1) The linear part of a convolution with periodic boundary is $y_t=\sum_{u=-r}^{r}w_ux_{t+u}$. Let $s=t+u$. The coefficient in $y_t=\sum_sC_{ts}x_s$ is $C_{ts}=w_u$ if there is a $u\in\{-r,\dots,r\}$ with $s-t\equiv u$ ($\bmod T$), and $0$ otherwise. This is determined by $s-t$ alone, so if we set $c_m=w_m$ ($0\le m\le r$), $c_{T-m}=w_{-m}$ ($1\le m\le r$), and $c_m=0$ otherwise, we can write $C_{ts}=c_{(s-t)\bmod T}$ (assuming $2r+1\le T$).

(2) From $(\bm{S}\bm{x})_t=x_{t-1}$, we have $S_{qs}=1$ only for $s=q-1$, that is, $q=s+1$, and the entry is 0 otherwise. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} (\bm{C}\bm{S})_{ts}&=\sum_qC_{tq}S_{qs}=C_{t,s+1}\\ &=c_{s+1-t},\\ (\bm{S}\bm{C})_{ts}&=\sum_qS_{tq}C_{qs}=C_{t-1,s}\\ &=c_{s-(t-1)}=c_{s+1-t}. \end{aligned} $$

The two are equal, so $\bm{C}\bm{S}=\bm{S}\bm{C}$.

(3) The computation in (2) gives $(\bm{C}\bm{S})_{ts}=C_{t,s+1}$ and $(\bm{S}\bm{C})_{ts}=C_{t-1,s}$ without using the form of $\bm{C}$. Hence if $\bm{C}\bm{S}=\bm{S}\bm{C}$, then $C_{t,s+1}=C_{t-1,s}$ for all $t,s$. Replacing $t$ by $t+1$ gives $C_{t+1,s+1}=C_{t,s}$; that is, the entries of $\bm{C}$ do not change as we move diagonally toward the lower right. Repeating this $t$ times gives

$$ C_{t,s}=C_{t-1,s-1}=\cdots=C_{0,\,s-t} $$

and if we set $c_m\equiv C_{0,m}$, then $C_{ts}=c_{(s-t)\bmod T}$, which is the form in (1) (a circulant matrix).

(4) Commuting with $\bm{S}$ means “shifting by one and then mapping gives the same result as mapping and then shifting”, which is the same as translation equivariance (since $\bm{S}^{\tau}=S_\tau$, a shift by one is enough). Therefore, under periodic boundary conditions, a translation-equivariant linear layer is necessarily a convolution with some filter. However, the filter width can be as large as $T$. That the filter is small (local) is an additional assumption, separate from translation equivariance.

Exercise 16.4: Magnification and the receptive field

Solution

(1) At the end of Example 16.4, $r=32$ and $j=4$. For 2×2 max pooling ($k=2$, $s=2$), $r=32+(2-1)\times4=36$ and $j=4\times2=8$. For the 3×3 convolution, $r=36+(3-1)\times8=52$. The receptive field is 52 pixels.

(2) At 0.1 μm/pixel, one grain is $5/0.1=50$ pixels, and the receptive field is $52\times0.1=5.2$ μm, about the size of one grain. At 0.5 μm/pixel, one grain is $5/0.5=10$ pixels, and the receptive field is $52\times0.5=26$ μm, a range that spans about five grains.

(3) A filter learned at 0.1 μm/pixel is fitted to the patterns of “grains about 50 pixels across” (how the grain boundaries curve, how uniform the brightness inside a grain is). In an image at 0.5 μm/pixel, a grain of the same microstructure is only 10 pixels across, which looks like an entirely different pattern to the filter, so the prediction cannot be trusted (a difference between the training and test distributions). Example countermeasures: (i) before predicting, enlarge or shrink the image so that the pixel size becomes 0.1 μm/pixel (however, enlarging a 0.5 μm/pixel image does not bring back the details that were lost, so it may be safer to match the training side to 0.5 μm/pixel). (ii) Include images at both magnifications in the training data, or apply data augmentation by enlarging and shrinking. (iii) Report the evaluation separately for each magnification, and check whether the model can be used on images at a different magnification.

Exercise 16.5: The graph of a simple cubic lattice

Solution

Taking the position of the atom as the origin, the periodic images lie at $a\bm{n}$ ($\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}$, $\bm{n}\ne\bm{0}$), at distance $a\norm{\bm{n}}$.

(1) The condition $a\norm{\bm{n}}\lt4.5$ Å, that is, $\norm{\bm{n}}\lt1.5$, is satisfied by $\norm{\bm{n}}=1$ (6 images such as $(\pm1,0,0)$, at distance 3.0 Å) and $\norm{\bm{n}}=\sqrt2\approx1.414$ (12 images such as $(\pm1,\pm1,0)$, at distance $3\sqrt2=4.243$ Å). $\norm{\bm{n}}=\sqrt3\approx1.732$ is not included. There are $6+12=18$ edges, of two lengths: 3.0 Å and 4.243 Å.

(2) Since $\norm{\bm{n}}\lt5.5/3=1.833$, $\norm{\bm{n}}=\sqrt3$ (8 images $(\pm1,\pm1,\pm1)$, at distance $3\sqrt3=5.196$ Å) is added, giving $18+8=26$ edges of three lengths. $\norm{\bm{n}}=2$ is 6.0 Å and is not included.

(3) The unit cell contains only one atom, so the partner of every edge is a periodic image of the atom itself (a self-loop). In a graph that places one edge for each pair of atoms inside the unit cell, there is no pair at all, so there are 0 edges and the graph cannot be distinguished from a single isolated atom. All the information on the coordination number (18 or 26), the nearest-neighbor distance, and the lattice constant is lost. We need a multigraph that distinguishes edges by periodic image.

(4) Even for a 2×2×2 supercell (6.0 Å on a side, 8 atoms), the arrangement of atoms around each atom is exactly the same, so the number of edges leaving each atom is still 18 ($r_{\mathrm c}=4.5$ Å). However, the partners are now other atoms in the supercell or their periodic images, and there can be two edges to the same partner (for example, from $(0,0,0)$ to $(3,0,0)$ there are two edges: to the atom itself and to its periodic image shifted by $-6$ Å). The features of all the atoms take the same values as in the original one-atom case, so the output of a mean readout does not change (with a sum readout it becomes 8 times larger).

Exercise 16.6: Aggregating by sum does not depend on the labeling

Solution

(1) We mark the quantities after relabeling with a tilde. The new vertex $i$ is the original vertex $\pi(i)$, so $\tilde{\bm{h}}_i=\bm{h}_{\pi(i)}$, the edge features are $\tilde{\bm{e}}_{ij}=\bm{e}_{\pi(i)\pi(j)}$, and the neighborhood satisfies “$\tilde{j}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(i)$ ⇔ $\pi(\tilde{j})\in\mathcal{A}(\pi(i))$”. The sum of messages at the new vertex $i$ is

$$ \begin{aligned} \tilde{\bm{m}}_i&=\sum_{\tilde{j}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(i)}M\bigl(\tilde{\bm{h}}_i,\tilde{\bm{h}}_{\tilde{j}},\tilde{\bm{e}}_{i\tilde{j}}\bigr)\\ &=\sum_{\tilde{j}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(i)}M\bigl(\bm{h}_{\pi(i)},\bm{h}_{\pi(\tilde{j})},\bm{e}_{\pi(i)\pi(\tilde{j})}\bigr). \end{aligned} $$

If we substitute $j=\pi(\tilde{j})$, then as $\tilde{j}$ runs over $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(i)$, $j$ runs over $\mathcal{A}(\pi(i))$ exactly once each ($\pi$ is one-to-one). A sum does not depend on the order of addition, so

$$ \tilde{\bm{m}}_i=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}(\pi(i))}M\bigl(\bm{h}_{\pi(i)},\bm{h}_j,\bm{e}_{\pi(i)j}\bigr)=\bm{m}_{\pi(i)} . $$

Therefore $\tilde{\bm{h}}'_i=U(\tilde{\bm{h}}_i,\tilde{\bm{m}}_i)=U(\bm{h}_{\pi(i)},\bm{m}_{\pi(i)})=\bm{h}'_{\pi(i)}$. After one step, “the feature of the new vertex $i$ equals the feature of the original vertex $\pi(i)$” still holds, so by repeating the same argument it holds after any number of steps (mathematical induction).

(2) We have $\sum_ig(\tilde{\bm{h}}_i)=\sum_ig(\bm{h}_{\pi(i)})$, and substituting $k=\pi(i)$, as $i$ runs over $1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{at}}$, $k$ also runs over each value exactly once, so the sum is unchanged: $=\sum_kg(\bm{h}_k)$. Multiplying by $R$ does not change this, so the readout is invariant. The mean only multiplies by $1/N_{\mathrm{at}}$, so it is also invariant.

(3) For the output not to change when atoms 1 and 2 are swapped, we need $w_1h_2+w_2h_1+w_3h_3=w_1h_1+w_2h_2+w_3h_3$ for all $h_1,h_2,h_3$, that is, $(w_1-w_2)(h_2-h_1)=0$. There are inputs with $h_1\ne h_2$, so $w_1=w_2$. Similarly, from swapping 1 and 3 and swapping 2 and 3, $w_1=w_3$. Therefore the output is invariant only when $w_1=w_2=w_3=w$, and in that case $\hat{y}=w(h_1+h_2+h_3)$ is exactly the sum readout. If the three atoms are of different elements and we only need to consider swaps between atoms of the same element, the only condition is that the weights of atoms of the same element are equal.

Exercise 16.7: Rotating the forces on two atoms

Solution

(1) The distance is $r=\sqrt{0.6^{2}+0.8^{2}}=1.0$ Å, which is shorter than $r_0=1.2$ Å, so the spring is compressed.

$$ \begin{aligned} E&=\tfrac{4}{2}(1.0-1.2)^{2}\\ &=2\times0.04=0.08\ \text{eV},\\ V'(r)&=k(r-r_0)=4\times(-0.2)=-0.8\ \text{eV/Å}. \end{aligned} $$

From Eq. (16.17) of Chapter 16 (the force from a pair potential),

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{F}_1&=V'(r)\frac{\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1}{r}\\ &=-0.8\times(0.6,0.8)\\ &=(-0.48,\,-0.64),\\ \bm{F}_2&=V'(r)\frac{\bm{r}_1-\bm{r}_2}{r}\\ &=(0.48,\,0.64) \end{aligned} $$

(in eV/Å). Since the spring is compressed, atom 1 is pushed away from atom 2, and atom 2 is also pushed away from atom 1.

(2) Rotating with $\bm{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ gives $\bm{r}'_1=(0,0)$ and $\bm{r}'_2=(-0.8,0.6)$. The distance remains 1.0 Å, so $E=0.08$ eV.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{F}'_1&=-0.8\times(-0.8,0.6)=(0.64,\,-0.48),\\ \bm{F}'_2&=(-0.64,\,0.48). \end{aligned} $$

On the other hand, $\bm{Q}\bm{F}_1=\bm{Q}(-0.48,-0.64)^{\tp}=(0.64,-0.48)^{\tp}$ and $\bm{Q}\bm{F}_2=(-0.64,0.48)^{\tp}$, which agree.

(3) Let the positions after the rotation and translation be $\bm{r}'_i=\bm{Q}\bm{r}_i+\bm{t}$. Then $\bm{r}'_2-\bm{r}'_1=\bm{Q}(\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1)$, and its length is $\norm{\bm{Q}(\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1)}=r$ (because $\bm{Q}^{\tp}\bm{Q}=\bm{I}$). Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{F}_1(\bm{\mathcal{R}}')&=V'(r)\frac{\bm{Q}(\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1)}{r}\\ &=\bm{Q}\Bigl(V'(r)\frac{\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1}{r}\Bigr)=\bm{Q}\bm{F}_1(\bm{\mathcal{R}}). \end{aligned} $$

The same holds for $\bm{F}_2$. The vector $\bm{t}$ cancels when we take the difference, so the forces do not change under translation. Also, $\bm{F}_1+\bm{F}_2$ is $V'(r)/r$ times $(\bm{r}_2-\bm{r}_1)+(\bm{r}_1-\bm{r}_2)=\bm{0}$, so it is $\bm{0}$. This confirms the two properties of Theorem 16.2 (rotation equivariance and zero net force).

Exercise 16.8: Designing a model that predicts the band gap

Solution

The following is one example of a solution.

(1) Input: the crystal structure (lattice vectors, and the positions and elements of the atoms in the unit cell). Convert it to the periodic multigraph of Chapter 16, Section 16.5, with the vertex features given by information on the element and the edge features by the distance (expanded in radial basis functions). Output: the band gap (a scalar, in eV, nonnegative). Using a softplus or similar in the output layer ensures that no negative values are produced. Invariance: invariant under translation, rotation, and permutation of atoms of the same element. In addition, the prediction should not depend on the choice of unit cell (the position of the origin, or whether it is a primitive cell, a conventional cell, or a supercell). Readout: the band gap is an intensive quantity that does not depend on the size of the crystal, so we use the mean. With a sum, simply entering the same crystal as a supercell would multiply the prediction by the number of atoms.

(2) Things that cause leakage in a random split: structures with the same composition but slightly different structures (polymorphs, slightly distorted structures), structures of the same type in which a chemically similar element is substituted (for example, substitution by an element of the same group), and duplicate data in which the same material was computed under different calculation conditions. If these are divided between training and test, the test becomes a problem of “guessing something almost identical”. Countermeasures: put all structures of the same composition on the same side (split by composition), split by groups according to the combination of elements (chemical system) or the structure type, or send everything containing a particular element to the test set (evaluating extrapolation to unknown elements).

(3) Values from first-principles calculations inherit the properties of the approximation used. For example, in density functional theory calculations with the widely used local density approximation (LDA), it is known that the band gaps of semiconductors and insulators come out smaller than the experimental values (J. P. Perdew, M. Levy, Phys. Rev. Lett. 51, 1884 (1983)). The model learns not the experimental values but “the values of that calculation method”. If we want to predict experimental values, we need, for example, to relearn the deviation from the computed values using a small number of experimental values (learning the difference; Chapter 27, Section 27.8), or to evaluate the correspondence between computed and experimental values separately on the test set. Also, if the data contain many metals (gap 0), the mean error looks small, so we also report the error on the nonzero cases only.

(4) If the new element has never appeared in the training data, the feature (embedding) of that element has not been learned, and the prediction has no basis. Ways to check: count how many entries in the training data contain that element or similar elements; estimate the magnitude of the extrapolation error with a test in which a particular element is left out of training and evaluation; use the spread of the predictions of several models trained with different random-number seeds as a rough measure of uncertainty; and send only promising candidates to experiment after checking them with first-principles calculations.

Chapter 17 Clustering, Mixture Distributions, and the EM Algorithm — Estimating Unseen Labels

Exercise 17.1: k-means with $K=3$ and how $J$ decreases

Solution

(1) Assignment (representatives $1,6,11$): the point $x=1$ is at distance 0 from 1, and $x=2$ is at distance 1 from 1 and 4 from 6, so it goes to cluster 1. The point $x=6$ is at distance 0 from 6, so it goes to cluster 2. The point $x=9$ is at distance 3 from 6 and 2 from 11, so it goes to cluster 3. The points $x=10,11$ also go to cluster 3. Update: $\mu_1=(1+2)/2=1.5$, $\mu_2=6$, $\mu_3=(9+10+11)/3=10$. Assignment: $x=2$ is at distance 0.5 from 1.5 and 4 from 6, $x=6$ is at distance 0 from 6, and $x=9$ is at distance 3 from 6 and 1 from 10, so the assignment does not change. At convergence,

$$ \begin{aligned} J&=(0.5^{2}+0.5^{2})+0^{2}+(1^{2}+0^{2}+1^{2})\\ &=0.5+0+2=2.5\ \mu\mathrm{m}^{2}. \end{aligned} $$

(2) For $K=1$: the representative is the mean of all the data, $(1+2+6+9+10+11)/6=39/6=6.5$, and

$$ \begin{aligned} J^{*}_1&=5.5^{2}+4.5^{2}+0.5^{2}\\ &\quad+2.5^{2}+3.5^{2}+4.5^{2}\\ &=30.25+20.25+0.25\\ &\quad+6.25+12.25+20.25\\ &=89.5 . \end{aligned} $$

For $K=2$: as found in Example 17.3, $J^{*}_2=14.5$. For $K=3$: in one dimension it suffices to examine the ways of splitting the points into three consecutive groups ($\binom{5}{2}=10$ ways). From the smallest, $\{1,2\}\{6\}\{9,10,11\}$ gives $0.5+0+2=2.5$, $\{1,2\}\{6,9\}\{10,11\}$ gives $0.5+4.5+0.5=5.5$, $\{1,2\}\{6,9,10\}\{11\}$ gives $0.5+8.67+0=9.17$, and $\{1\}\{2,6\}\{9,10,11\}$ gives $0+8+2=10$; the others are larger still. Thus $J^{*}_3=2.5$, and the answer to (1) is the optimal solution.

(3) $J^{*}_K$ decreases as $89.5\to14.5\to2.5$. In terms of differences, the drop is 75 from $K=1\to2$ and 12 from $2\to3$, so there seems to be an elbow at $K=2$. In terms of ratios, however, both steps reduce it to about $1/6$, and the elbow is not clear. For $K=3$, the point $x=6$ forms a cluster by itself. Whether this is a “third group” or an “outlier” cannot be decided from six data points. The judgment of an elbow also depends on whether we look at $J$ through differences or through ratios, and it is especially risky when there are few data.

Exercise 17.2: A Gaussian mixture with a variance shared by all components

Solution

(1) The log-likelihood is $\ln p=\sum_n\ln\sum_k\pi_k\Normal(x_n\mid\mu_k,\sigma^{2})$, and $\sigma^{2}$ enters every component. As in Chapter 17, Section 17.5.2, we use $\partial f/\partial\sigma^{2}=f\,\partial\ln f/\partial\sigma^{2}$ to obtain

$$ \pdiff{\ln p}{\sigma^{2}}=\sum_{n}\sum_{k}\gamma_{nk}\,\pdiff{\ln\Normal(x_n\mid\mu_k,\sigma^{2})}{\sigma^{2}} . $$

Since $\ln\Normal(x\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})=-\tfrac12\ln2\pi$ $-\tfrac12\ln\sigma^{2}-\dfrac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}$, the derivative with respect to $\sigma^{2}$ is $-\dfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}+\dfrac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{4}}$. Substituting and using $\sum_k\gamma_{nk}=1$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} \pdiff{\ln p}{\sigma^{2}}&=-\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\\ &\quad+\frac{1}{2\sigma^{4}}\sum_n\sum_k\gamma_{nk}(x_n-\mu_k)^{2}=0 . \end{aligned} $$

Multiplying both sides by $2\sigma^{4}$ and solving for $\sigma^{2}$ gives

$$ \sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\gamma_{nk}(x_n-\mu_k)^{2}. $$

The update formulas for $\mu_k$ and $\pi_k$ are the same as in the case of component-wise variances (in the stationarity condition for $\mu_k$, $\sigma^{2}$ comes out as a common factor and cancels).

(2) In Example 17.7, the weighted sums of squares around the new means were $\sum_n\gamma_{n1}(x_n-\mu_1)^{2}=1.92348$ and $\sum_n\gamma_{n2}(x_n-\mu_2)^{2}=11.71107$. Therefore

$$ \begin{aligned} \sigma^{2}&=\frac{1.92348+11.71107}{4}\\ &=\frac{13.63455}{4}=3.40864 . \end{aligned} $$

This is the same value as the average $(1.60126\times1.20123+2.39874\times4.88218)/4$ of the component-wise values 1.20123 and 4.88218, weighted by the effective numbers of points $N_1=1.60126$ and $N_2=2.39874$.

(3) Degeneration does not occur. For the variance to shrink onto a single point we would need $\sigma^{2}\to0$, but since the variance is shared, all components shrink at the same time. If the number of distinct values in the data is larger than $K$, there is always at least one point at a distance $\delta\gt0$ or more from every mean, and the density at that point tends to 0 as $\sigma^{2}\to0$, like $e^{-\delta^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}/\sigma$. The logarithm of this factor, $-\delta^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})-\ln\sigma$, tends to $-\infty$ far faster than the term in which a mean sits on a single point increases through $-\ln\sigma$, so the log-likelihood does not diverge.

Exercise 17.3: The EM algorithm with a prior distribution (MAP estimation)

Solution

In the decomposition for the EM algorithm, consider $\ln p(\bm{X}\mid\bm{\theta})+\ln p(\bm{\theta})$ in place of $\ln p(\bm{X}\mid\bm{\theta})$. Since $\ln p(\bm{\theta})$ does not depend on $\bm{Z}$, the E step is unchanged, and the M step becomes the maximization of $\mathcal{Q}+\ln p(\bm{\theta})$. The log posterior distribution also does not decrease over the iterations.

(1) In $\mathcal{Q}$ of Chapter 17, Section 17.7.3, write the logarithm of the Gaussian distribution of component $k$ with precision $\tau_k=1/\sigma_k^{2}$: $\ln\Normal(x\mid\mu_k,1/\tau_k)=-\tfrac12\ln2\pi$ $+\tfrac12\ln\tau_k-\tfrac12\tau_k(x-\mu_k)^{2}$. Collecting the terms that contain $\tau_k$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} F(\tau_k)&=\sum_n\gamma_{nk}\Bigl[\tfrac12\ln\tau_k-\tfrac12\tau_k(x_n-\mu_k)^{2}\Bigr]\\ &\quad+(a-1)\ln\tau_k-b\tau_k\\ &=\Bigl(\tfrac{N_k}{2}+a-1\Bigr)\ln\tau_k\\ &\quad-\Bigl(\tfrac{S_k}{2}+b\Bigr)\tau_k . \end{aligned} $$

Differentiating and setting the result to 0 gives

$$ \frac{N_k/2+a-1}{\tau_k}-\frac{S_k}{2}-b=0 . $$

Multiplying both sides by $2\tau_k$ and solving for $\tau_k$ gives

$$ \tau_k=\frac{N_k+2a-2}{S_k+2b},\qquad \sigma_k^{2}=\frac{S_k+2b}{N_k+2a-2}. $$

Since $F''(\tau_k)=-(N_k/2+a-1)/\tau_k^{2}\lt0$ (for $a\gt1$), this is a maximum point. Because $\mu_k$ does not appear in the prior distribution, its update formula remains the weighted average ($S_k$ is computed with this new $\mu_k$). With $a=1$ and $b=0$ we recover the formula for maximum likelihood estimation.

(2) The terms containing $\pi_k$ are $\sum_k N_k\ln\pi_k+(\alpha-1)\sum_k\ln\pi_k$ $=\sum_k(N_k+\alpha-1)\ln\pi_k$. Under the constraint $\sum_k\pi_k=1$, differentiate the Lagrangian $\Lambda=\sum_k(N_k+\alpha-1)\ln\pi_k+\nu\bigl(\sum_k\pi_k-1\bigr)$ with respect to $\pi_k$ and set the result to 0:

$$ \frac{N_k+\alpha-1}{\pi_k}+\nu=0, $$

that is, $\pi_k=-(N_k+\alpha-1)/\nu$.

Summing over $k$ gives $1=-\bigl(N+K(\alpha-1)\bigr)/\nu$, hence $\nu=-\bigl(N+K(\alpha-1)\bigr)$, and

$$ \pi_k=\frac{N_k+\alpha-1}{N+K(\alpha-1)} . $$

If $\alpha\gt1$, then $\pi_k\gt0$ remains even for a component that is responsible for no point ($N_k=0$). If $\alpha=1$ (a uniform prior distribution), we recover the maximum likelihood value $N_k/N$.

(3) $\sigma_k^{2}=(0+2\times0.1)/(1+2\times2-2)$ $=0.2/3=0.0667$. In general, since $S_k\ge0$, we have $\sigma_k^{2}\ge2b/(N_k+2a-2)\gt0$, so the variance never becomes 0. In the log posterior distribution, the term $-b\tau_k$ tends to $-\infty$ as $\tau_k\to\infty$ ($\sigma_k\to0$) and cancels the increase of $\tfrac12\ln\tau_k$ due to the density at a single point, so degeneration does not occur. This can be read as “the prior distribution adds virtual data (a sum of squares $2b$ and a count of $2a-2$ points) to each component.”

Exercise 17.4: Identifying grains with abnormal grain growth

Solution

(1) $u=\ln4=1.38629$. The standardized distances are $z_1=(1.38629-0.718)/0.282=2.36984$ and $z_2=(1.38629-2.122)/0.255=-2.88512$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \pi_1\Normal(u\mid\mu_1,\sigma_1^{2})&=0.866\times\frac{e^{-2.36984^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\times0.282}\\ &=0.866\times\frac{0.060322}{0.70687}\\ &=0.073902,\\ \pi_2\Normal(u\mid\mu_2,\sigma_2^{2})&=0.134\times\frac{e^{-2.88512^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\times0.255}\\ &=0.134\times\frac{0.015577}{0.63919}\\ &=0.0032656 . \end{aligned} $$

$\gamma_2=0.0032656/(0.073902+0.0032656)$ $=0.0423$. A grain with diameter 4 μm is assigned only 4 % responsibility by the component of large grains.

(2) We solve $\ln\bigl[\pi_1\Normal(u\mid\mu_1,\sigma_1^{2})\bigr]=\ln\bigl[\pi_2\Normal(u\mid\mu_2,\sigma_2^{2})\bigr]$, that is,

$$ \ln\frac{\pi_1}{\sigma_1}-\frac{(u-\mu_1)^{2}}{2\sigma_1^{2}}=\ln\frac{\pi_2}{\sigma_2}-\frac{(u-\mu_2)^{2}}{2\sigma_2^{2}} $$

($\sqrt{2\pi}$ cancels on both sides). Expanding into the form $Au^{2}+Bu+C=0$ gives

$$ \begin{aligned} A&=\frac{1}{2\sigma_2^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sigma_1^{2}}\\ &=7.68935-6.28741=1.40194,\\ B&=\frac{\mu_1}{\sigma_1^{2}}-\frac{\mu_2}{\sigma_2^{2}}\\ &=9.02872-32.63360=-23.60488,\\ C&=\frac{\mu_2^{2}}{2\sigma_2^{2}}-\frac{\mu_1^{2}}{2\sigma_1^{2}}+\ln\frac{\pi_1\sigma_2}{\pi_2\sigma_1}\\ &=34.62425-3.24131+1.76540\\ &=33.14834 . \end{aligned} $$

By the quadratic formula,

$$ \begin{aligned} u&=\frac{23.60488\pm\sqrt{557.19-185.89}}{2.80388}\\ &=\frac{23.60488\pm19.26921}{2.80388}, \end{aligned} $$

that is, $u=1.5463$ and $u=15.291$. The former corresponds to $d=e^{1.5463}=4.69\ \mu\mathrm{m}$; for grains larger than this, the responsibility of component 2 exceeds 0.5. The latter ($d\approx4\times10^{6}\ \mu\mathrm{m}$) is a solution that appears because the tail of component 1, which has the larger variance, wins again far away, and it has no physical meaning (when the variances differ, the boundary is the solution of a quadratic equation, and two of them can appear).

(3) In a converged EM algorithm, $\pi_2=N_2/N$, so $N_2=0.134\times300=40.2$. Of the 45 “large grains” used in the generation, 6 are pulled toward small values, with $u\lt1.546$ ($d\lt4.7\ \mu\mathrm{m}$), and are mixed into the peak of small grains. The Gaussian mixture judges “a value like this looks like the component of small grains,” so these are assigned to component 1. The count obtained from the responsibilities is not the number by origin itself but an estimate seen from the values.

(4) What the mixture model shows is only that “the distribution of the logarithm of the grain size has two peaks.” To call it abnormal grain growth, we need to confirm, through microstructure observation, things such as the shape of the large grains (straight grain boundaries, facets), how the fraction and the diameter of the large grains change with sintering time, the presence of compositional segregation or a second phase, and differences depending on the position of the observed cross section (the diameter seen on a cross section appears smaller than the three-dimensional diameter). A similar second peak can also arise from lumps of agglomerated powder, or from a measurement error in which two grains are counted as one because a grain boundary was overlooked.

Exercise 17.5: Patterns in a two-phase region lie on a line segment

Solution

(1) Since $\bm{y}(f)=\bm{a}+f(\bm{b}-\bm{a})$, we have $\bm{y}(f)-\bm{y}(f')=(f-f')(\bm{b}-\bm{a})$. From the property of the norm $\norm{c\bm{v}}=\abs{c}\norm{\bm{v}}$, we get $\norm{\bm{y}(f)-\bm{y}(f')}=\abs{f-f'}\norm{\bm{a}-\bm{b}}$. The patterns lie on the line segment $\bm{a}\to\bm{b}$ in $\R^{D}$, at positions proportional to $f$.

(2) By (1), the $J$ of k-means is the same as that of k-means on the one-dimensional values $t=f\norm{\bm{a}-\bm{b}}$, and $J$ is $\norm{\bm{a}-\bm{b}}^{2}$ times the sum of squares of $f$. For the five ways of splitting into the smallest $s$ points and the rest, we compute the sum of squares of $f$ (around the mean of each group).

  • $s=1$: $\{0\}$ gives 0, and $\{0.2,\dots,1\}$ gives $0.16+0.04+0+0.04+0.16=0.40$ around the mean 0.6. The total is 0.40.
  • $s=2$: $\{0,0.2\}$ gives $0.02$, and $\{0.4,\dots,1\}$ gives $0.09+0.01+0.01+0.09=0.20$ around the mean 0.7. The total is 0.22.
  • $s=3$: $\{0,0.2,0.4\}$ gives $0.04+0+0.04=0.08$ around the mean 0.2, and the rest gives 0.08 in the same way. The total is 0.16.
  • $s=4,5$: by symmetry, 0.22 and 0.40.

The minimum is at $s=3$, with $J=0.16\norm{\bm{a}-\bm{b}}^{2}$. The clusters split into $f\le0.4$ and $f\ge0.6$. Since $x=0.35+0.30f$, $f=0.4$ corresponds to $x=0.47$ and $f=0.6$ to $x=0.53$, so the boundary falls at the middle of the two-phase region, $x\approx0.50$.

(3) There is no phase boundary at $x=0.50$. In a two-phase region the peak positions do not move; only the ratio of the amounts of the two phases changes smoothly. Since k-means can express this continuous change only in the language of “discrete groups,” it creates an artificial boundary at a position that divides the line segment equally. Reading from this that “there is a phase boundary at $x=0.5$” would be an error. To represent a two-phase region correctly, it is better to use a model that expresses each pattern as “a sum, with nonnegative weights, of the end patterns (patterns of the pure phases)” (linear mixing, nonnegative matrix factorization, and so on), together with the physical constraint that the weights change linearly with the composition (the lever rule). Note also that if each pattern is normalized to length 1, the points lie not on a line segment but on a circular arc; however, the order in which they lie does not change, so the conclusion is the same.

Exercise 17.6: BIC and AIC

Solution

(1) $P=3K-1=2,5,8,11$, and $-2\ln\hat{L}=496.34,\ 311.08,\ 299.16,\ 290.90$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{BIC}_1&=496.34+2\times5.7038=507.75,\\ \mathrm{BIC}_2&=311.08+5\times5.7038=339.60,\\ \mathrm{BIC}_3&=299.16+8\times5.7038=344.79,\\ \mathrm{BIC}_4&=290.90+11\times5.7038=353.64 . \end{aligned} $$

BIC is smallest at $K=2$.
(2) For AIC the penalty is 2 per parameter.

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{AIC}_1&=496.34+4=500.34,\\ \mathrm{AIC}_2&=311.08+10=321.08,\\ \mathrm{AIC}_3&=299.16+16=315.16,\\ \mathrm{AIC}_4&=290.90+22=312.90 . \end{aligned} $$

Among $K=1,\dots,4$, AIC is smallest at $K=4$, and it keeps decreasing as $K$ increases. BIC and AIC chose different values of $K$.
(3) The difference is the size of the penalty. BIC imposes $\ln N$ (here 5.70) per parameter, and AIC imposes 2. If $N\ge8$, then $\ln N\gt2$, so BIC tends to choose fewer components. The aims also differ. BIC is an approximation to the marginal likelihood of the model and aims at “identifying the model that generated the data.” AIC estimates the expectation of the log-likelihood of predictions for new data and aims at “a model with good prediction.” In this data set, the additional components at $K=3,4$ are narrow components fitted to a small number of points, with mixing coefficients of about 0.02 and standard deviations of about 0.04, and it is hard to interpret them as groups of grains. Whichever criterion is used, one should check what the added components represent.

Exercise 17.7: Adding components always improves the data fit

Solution

(1) Let the maximum point for $K$ components be $\bm{\theta}^{*}_K=\{\pi_k,\bm{\mu}_k,\bm{\Sigma}_k\}_{k=1}^{K}$. Add to it a component with mixing coefficient $\pi_{K+1}=0$ (its mean and covariance may be anything), and let $\bm{\theta}'$ be the resulting parameters of $K+1$ components. Then for every $\bm{x}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\bm{x}\mid\bm{\theta}')&=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_k\Normal(\bm{x}\mid\bm{\mu}_k,\bm{\Sigma}_k)+0\\ &=p(\bm{x}\mid\bm{\theta}^{*}_K) \end{aligned} $$

so the log-likelihood is the same. The maximum for $K+1$ components is the maximum over all parameters of $K+1$ components, including $\bm{\theta}'$, so $\ln\hat{L}_{K+1}\ge\ln p(\bm{X}\mid\bm{\theta}')=\ln\hat{L}_K$. (If $\pi_k\gt0$ is required, set $\pi_{K+1}=\varepsilon$, multiply the other $\pi_k$ by $1-\varepsilon$, and let $\varepsilon\to0$; the log-likelihood then approaches $\ln\hat{L}_K$ arbitrarily closely, so the same conclusion is obtained for the supremum.) Under the same variance floor, the same argument holds if the covariance of the added component is chosen to satisfy the floor.

(2) To the optimal set $\{\bm{\mu}_k\}$ of $K$ representatives, add an arbitrary single point $\bm{\mu}_{K+1}$. If we reassign each point to the nearest of the $K+1$ representatives, the “squared distance to the nearest representative” of each point does not increase, because only the candidates have increased. Therefore $J$ for these $K+1$ representatives is at most $J^{*}_K$, and its minimum $J^{*}_{K+1}$ is also at most $J^{*}_K$. If $K=N$ and all points are distinct, making each point a cluster with itself as the representative gives all distances 0, so $J^{*}_N=0$.

(3) Parts (1) and (2) show that the data fit to the training data (the log-likelihood and $J$) always improves merely by making the model more complex. This is the same structure as in Chapter 6, where the training error kept decreasing as the polynomial degree was raised, and became 0 for a polynomial passing through $N$ points. If $K$ is chosen by the data fit to the training data, the largest $K$ (or $K=N$) is chosen, and the result is an “overfitted” model tuned to the accidental positions of individual points. The number of components must be chosen by a criterion that penalizes complexity (such as BIC), by the log-likelihood on validation data (cross-validation), or by the physical interpretation of the components.
