(23.23) corresponds to an RNN whose hidden state $h_k=\Theta_k$ is one-dimensional, with $W_{hh}=1$, an identity activation (not passed through tanh), and an input given by the temperature $T_k$ passed through the nonlinear function $\phi(T_k)=\Delta t\,T_k^{-1}\exp\{-Q/(RT_k)\}$ (we add $\phi(T_k)$ in place of $\bm{W}_{xh}\bm{x}_t$). The bias is 0. To perform the same computation with the LSTM of Eq. (23.17), it suffices to set the forget gate to $f_k=1$ (keep all of the previous value), the input gate to $i_k=1$, and the candidate to $\tilde{c}_k=\phi(T_k)$. However, since $\tilde{c}_k$ is the output of a tanh, the scale of $\Theta$ must be chosen so that it fits in $(-1,1)$.

(2) As in Example 23.10, the ratio is $\dfrac{T_1}{T_2}\exp\!\left[\dfrac{Q}{R}\Bigl(\dfrac{1}{T_1}-\dfrac{1}{T_2}\Bigr)\right]$, with $T_1=1673.15\ \mathrm{K}$ and $T_2=1723.15\ \mathrm{K}$. We have $1/T_1-1/T_2=5.9767\times10^{-4}-5.8033\times10^{-4}$ $=1.7341\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$ and $Q/R=57734\ \mathrm{K}$, so the exponent is $1.0012$ and $e^{1.0012}=2.7215$. Multiplying by $T_1/T_2=0.97098$ gives a ratio of $2.643$. This corresponds to 30 minutes × 2.643 = 79.3 minutes.

(3) $0.99^{600}=\exp(600\ln0.99)$ $=\exp(600\times(-0.010050))$ $=\exp(-6.030)=0.0024$. Only 0.24 % of the contribution from 600 steps earlier remains. On the other hand, $0.999^{600}=\exp(600\times(-0.0010005))$ $=\exp(-0.6003)=0.549$, so about half remains. To represent the accumulated $\Theta$ correctly, the factor must be almost exactly 1, and it is hard for a tanh RNN to maintain this.

(4) Examples: (i) Do not split fragments of the same run's record, cut by time, between training and validation (split by run, and further by raw-material lot or furnace; Chapter 6, Section 6.8). (ii) When predicting partway through a run, do not use temperatures after the time being predicted as inputs or features (including statistics such as the mean or standardization statistics computed over the whole interval) (leakage in time series, Chapter 22). (iii) When the temperature conditions of a validation run lie outside the training range, state explicitly that this is extrapolation.

Exercise 23.7: Another way to write the same molecule

Solution

(1) Both C=Cc1ccccc1 and c1ccccc1C=C have 11 characters, so the number of tokens is 11 in both. In the second, the 1 that opens the ring is the second token and the 1 that closes it is the eighth, so they are six tokens apart (in the first as well, they are the fifth and 11th, six tokens apart).

(2) Even though the strings differ, they represent the same molecule, so the property value of a test-set molecule is already contained in the training data as is. If the model learns that these are the same molecule despite the difference in strings, the test error measures “recall of a known molecule” rather than “prediction for an unknown molecule”, and the generalization performance is overestimated (data leakage; Chapter 6, Section 6.7). To prevent this, before splitting, convert all molecules to a unique representation (canonicalized SMILES) or remove duplicates by molecular identifier, and then split into training and test sets by molecule.

(3) Another way of writing the same molecule is an “input transformation” that does not change the property value, so adding such variants to the training data amounts to data augmentation that teaches the model to “return the same answer regardless of how the molecule is written” (the same idea as the augmentation that rotates an image, since the object pictured is the same). However, because of the caution in (2), the augmentation is done after the split, and only within the training data.

(4) Examples: (i) Even a grammatically valid SMILES is not a new candidate if it is a copy of a molecule in the training data (novelty must be checked separately). (ii) That a molecule can exist as a molecule is distinct from its being actually synthesizable and from its being stable. (iii) Whether it has the target property must be confirmed by a prediction separate from the generative model, or by experiment or calculation, and that prediction too cannot be trusted outside the training range.

Exercise 23.8: The bottleneck and rereading

Solution

(1) As in Example 23.8, $c=h_{20}=\sum_{t=1}^{20}a^{20-t}x_t$, so the coefficient of $x_1$ is $a^{19}=0.8^{19}=0.0144$. This is only 1.4 % of the coefficient 1 of the last input.

(2) If the scores are $(3,0,0,0)$, then $A_{i1}=\dfrac{e^{3}}{e^{3}+3e^{0}}=\dfrac{20.086}{23.086}=0.870$ (each of the rest is $1/23.086=0.0433$). When all the scores are equal, the numerators of Eq. (23.21) are all the same, so $A_{ij}=1/4=0.25$, and the context is a simple average of the four states. In either case, the information at position 1 arrives with a single weight, without passing through the stages of the encoder. The higher the score of a position, the more strongly it is read.

(3) In an RNN, computing $\bm{h}_t$ requires $\bm{h}_{t-1}$, so the 512 computations must be performed in sequence. In self-attention, the computation at each position does not wait for the results of the others, so the number of sequential computations is one (all positions are computed at once as a matrix product). On a device such as a GPU that can perform many operations simultaneously, the fewer the sequential waits, the faster a single training step finishes, and the more data can be learned in the same time. However, the computational cost of self-attention grows in proportion to $T^{2}$ (Table 23.3).

← Back to Chapter 23, “RNNs, LSTMs, and Sequence Representations — Networks with Memory”

Chapter 24 How Attention Works — Gathering Relevant Information with Weights

Exercise 24.1: Attention with two queries

Solution

(1) $\bm{Q}$ is $2\times2$, $\bm{K}$ is $3\times2$, and $\bm{V}$ is $3\times1$. Hence $T_q=2$, $T_k=3$, $d_k=2$, and $d_v=1$. The scores and the weights are $2\times3$, and the output is $2\times1$.

(2) The matrix of dot products is

$$ \begin{gathered} \bm{Q}\bm{K}^{\tp}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\0&2&2\end{pmatrix},\\ \bm{S}=\frac{\bm{Q}\bm{K}^{\tp}}{\sqrt2}=\begin{pmatrix}0.7071&0&0.7071\\0&1.4142&1.4142\end{pmatrix}. \end{gathered} $$

Row 1: $\exp$ gives $(2.0281,\ 1,\ 2.0281)$, the sum is $5.0562$, and the weights are $(0.4011,\ 0.1978,\ 0.4011)$. Row 2: $\exp$ gives $(1,\ 4.1133,\ 4.1133)$, the sum is $9.2265$, and the weights are $(0.1084,\ 0.4458,\ 0.4458)$. The output is

$$ \begin{aligned} z_1&=0.4011\times1+0.1978\times2\\ &\quad+0.4011\times3=2.0000,\\ z_2&=0.1084\times1+0.4458\times2\\ &\quad+0.4458\times3=2.3374 . \end{aligned} $$

The sum of each row is $1.0000$.

(3) It is not a coincidence. In row 1 we have $A_{11}=A_{13}$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} z_1&=A_{11}(v_1+v_3)+A_{12}v_2\\ &=A_{11}\times4+A_{12}\times2=2\,(2A_{11}+A_{12}). \end{aligned} $$

Since the row sums to 1, $2A_{11}+A_{12}=1$, and therefore $z_1=2$ regardless of the values of the weights. This is due to the symmetry that the mean of value 1 and value 3 equals value 2, and that key 1 and key 3 receive equal weights. In row 2 the weights lean toward key 2 and key 3, so the output is $2.3374$, larger than the mean 2.

Exercise 24.2: Properties of softmax and the mask

Solution

(1) If $s_j\to s_j+c$, then

$$ \frac{e^{s_j+c}}{\sum_{j'}e^{s_{j'}+c}}=\frac{e^{c}e^{s_j}}{e^{c}\sum_{j'}e^{s_{j'}}}=\frac{e^{s_j}}{\sum_{j'}e^{s_{j'}}} $$

so the result does not change.

(2) If all the keys are equal, $\bm{k}_j=\bm{k}$, then for any query $\bm{q}$ the score $s_j=\bm{q}^{\tp}\bm{k}/\sqrt{d_k}$ takes the same value $s$ for every $j$. Then $a_j=e^{s}/(T_ke^{s})=1/T_k$, so the output is $\bm{z}=\frac{1}{T_k}\sum_j\bm{v}_j$ (the simple mean of the values), which does not depend on the query.

(3) If $s_j\to s_j+b_j$, then

$$ a'_j=\frac{e^{s_j}e^{b_j}}{\sum_{j'}e^{s_{j'}}e^{b_{j'}}}=\frac{a_je^{b_j}}{\sum_{j'}a_{j'}e^{b_{j'}}} $$

(for the second equality, we divided the numerator and the denominator by $\sum_{j'}e^{s_{j'}}$). The new weights are obtained by multiplying the original weights by $e^{b_j}$ and then renormalizing so that they sum to 1. Unlike in (1), the weights do change when $b_j$ differs from column to column. The causal mask is the case $b_j=0$ ($j\le i$), $b_j=-\infty$ ($j\gt i$): multiplying by $e^{-\infty}=0$ sets the weights of the positions that must not be seen to 0, and the remaining weights are renormalized.

(4) Let $s_{\max}$ be the largest score among the unmasked positions in the row (at least $j=i$ remains). The ratio of the $\exp$ value at a masked position to $e^{s_{\max}}$ is $e^{s_{ij}-10^{9}-s_{\max}}\le e^{20-10^{9}}$ (since $|s|\le10$, we have $s_{ij}-s_{\max}\le20$), and the weight is at most this. The number $e^{-10^{9}}$ is so small that it is rounded to 0 even in 64-bit floating-point arithmetic, so the result is effectively the same as with $-\infty$.

Exercise 24.3: Variance of the dot product and the Jacobian matrix of softmax

Solution

(1) For each term, independence gives $\Ex[q_lk_l]=\Ex[q_l]\Ex[k_l]=0$ and $\Ex[q_l^{2}k_l^{2}]=\Ex[q_l^{2}]\Ex[k_l^{2}]=\sigma_q^{2}\sigma_k^{2}$ (since the mean is 0, $\Ex[q_l^{2}]=\sigma_q^{2}$). Hence $\Var[q_lk_l]=\sigma_q^{2}\sigma_k^{2}-0^{2}=\sigma_q^{2}\sigma_k^{2}$. Terms with different $l$ are independent, so the variances add:

$$ \Var\bigl[\bm{q}^{\tp}\bm{k}\bigr]=\sum_{l=1}^{d_k}\sigma_q^{2}\sigma_k^{2}=d_k\sigma_q^{2}\sigma_k^{2}. $$

With $\sigma_q=\sigma_k=1$ we recover Theorem 24.1 of Chapter 24.

(2) The $(j,l)$ component of the Jacobian matrix is $J_{jl}=\delta_{jl}a_j-a_ja_l$. The sum of the $j$th row is

$$ \sum_lJ_{jl}=a_j-a_j\sum_la_l=a_j-a_j\times1=0 . $$

Since $J$ is symmetric ($J_{jl}=J_{lj}$), the column sums are also 0. Meaning: for the change $\Delta\bm{s}=\varepsilon(1,\dots,1)^{\tp}$ that increases all the scores by the same amount $\varepsilon$, the change in the weights is $J\Delta\bm{s}=\bm{0}$. This is the differential version of the statement in Exercise 24.2 (1) that “adding the same number to all components does not change the softmax.”

(3) The diagonal components $a_j(1-a_j)$ are

$$ \begin{aligned} J_{11}&=(1-\varepsilon)\,\varepsilon=\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}\approx\varepsilon,\\ J_{22}=J_{33}&=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)\approx\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \end{aligned} $$

The off-diagonal components are $-a_ja_l$, for example $-(1-\varepsilon)\varepsilon/2\approx-\varepsilon/2$ and $-\varepsilon^{2}/4$, all of which have magnitude of order $\varepsilon$ or smaller. As $\varepsilon\to0$ (the weights concentrate on a single position), the whole Jacobian matrix approaches 0, so moving the scores hardly changes the weights. This is why gradients do not flow when the softmax is saturated.

Exercise 24.4: Number of parameters of multi-head attention

Solution

(1) $\bm{W}_Q^{(j)},\bm{W}_K^{(j)},\bm{W}_V^{(j)}$ are each $512\times64$, so there are $3\times512\times64=98{,}304$ parameters.

(2) $\bm{W}_O$ is $(n_{\mathrm{head}}d_v)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}=512\times512=262{,}144$ parameters.

(3) Without biases: $8\times98{,}304+262{,}144$ $=786{,}432+262{,}144$ $=1{,}048{,}576$ parameters. This equals $4d_{\mathrm{model}}^{2}=4\times512^{2}$. With biases: adding 512 each after $\bm{Q},\bm{K},\bm{V}$ and $\bm{W}_O$ gives $4\times512=2048$ more, for a total of $1{,}050{,}624$ parameters.

(4) $\bm{W}_Q,\bm{W}_K,\bm{W}_V$ are each $512\times512$ and $\bm{W}_O$ is $512\times512$, so there are $4\times512^{2}=1{,}048{,}576$ parameters, the same as in (3). The number of heads does not change the number of parameters; it determines into how many independent attentions the same number of parameters is divided.

Exercise 24.5: Attention with a distance bias on a chain of atoms

Solution

(1) The query of atom 1 is $(1,0)$. The dot products with the features of the atoms are $(1,0,1,0)$, and dividing by $\sqrt2$ gives $(0.7071,\ 0,\ 0.7071,\ 0)$. The distances are $r_{1j}=0,\ 1.5,\ 3.0,\ 4.5$ Å, so the bias $-r_{1j}^{2}/(2\times1.5^{2})$ is $0,\ -0.5,\ -2,\ -4.5$. The scores are

$$ (0.7071,\ -0.5,\ -1.2929,\ -4.5). $$

$\exp$ gives $(2.0281,\ 0.6065,\ 0.2745,\ 0.0111)$, with sum $2.9202$. The weights are $(0.6945,\ 0.2077,\ 0.0940,\ 0.0038)$ (sum 1). The weights of atoms 1 and 3 go into the A component of the output, and those of atoms 2 and 4 into the B component, so

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{z}_1&=(0.6945+0.0940,\ 0.2077+0.0038)\\ &=(0.7885,\ 0.2115). \end{aligned} $$

(2) As $\ell\to\infty$ all the biases become 0, and the scores are $(0.7071,0,0.7071,0)$. $\exp$ gives $(2.0281,1,2.0281,1)$, with sum $6.0562$, and the weights are $(0.3349,\ 0.1651,\ 0.3349,\ 0.1651)$. The output is $(0.6698,\ 0.3302)$. Since distance has no effect, atom 1 and atom 3, which are of the same element A, receive the same weight even though their distances differ.

(3) As $\ell\to0$, the bias $-r_{1j}^{2}/(2\ell^{2})$ for $j\ne1$ tends to $-\infty$, so the weights become $(1,0,0,0)$ and the output is the atom's own feature $(1,0)$. No information is gathered from the surrounding atoms. The quantity $\ell$ is a length scale that determines out to what distance atoms are taken into account, and it plays the same role as the length scale of the kernel in Chapter 10.

(4) The score is determined only by the features (elements) of atoms $i$ and $j$ and the distance $r_{ij}$; the index numbers are not used. Relabeling the atoms changes the order of the components of the weight vector, but “how much weight goes to which atom (which element, at which distance)” does not change. This is for the same reason as the permutation equivariance in Theorem 24.2 of Chapter 24 (the distance bias is also merely rearranged into $\bm{P}\bm{B}\bm{P}^{\tp}$, so the proof carries over as it is).

Exercise 24.6: Aggregating the temperature history of firing with attention

Solution

(1) The dot products $\bm{q}^{\tp}\bm{k}_j$ are $2\times1+1\times0=2$, $1$, $2+1=3$, $1$. Dividing by $\sqrt2$ gives the scores $(1.4142,\ 0.7071,\ 2.1213,\ 0.7071)$. $\exp$ gives $(4.1133,\ 2.0281,\ 8.3421,\ 2.0281)$, with sum $16.5116$. The weights are

$$ \begin{aligned} &(a_1,a_2,a_3,a_4)\\ &\quad=(0.2491,\ 0.1228,\ 0.5052,\ 0.1228). \end{aligned} $$

The output is

$$ \begin{aligned} &0.2491\times0.0+0.1228\times1.0\\ &\quad+0.5052\times0.5+0.1228\times0.2\\ &=0+0.1228+0.2526+0.0246=0.4000 . \end{aligned} $$

(2) The contributions $a_jv_j$ are $(0,\ 0.1228,\ 0.2526,\ 0.0246)$. The order of the magnitudes of the weights is interval 3 (0.505) > interval 1 (0.249) > interval 2 = interval 4 (0.123), but the order of the magnitudes of the contributions is interval 3 > interval 2 > interval 4 > interval 1. Interval 1, the heating interval, which has the second-largest weight, contributes nothing to the output because its value is 0.

(3) We must not conclude this. Interval 3, which has the largest weight, also has the largest contribution, but (a) the contribution is determined by the product of the weight and the value, so the ranking can change depending on how the values are constructed ($\bm{W}_V$); (b) if the information in interval 3 is strongly correlated with that in other intervals, the same prediction may be obtained even if the weight is moved to another interval (Chapter 24, Section 24.10); and (c) the weight is the proportion in the aggregation of a single layer and does not include the computations in the layers before and after it. Examples of ways to check:

  • Change the temperature data of interval 3 (for example, replace it with the values of another sample, or with the mean value) or remove it, and examine how much the prediction changes. Do the same for the other intervals and compare.
  • Change the random-number seed or the way the training data are split, retrain, and examine whether the weight pattern is reproduced stably (also check that samples from the same furnace and the same lot have not been split between the training and test sets; see data leakage in Chapter 6).
  • Actually prepare samples with different cooling rates and measure the relative density, to check experimentally whether the model's suggestion holds physically.

The first two of the above are ways of checking “which intervals the model is using” inside the model, while the third is a way of checking the physical question “is the first half of the cooling the cause of the relative density?”; the two are different questions.

Exercise 24.7: The causal mask and permutation equivariance

Solution

(1) The first row of the mask is $M_{11}=0$, $M_{1j}=-\infty$ ($j\ge2$). Since $\exp(-\infty)=0$, the weights at position 1 are $A_{11}=e^{s_{11}}/e^{s_{11}}=1$ and $A_{1j}=0$ ($j\ge2$). The output is $\bm{z}_1^{\tp}=1\times\bm{v}_1^{\tp}=\bm{x}_1^{\tp}\bm{W}_V$, which does not depend on the values of the scores.

(2) With the mask applied to the $\bm{X}$ of Section 24.3 (rows $(1,0)$, $(0,1)$, $(1,1)$, $\bm{W}_V=\bm{I}$), the output is $(1,0)$, $(0.3302,0.6698)$, $(0.7517,0.7517)$. If we feed in $\bm{X}'$ with the first and second rows swapped (rows $(0,1)$, $(1,0)$, $(1,1)$), then by (1) the output at the new position 1 is $\bm{x}'_1=(0,1)$. If it were permutation equivariant, the output would have to equal the second row $(0.3302,0.6698)$ of the original output, so they do not agree. Therefore self-attention with a causal mask is not permutation equivariant in general. (The output at the new position 2 is $(0.6698,0.3302)$, which also does not agree with the original first row $(1,0)$.)

(3) By Theorem 24.2, $\bm{Z}(\bm{P}\bm{X})=\bm{P}\bm{Z}(\bm{X})$. The rows of $\bm{P}\bm{Z}$ are the rows of $\bm{Z}$ rearranged, so

$$ \bar{\bm{z}}(\bm{P}\bm{X})=\frac1T\sum_{i=1}^{T}\bigl(\bm{P}\bm{Z}\bigr)_{i}=\frac1T\sum_{i=1}^{T}\bm{z}_i=\bar{\bm{z}}(\bm{X}) $$

(a sum does not depend on the order of addition). The order in which the elements of a composition are listed has no physical meaning (for example, Al$_2$O$_3$ is represented as “Al 0.4, O 0.6”). Listing them in the order “Al, O” or in the order “O, Al” gives the same substance, so the prediction must not change with the way they are listed. Using a permutation-invariant aggregation guarantees this requirement as a structural property of the model.

Exercise 24.8: Computational cost and memory for long sequences

Solution

(1) Dividing both sides of $2T^{2}d\gt4Td^{2}$ by $2Td\ (\gt0)$ gives $T\gt2d$. For $d=1024$, this is $T\gt2048$.

(2) The number of components is $T^{2}n_{\mathrm{head}}=8192^{2}\times16=1{,}073{,}741{,}824$. Multiplying by 4 bytes gives

$$ 4{,}294{,}967{,}296\ \text{byte}=4\ \text{GiB}\ (\approx4.29\ \text{GB}). $$

For $T=1024$, $1024^{2}\times16\times4=67{,}108{,}864$ byte $=64$ MiB. The ratio is $(8192/1024)^{2}=8^{2}=64$, so multiplying the length by 8 multiplies the memory by 64. This is the amount per layer and per sequence.

(3) Disadvantage: the computational cost and the memory grow as $T^{2}$. An RNN grows in proportion to $T$, and what it keeps along the way is only the $d$-dimensional hidden state. Advantages: all positions can be computed in parallel in a single step, and positions far apart are connected directly in a single step. An RNN requires $T$ steps of sequential computation, and information from distant positions passes through the product of many matrices, so gradients tend to vanish or explode.

← Back to Chapter 24, “How Attention Works — Gathering Relevant Information with Weights”

Chapter 25 The Structure of the Transformer — Stacking Attention

Exercise 25.1: Shifting the positional encoding and the dot product

Solution

For $d_{\mathrm{model}}=4$ we have $\omega_0=1$ and $\omega_1=1/10000^{2/4}=0.01$, and the components are ordered as $(\sin\omega_0pos,\ \cos\omega_0pos,$ $\sin\omega_1pos,\ \cos\omega_1pos)$.

(1) Substitute $pos=3$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{PE}_3&=(\sin3,\ \cos3,\ \sin0.03,\ \cos0.03)\\ &=(0.1411,\ -0.9900,\ 0.0300,\ 0.9996). \end{aligned} $$

(2) Using the angle of each pair, $\omega_0k=2$ and $\omega_1k=0.02$, the rotation matrix for the shift $k=2$ is

$$ \bm{R}_2=\begin{pmatrix}c_2&-s_2&0&0\\ s_2&c_2&0&0\\ 0&0&c'&-s'\\ 0&0&s'&c'\end{pmatrix} $$

where $c_2=\cos2$, $s_2=\sin2$, $c'=\cos0.02$, and $s'=\sin0.02$. For the first and second components, multiplying $(\sin1,\cos1)=(0.8415,0.5403)$ of $\bm{PE}_1$ by the upper-left $2\times2$ block gives

$$ \begin{aligned} &\bigl(\sin1\cos2+\cos1\sin2,\\ &\qquad -\sin1\sin2+\cos1\cos2\bigr)\\ &=\bigl(\sin(1+2),\ \cos(1+2)\bigr)\\ &=(0.1411,\ -0.9900) \end{aligned} $$

which agrees with the first and second components of $\bm{PE}_3$ (numerically as well: $0.8415\cdot(-0.4161)$ $+0.5403\cdot0.9093$ $=0.1411$ and $-0.8415\cdot0.9093$ $+0.5403\cdot(-0.4161)$ $=-0.9900$). In the same way, the third and fourth components become $(\sin0.03,\cos0.03)$.

(3) By Eq. (25.8) of Chapter 25 (the formula for the dot product of positional encodings), the dot product is determined only by the difference $k$ of the positions and equals $\sum_i\cos(\omega_ik)$. Both $\bm{PE}_0\bm{PE}_3^{\tp}$ and $\bm{PE}_5\bm{PE}_8^{\tp}$ have the difference $k=3$, so they are equal, and

$$ \begin{aligned} \cos3+\cos0.03&=-0.98999+0.99955\\ &=0.0096 . \end{aligned} $$

A direct computation also gives $\bm{PE}_0\bm{PE}_3^{\tp}$ $=0\cdot0.1411+1\cdot(-0.9900)$ $+0\cdot0.0300+1\cdot0.9996$ $=0.0096$. The fast hand (the first and second components) turns through 3 rad and points almost in the opposite direction ($\cos3\approx-0.99$), while the slow hand points in almost the same direction ($\cos0.03\approx1$), so the sum is almost 0.

Exercise 25.2: Computing LayerNorm and its properties

Solution

(1) The mean is $\bar{x}=(2+4+6+8)/4=5$. The deviations are $(-3,-1,1,3)$. The variance is $s^{2}=(9+1+1+9)/4=5$, so $s=\sqrt5=2.2361$. For $\bm{g}=\bm{1}$ and $\bm{b}=\bm{0}$,

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{LN}(\bm{x})&=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt5}\,(-3,\ -1,\ 1,\ 3)\\ &=(-1.3416,\ -0.4472,\\ &\qquad 0.4472,\ 1.3416). \end{aligned} $$

For $\bm{g}=(1,1,2,2)$ and $\bm{b}=(0,0,0,1)$, multiply componentwise and then add:

$$ \begin{aligned} &(-1.3416,\ -0.4472,\\ &\qquad 2\cdot0.4472,\ 2\cdot1.3416+1)\\ &=(-1.3416,\ -0.4472,\ 0.8944,\ 3.6833). \end{aligned} $$

(2) Let $\bm{x}'=-\bm{x}$. The mean is $\bar{x}'=-\bar{x}$, the deviations are $x'_k-\bar{x}'=-(x_k-\bar{x})$, and the variance is $s'^{2}=\frac1d\sum_k(x_k-\bar{x})^{2}=s^{2}$, so $s'=s$. Therefore

$$ \frac{x'_k-\bar{x}'}{s'}=-\frac{x_k-\bar{x}}{s}, $$

and if $\bm{b}=\bm{0}$ then $\mathrm{LN}(-\bm{x})=-\mathrm{LN}(\bm{x})$. In general, for $a\bm{x}+c\bm{1}$ the denominator becomes $\sqrt{a^{2}s^{2}}=\abs{a}s$, so the ratio is $\dfrac{a}{\abs{a}}\cdot\dfrac{x_k-\bar{x}}{s}=\sgn(a)\dfrac{x_k-\bar{x}}{s}$. Only for $a\gt0$ does the sign stay unchanged so that the result equals the original, which is why Theorem 25.2 assumes $a\gt0$ (for $a\lt0$ the sign is reversed).

(3) If all components equal $c$, then $\bar{x}=c$, all deviations are 0, and $s^{2}=0$. For $\epsilon=0$ we get $0/0$, which is undefined. For $\epsilon\gt0$ the numerator is 0 and the denominator is $\sqrt{\epsilon}\gt0$, so $\mathrm{LN}(\bm{x})_k=g_k\cdot0+b_k=b_k$; that is, the output is exactly the bias $\bm{b}$. The constant $\epsilon$ is included so that the computation does not break down in such cases.

Exercise 25.3: The number of parameters of the base model in the original paper

Solution

Let $d=512$ and $d_{\mathrm{ff}}=2048$. We count part by part.

  • One multi-head attention: $\bm{W}_Q,\bm{W}_K,\bm{W}_V,\bm{W}_O$ are each $512\times512$ and each bias has 512 entries, so $4(512^{2}+512)=4\times262{,}656=1{,}050{,}624$.
  • One FFN: $512\cdot2048+2048$ $+2048\cdot512+512$ $=2{,}099{,}712$.
  • One LayerNorm: $2\times512=1024$.

(1) One encoder layer has one attention, one FFN, and two LayerNorms, so

$$ 1{,}050{,}624+2{,}099{,}712+2048=3{,}152{,}384 . $$

One decoder layer has two attentions (self and cross), one FFN, and three LayerNorms, so

$$ \begin{aligned} &2\times1{,}050{,}624+2{,}099{,}712+3072\\ &=4{,}204{,}032 . \end{aligned} $$

(2) The shared embedding matrix has $37000\times512=18{,}944{,}000$ entries (it is the same matrix on the input side, on the output side, and before the softmax, so it is counted only once). The positional encoding has no parameters. The total is

$$ \begin{aligned} &18{,}944{,}000+6\times3{,}152{,}384+6\times4{,}204{,}032\\ &=18{,}944{,}000+18{,}914{,}304+25{,}224{,}192\\ &=63{,}082{,}496\approx6.31\times10^{7}. \end{aligned} $$

This is about $1.9\times10^{6}$ (about 3 %) fewer than the $65\times10^{6}$ in the paper's table. Converted into rows of the embedding matrix, the difference is about 3700 words, and since the paper gives the vocabulary only as “about 37000,” a difference of this size can be explained by the exact vocabulary size or by implementation details (for example, the presence or absence of biases). From the information in the paper alone, the cause of the difference cannot be narrowed down to a single one. The framework of the count (the blocks account for about 70 % of the total and the embedding for about 30 %) is correct, and the order of magnitude agrees.

Exercise 25.4: A Transformer that takes a composition as input

Solution

(1) For the order “A, B,” $\bm{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ and $\bm{Q}=\bm{K}=\bm{V}=\bm{X}$. The scores are $\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\tp}/\sqrt2=\begin{pmatrix}0.7071&0\\0&0.7071\end{pmatrix}$. The exponentials in the first row are $(2.0281,\ 1)$, with sum $3.0281$, so the weights are $(0.6698,\ 0.3302)$. The second row is $(0.3302,\ 0.6698)$.

$$ \bm{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0.6698&0.3302\\0.3302&0.6698\end{pmatrix},\qquad \bm{Z}=\bm{A}\bm{X}=\bm{A}. $$

The mean is $(0.5,\ 0.5)$, since each component is $(0.6698+0.3302)/2=0.5$. The order “B, A” is obtained by swapping rows; since self-attention is permutation equivariant, the rows of $\bm{Z}$ are merely swapped, and the mean is the same $(0.5,\ 0.5)$.

(2) “A, B”: $\bm{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1.5&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$. The scores are $\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\tp}/\sqrt2=\begin{pmatrix}1.5910&0\\0&0.7071\end{pmatrix}$. The exponentials in the first row are $(4.9086,\ 1)$, giving weights $(0.8308,\ 0.1692)$, and those in the second row are $(1,\ 2.0281)$, giving $(0.3302,\ 0.6698)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{z}_1&=0.8308\,(1.5,0)+0.1692\,(0,1)\\ &=(1.2461,\ 0.1692),\\ \bm{z}_2&=0.3302\,(1.5,0)+0.6698\,(0,1)\\ &=(0.4954,\ 0.6698), \end{aligned} $$

The mean is $(0.8707,\ 0.4195)$.

“B, A”: $\bm{X}=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Dividing $\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\tp}=\begin{pmatrix}1.25&0.5\\0.5&1\end{pmatrix}$ by $\sqrt2$ gives the scores $\begin{pmatrix}0.8839&0.3536\\0.3536&0.7071\end{pmatrix}$. The exponentials in the first row are $(2.4203,\ 1.4241)$, giving weights $(0.6296,\ 0.3704)$, and those in the second row are $(1.4241,\ 2.0281)$, giving $(0.4125,\ 0.5875)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{z}_1&=0.6296\,(0.5,1)+0.3704\,(1,0)\\ &=(0.6852,\ 0.6296),\\ \bm{z}_2&=0.4125\,(0.5,1)+0.5875\,(1,0)\\ &=(0.7937,\ 0.4125), \end{aligned} $$

The mean is $(0.7395,\ 0.5210)$. When positional information is added, the output changes with the ordering even for the same composition.

(3) A composition is a set of elements, and the same substance results whichever order it is written in. If positional information is included, the prediction changes with the ordering as in (2), so the same substance would receive different predictions. If self-attention and the FFN are applied without positional information and the outputs are finally averaged (or summed), the prediction does not depend on the ordering (it is permutation invariant). The composition ratios can be included by attaching a quantity that represents the ratio to the embedding of each token. However, substances with the same composition but different crystal structures (polymorphs) cannot be distinguished by composition alone, so for properties where this matters, structural information is also needed separately.

Exercise 25.5: Turning an XRD pattern into a token sequence

Solution

(1) Since $4000/20=200$, $T=200$.

(2) The weight matrix is $T\times T$.

  • $T=4000$: $4000^{2}=1.6\times10^{7}$ entries, $\times4$ bytes $=6.4\times10^{7}$ bytes $=64$ MB.
  • $T=200$: $200^{2}=4\times10^{4}$ entries, $\times4$ bytes $=1.6\times10^{5}$ bytes $=160$ kB.

Reducing $T$ by a factor of 20 reduces the weight matrix by a factor of $20^{2}=400$. This is for one layer and one head, so the total is further multiplied by the number of layers and heads.

(3) The ratio is $T/(6d_{\mathrm{model}})$, and $6\times256=1536$. For $T=4000$, $4000/1536=2.60$ (attention is 2.6 times the linear transformations); for $T=200$, $200/1536=0.130$ (the linear transformations dominate).

(4) Advantage: the computation and memory of attention decrease as $T^{2}$, making training and inference lighter. Since one token represents a shape spanning a width of $0.4^{\circ}$ (the rise of a peak, its vertex, and so on) all at once, the connection between neighboring points can be handled by the first linear transformation. Disadvantage: fine differences of position within one token (peak shifts on the order of $0.02^{\circ}$) are not distinguished as token positions and are pushed into the vector that makes up the token's content. In analyses where fine angular information is important, such as peak shifts due to small changes in the lattice constant or the separation of two closely spaced peaks, some measures are needed, such as narrowing the patch width or supplying the peak positions as a separate feature. How to supply the physical quantity called the diffraction angle as positional information (as a position index, or as a function of the angle itself) is also a design choice.

Exercise 25.6: The crossover point of computational cost

Solution

(1) Solve $12Td^{2}=2T^{2}d$. Dividing both sides by $2Td$ ($\gt0$) gives $6d=T$. Therefore the two are equal at $T=6d$; for $T\lt6d$ the linear transformations dominate, and for $T\gt6d$ attention dominates.

(2) The ratio is $T/(6d)$. The small model of S10: $24/(6\cdot32)=24/192=0.125$. The smallest GPT-2 model: $1024/(6\cdot768)=1024/4608=0.222$. In both cases the linear transformations dominate even at the full context length.

(3) $12\times768^{2}\times12$ $=12\times589{,}824\times12$ $=84{,}934{,}656$ operations. The number of parameters of the 12 blocks of Example 25.10 is $12\times7{,}087{,}872=85{,}054{,}464$, and the difference is only $119{,}808$ (which corresponds to the number of bias and LayerNorm parameters, $12\times(4\cdot768+3072+768+4\cdot768)$). Each individual weight of a linear transformation is used in exactly one multiply–add per token, so “the number of multiply–adds per token ≈ the number of weight parameters.” This is why the computational cost of a large model can be estimated from its number of parameters (the $2Td$ part of attention and the $dV_{\mathrm{voc}}$ part of the output logits are added separately).

Exercise 25.7: The causal mask and “not looking ahead”

Solution

We prove, for $l=0,1,\dots,n_{\mathrm{layer}}$, the proposition $P(l)$: “the $t$th row of the output $\bm{X}^{(l)}$ of the $l$th block is determined only by $x_1,\dots,x_t$ (for all $t$).”

$l=0$: The $t$th row of $\bm{X}^{(0)}$ is $\bm{e}_{x_t}\bm{W}_E+\bm{p}_t$, which is determined only by $x_t$. Hence $P(0)$ holds.

$P(l-1)\Rightarrow P(l)$: Assume $P(l-1)$ and examine each operation of the $l$th pre-LN block in turn.

  1. $\mathrm{LN}_1$ is applied to each row separately, so the $t$th row of $\mathrm{LN}_1(\bm{X}^{(l-1)})$ is determined only by the $t$th row of $\bm{X}^{(l-1)}$, and by the assumption only by $x_{\le t}$.
  2. $\bm{Q}_j,\bm{K}_j,\bm{V}_j$ are obtained by multiplying each row by a matrix from the right, so the $s$th row is determined only by $x_{\le s}$.
  3. The $t$th row of the self-attention output is $\sum_{s}A_{ts}\bm{v}_s$. Because of the causal mask, the weights with $s\gt t$ satisfy $A_{ts}=0$, so the sum runs only over $s\le t$. The weights $A_{ts}$ ($s\le t$) are built from $\bm{q}_t$ and $\bm{k}_1,\dots,\bm{k}_t$, and $\bm{v}_s$ ($s\le t$) is also determined only by $x_{\le t}$. Hence the $t$th row is determined only by $x_{\le t}$. Concatenating the heads and multiplying by $\bm{W}_O$ is a row-wise operation and so changes nothing.
  4. $\bm{U}=\bm{X}^{(l-1)}+\mathrm{MHA}(\cdot)$ is a row-wise sum, so the $t$th row of $\bm{U}$ is also determined only by $x_{\le t}$.
  5. $\mathrm{LN}_2$, the FFN, and the residual sum are all row-wise operations, so the $t$th row of $\bm{X}^{(l)}$ is also determined only by $x_{\le t}$.

Hence $P(l)$ holds. By induction $P(n_{\mathrm{layer}})$ holds, and since the final LayerNorm and the multiplication by $\bm{W}_E^{\tp}$ are also row-wise, the $t$th row $\bm{o}_t$ of the logits is determined only by $x_1,\dots,x_t$.

What goes wrong in training: In next-token prediction, the model is trained to predict $x_{t+1}$ from the output at position $t$. If $\bm{o}_t$ could depend on $x_{t+1}$, the model could make the loss almost 0 merely by “copying” $x_{t+1}$ from the input, and would learn no ability to predict the next token at all. At generation time $x_{t+1}$ does not yet exist, so such a model would be useless. Thanks to the causal mask, even when the whole correct sequence is fed in once, the prediction at each position is computed without peeking at the answer, and the losses at all positions can be obtained simultaneously in a single forward propagation.

Exercise 25.8: How much does weight sharing save?

Solution

(1) Keeping a separate $\bm{W}_U\in\R^{768\times50257}$ adds $38{,}597{,}376$ parameters, giving

$$ 124{,}439{,}808+38{,}597{,}376=163{,}037{,}184 . $$

The reduction due to sharing is $38{,}597{,}376/163{,}037{,}184=0.237$; that is, 23.7 % of the unshared total.

(2) In the small model of S10, $\bm{W}_U$ has $32\times67=2144$ entries, and without sharing the total would be $28{,}384+2144=30{,}528$. The reduction is $2144/30{,}528=0.070$, or 7.0 %. The vocabulary is small, so the effect is also small.

(3) Writing the $i$th row of $\bm{W}_E$ as $\bm{w}_i$, under weight sharing $o_{t,i}=\bm{h}_t\bm{w}_i^{\tp}$. The difference of the logits of two tokens $i,j$ is

$$ o_{t,i}-o_{t,j}=\bm{h}_t\bm{w}_i^{\tp}-\bm{h}_t\bm{w}_j^{\tp}=\bm{h}_t(\bm{w}_i-\bm{w}_j)^{\tp} $$

and by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

$$ \abs{o_{t,i}-o_{t,j}}\le\norm{\bm{h}_t}\,\norm{\bm{w}_i-\bm{w}_j} $$

holds. Two tokens whose input embeddings are close ($\norm{\bm{w}_i-\bm{w}_j}$ is small) have a small logit difference at every position, and so receive similar probabilities at the output as well. Tokens that tend to appear in the same contexts are learned to have close embeddings, so the learning on the input side and on the output side help each other through a single matrix.

Chapter 26 How GPT Learns and Generates Text

Exercise 26.1: Greedy decoding and the most probable sequence

Solution

Each factor is determined by the preceding token alone, so we substitute the values in the table into each factor of the autoregressive decomposition, Eq. (26.1) of Chapter 26, and multiply.

(1)

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\text{A }\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.55\times0.25=0.1375,\\ p(\text{B }\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.45\times0.90=0.405 . \end{aligned} $$

(2) There are four sequences with two tokens before <eos>: AA, AB, BA, and BB.

$$ \begin{aligned} p(\text{AA}\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.55\times0.40\times0.25=0.0550,\\ p(\text{AB}\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.55\times0.35\times0.90=0.17325,\\ p(\text{BA}\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.45\times0.05\times0.25=0.005625,\\ p(\text{BB}\langle\text{eos}\rangle)&=0.45\times0.05\times0.90=0.02025 . \end{aligned} $$

The sum is $0.0550+0.17325$ $+0.005625+0.02025$ $=0.254125$.

(3) Greedy decoding first chooses A, which has the larger probability (0.55). After A, the largest probability is 0.40 for A, so it chooses A again, and from then on it always chooses A after A. Since <eos> (0.25) is never the largest, greedy decoding keeps generating “A A A …” up to the upper limit of the context length.

On the other hand, the probability of the whole sequence is largest for “B <eos>”, at 0.405. Every sequence that starts with A is the leading factor 0.55 multiplied by numbers less than 1, such as 0.1375 for A <eos> and 0.17325 for AB <eos>, so none of them reaches 0.405 (even the largest continuation among the three after A gives $0.55\times0.35\times0.90=0.173$). Among the sequences starting with B, those that go on to something other than <eos> have probability at most $0.45\times0.05$. Therefore the maximum is B <eos>.

Because greedy decoding chooses at each step the word with the largest immediate probability, it cannot undo a choice that is “advantageous at that moment but followed by a poor continuation,” like the initial A. Maximizing the probability of the whole sequence requires something like beam search, which keeps several partial sequences. Also, as in this example, greedy decoding can fail to escape from repeating the same word.

Exercise 26.2: Segmenting a new word with learned merges

Solution

(1) Split the word into characters, append “_” at the end of the word, and apply the merges in the order n+_, i+o, io+n_, o+x.

  • ions: i o n s _ → (n+_ does not apply; n is followed by s) → io n s _ → (io+n_ does not apply) → (o+x does not apply). The result is the four tokens io, n, s, _.
  • onion: o n i o n _ → o n i o n_ → o n io n_ → o n ion_ → (o is followed by n, so o+x does not apply). The result is the three tokens o, n, ion_.
  • nation: n a t i o n _ → n a t i o n_ → n a t io n_ → n a t ion_. The result is the four tokens n, a, t, ion_.
  • oxygen: o x y g e n _ → o x y g e n_ → (no i o) → (no io n_) → ox y g e n_. The result is the five tokens ox, y, g, e, n_.

(2) After four rounds, the symbol sequences are ox i d e _ (once), ion_ (four times), io n s _ (once), and ox y g e n_ (twice). The counts of adjacent pairs are 2 for each of (ox, y), (y, g), (g, e), and (e, n_), all from oxygen occurring twice, and 1 for all the others. The maximum is tied among four pairs, so a rule for deciding which one to merge is needed (for example, choose the pair that appeared first, or choose by character order). Since the rule changes the vocabulary and the segmentation, when implementing BPE one must decide on and record the handling of ties; otherwise the same vocabulary cannot be reproduced from the same text.

(3) For the model, a token is just an ID (the row number of the embedding matrix), and the fact that “ion_” and “io” and “n” contain the same characters is not directly visible from the input. The relation between ion and ions (singular and plural) can only be learned within the embeddings and weights, from the fact that the two appear in similar contexts. The fewer times a word appears in the training data, the more weakly such a relation is learned.

Exercise 26.3: Temperature and entropy

Solution

(1) From $p_i=\exp(o_i/\tau)/Z$ we get $\ln p_i=o_i/\tau-\ln Z$. Substitute this into $H$ and use $\sum_ip_i=1$.

$$ \begin{aligned} H(\tau)&=-\sum_ip_i\Bigl(\frac{o_i}{\tau}-\ln Z\Bigr)\\ &=-\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_ip_io_i+\ln Z\sum_ip_i\\ &=\ln Z(\tau)-\frac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau} . \end{aligned} $$

(2) First we find the derivatives of $\ln Z$ and $p_i$. Since $\diff{}{\tau}\exp(o_j/\tau)=-\dfrac{o_j}{\tau^{2}}\exp(o_j/\tau)$,

$$ \diff{\ln Z}{\tau}=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_j\Bigl(-\frac{o_j}{\tau^{2}}\Bigr)e^{o_j/\tau}=-\frac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau^{2}} . $$

Differentiating $\ln p_i=o_i/\tau-\ln Z$ gives $\dfrac{1}{p_i}\diff{p_i}{\tau}=-\dfrac{o_i}{\tau^{2}}+\dfrac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau^{2}}$, that is,

$$ \diff{p_i}{\tau}=-\frac{p_i\,(o_i-\langle o\rangle)}{\tau^{2}} . $$

Using this,

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{\langle o\rangle}{\tau}&=\sum_io_i\diff{p_i}{\tau}=-\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_ip_io_i(o_i-\langle o\rangle)\\ &=-\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\bigl(\langle o^{2}\rangle-\langle o\rangle^{2}\bigr)=-\frac{\Var_p[o]}{\tau^{2}} . \end{aligned} $$

Differentiate the expression in (1) (the derivative of a quotient: $\diff{}{\tau}\dfrac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau}=\dfrac{1}{\tau}\diff{\langle o\rangle}{\tau}-\dfrac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau^{2}}$).

$$ \begin{aligned} \diff{H}{\tau}&=-\frac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau^{2}}-\Bigl(-\frac{\Var_p[o]}{\tau^{3}}-\frac{\langle o\rangle}{\tau^{2}}\Bigr)\\ &=\frac{\Var_p[o]}{\tau^{3}} . \end{aligned} $$

The variance is at least 0 and $\tau\gt0$, so $dH/d\tau\ge0$, and $H$ increases monotonically in $\tau$ (only when all the logits are equal is the variance 0, and then $H$ is constant at $\ln V_{\mathrm{voc}}$).

(3) The probabilities at $\tau=1$ are $\bm{p}=(0.46306,\,0.28086,$ $0.17035,\,0.06267,\,0.02305)$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \langle o\rangle&=2.0(0.46306)+1.5(0.28086)\\ &\quad+1.0(0.17035)-1.0(0.02305)\\ &=1.4947,\\ \langle o^{2}\rangle&=4(0.46306)+2.25(0.28086)\\ &\quad+1(0.17035)+1(0.02305)\\ &=2.6776 . \end{aligned} $$

$\Var_p[o]=2.6776-1.4947^{2}$ $=2.6776-2.2342=0.4434$. Since $\tau=1$, $dH/d\tau=0.4434$ nat. As a check, by the expression in (1), $\ln Z=\ln15.957=2.7699$ and $H=2.7699-1.4947=1.2752$ nat, which agrees with the value in Example 26.6.

Exercise 26.4: Combining top-k and top-p

Solution

(1) The sum of the exp values is $2.718+2.226+1.221$ $+0.607+0.223=6.995$ (6.9949 without rounding).

$$ \begin{aligned} \bm{p}(1)=(&0.3886,\ 0.3182,\ 0.1746,\\ &0.0867,\ 0.0319) . \end{aligned} $$

(2) The cumulative sums in decreasing order are $0.3886$, $0.7068$ ($\lt0.75$), and $0.8814$ ($\ge0.75$). Three words remain, and renormalizing by $0.8814$ gives $(0.4409,\ 0.3610,\ 0.1981,\ 0,\ 0)$.

(3) Dividing the logits by 0.5 gives $(2.0,\,1.6,\,0.4,\,-1.0,\,-3.0)$. The exp values are $7.389$, $4.953$, $1.492$, $0.368$, $0.050$, with sum $14.252$, and $\bm{p}(0.5)=(0.5185,\,0.3475,$ $0.1047,\,0.0258,\,0.0035)$. The cumulative sums are $0.5185$ and $0.8660$ ($\ge0.75$), so two words remain, and the result is $(0.5987,\ 0.4013,\ 0,\ 0,\ 0)$. When the distribution becomes sharper, fewer words remain for the same $p$.

(4) Keeping three words with top-k $=3$ and renormalizing gives $(0.4409,\,0.3610,\,0.1981)$. Taking the cumulative sums with these renormalized probabilities gives $0.4409$ and $0.8019$ ($\ge0.75$), so two words remain, and renormalizing by $0.8019$ gives $(0.5498,\ 0.4502,\ 0,\ 0,\ 0)$. In (2) three words remained, whereas here there are two. This is because renormalizing after cutting the tail with top-k raises the probabilities of the top words, so the cumulative sum reaches $p$ sooner. When top-k and top-p are combined, the result depends on the order of application and on which probabilities the cumulative sum is taken over.

Exercise 26.5: The gradient and the optimal solution of next-token prediction

Solution

(1) $\ell=-\ln p_y(\tau)=-\dfrac{o_y}{\tau}+\ln\sum_j\exp(o_j/\tau)$. The derivative of the first term with respect to $o_i$ is $-[i=y]/\tau$. For the second term, by the chain rule,

$$ \pdiff{}{o_i}\ln\sum_j e^{o_j/\tau}=\frac{1}{\sum_je^{o_j/\tau}}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau}e^{o_i/\tau}=\frac{p_i(\tau)}{\tau} . $$

Together, $\pdiff{\ell}{o_i}=\bigl(p_i(\tau)-[i=y]\bigr)/\tau$. For $\tau=1$ this reduces to Eq. (26.5) of Chapter 26.

(2) $\bm{o}/\tau=(0.5,\,0,\,-0.5)$. We have $e^{0.5}=1.6487$, $e^{0}=1$, $e^{-0.5}=0.6065$, with sum $3.2553$, so $\bm{p}=(0.5065,\,0.3072,\,0.1863)$. The gradient is

$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{\bm{p}-\bm{y}}{\tau}&=\frac{(0.5065,\,-0.6928,\,0.1863)}{2}\\ &=(0.2532,\,-0.3464,\,0.0932) . \end{aligned} $$

(3) By Eq. (26.4) of Chapter 26, the expected loss is $H[p_{\mathrm{data}}]+\KL(p_{\mathrm{data}}\,\|\,q)$. The first term does not depend on $q$. The second term is at least 0, and it is 0 only when $q=p_{\mathrm{data}}$ (Chapter 5). Therefore the $q$ that minimizes the expected loss is $p_{\mathrm{data}}$ itself, and the minimum value is the entropy $H[p_{\mathrm{data}}]$.

Exercise 26.6: Decomposing the loss of the mercury sentence

Solution

(1) The correct sentence (liquid):

$$ \begin{aligned} \ln p&=-6.859-0.116-2.585\\ &\quad-0.003-8.112-0.007\\ &=-17.682 . \end{aligned} $$

The number of tokens is 8, so the perplexity is $\exp(17.682/8)=\exp(2.210)=9.12$. The incorrect sentence (solid): $-6.859-0.116-2.585$ $-0.003-0.002-0.001$ $=-9.566$, and the perplexity is $\exp(9.566/8)=\exp(1.196)=3.31$.

(2) The first four tokens are common to both, so the difference $-9.566-(-17.682)=8.116$ comes from the last four tokens. Among them, the difference at the position of “liquid” and “solid” is $-0.002-(-8.112)=8.110$, which accounts for almost all of it.

(3) The optimal distribution that does not distinguish contexts is $q_{\text{solid}}=590/591$, $q_{\text{liquid}}=1/591$ (Eq. (26.14)). The loss at the 591 positions is $-\ln(590/591)=0.001693$ for each of the 590 solid positions and $\ln591=6.382$ for the one liquid position. The average is

$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{590\times0.001693+6.382}{591}&=\frac{0.999+6.382}{591}\\ &=0.01249\ \text{nat} . \end{aligned} $$

The total is $7.381$ nat. For a model that can perfectly distinguish mercury alone (putting 1 on liquid in the mercury context and 1 on solid elsewhere), every position has probability 1, so the average loss is 0.

(4) The total loss over the entire training data is about 0.505 nat per token times about 56,500 tokens, or about $2.9\times10^{4}$ nat. Even if the mercury exception were learned perfectly, the reduction would be at most about 7.4 nat, only about 0.03 % of the whole. Seen against the total loss, the weight of memorizing a rare exception is extremely small, and learning by gradients does not necessarily give priority to memorizing this exception. The exceptions that matter in materials engineering (metals that are liquid at room temperature, ferromagnetic metals, insulators that conduct heat well, and so on) are often exactly the “rare” ones, so for the answers of a language model, the more exceptional the property, the more it needs to be checked.

Exercise 26.7: A sampling inspection that found no errors

Solution

(1) Of 15 items, 0 are wrong ($m=0$, $l=15$), so the posterior distribution is $\mathrm{Beta}(1+0,\,1+15)=\mathrm{Beta}(1,16)$. The posterior mean is $1/(1+16)=1/17=0.0588$.

(2) The density of $\mathrm{Beta}(1,16)$ is $16(1-\mu)^{15}$, and the cumulative distribution function is $\int_0^u16(1-\mu)^{15}\dd\mu=1-(1-u)^{16}$. Set this equal to 0.95.

$$ \begin{aligned} &(1-u)^{16}=0.05\\ &\Longrightarrow\ u=1-0.05^{1/16}=1-0.8293=0.1707 . \end{aligned} $$

Even if there is not a single error, the proportion of errors can be as high as about 17 %.

(3) If there are no errors among $n$ items, the posterior distribution is $\mathrm{Beta}(1,n+1)$, and the 95 % upper limit is $1-0.05^{1/(n+1)}$. The condition for this to be less than 0.05 is $0.05^{1/(n+1)}\gt0.95$; taking logarithms, $\dfrac{\ln0.05}{n+1}\gt\ln0.95$. Dividing by $\ln0.95\lt0$ reverses the inequality, giving $n+1\gt\ln0.05/\ln0.95=58.40$. Hence $n+1\ge59$, that is, $n\ge58$ items (at $n=58$ the upper limit is 0.0495, and at $n=57$ it is 0.0503).

(4) Finding no errors in 15 items does not show that the proportion of errors is 0. As in (2), it is consistent even with an error proportion of about 17 %. Moreover, among 15 randomly chosen items, rare kinds of errors (a particular way of writing a unit, a particular measurement method) are easily missed. A report should state the size of the inspection and the upper limit, for example: “15 items were checked against the source and 0 errors were found; the 95 % upper limit of the error proportion is about 17 %.”

Exercise 26.8: What a small model can tell us

Solution

  • (a) Can be said as a mechanism (but not as a number). In S10, the probability of the correct word dropped for the material that has only 8 sentences in the training data, and the top-ranked errors were also concentrated there (Figure 26.7). This is a mechanism that comes from the form of the model and its objective function, namely that the objective function of next-token prediction reflects frequency (the extreme case of Eq. (26.14)), so a trend in the same direction can occur in larger models of the same form. However, the strength of the trend changes with scale and data, and it cannot be determined from S10.
  • (b) Cannot be said. The 9.0 % is a value measured with the small model of S10 and a synthetic corpus for teaching purposes; the actual GPT differs in model size, training data, and tuning (Table 26.1).
  • (c) Cannot be said (rather, it is wrong). $\tau\to0$ is the same as greedy decoding and simply chooses the top-ranked word with certainty. In the mercury example the top-ranked word is the incorrect “solid,” so lowering $\tau$ makes the error appear with certainty. Temperature reduces Reason 3 (errors due to sampling), but it does not reduce the errors due to Reasons 1 and 2.
  • (d) Cannot be said. In step 5 of Simulator 26.2, the last position of the barium titanate context put a weight of 0.80 on the material name, but the answer was wrong. Attention weights are the proportions in which information is gathered; they do not guarantee that a judgment is correct or give its reasons (Chapter 24, Section 24.10).
  • (e) Can be said. The claim is the negation of “if the probability is high, it is correct,” so a single counterexample suffices to show it. In S10, the $\ln p$ of the incorrect “Mercury is solid at room temperature .” is −9.57, which is larger than −17.68 for the correct sentence. This counterexample holds even for a small model, and it is sufficient to negate the claim.

Chapter 27 Connections to Materials Science and Statistical Mechanics — From the Partition Function to Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials

Exercise 27.1: The energy reference and the free energy

Solution

(1) $k_{\mathrm B}T$ $=8.617\times10^{-5}\times600$ $=0.05170\ \mathrm{eV}$, $\beta$ $=1/0.05170$ $=19.34\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}}$. The weights are

$$ \begin{aligned} e^{-\beta\times0}&=1,\\ e^{-0.05/0.05170}&=e^{-0.9671}=0.3802,\\ e^{-0.10/0.05170}&=e^{-1.9341}=0.1446 . \end{aligned} $$

$Z=1+0.3802+0.1446=1.5248$. The probabilities are $p=(0.6558,\ 0.2494,\ 0.0948)$. $A$ $=-0.05170\times\ln1.5248$ $=-0.05170\times0.4218$ $=-0.02181\ \mathrm{eV}$. $U$ $=0.05\times0.2494+0.10\times0.0948$ $=0.01247+0.00948$ $=0.02195\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(2) Each weight is multiplied by $e^{-\beta\times1.00}$ $=e^{-19.34}$ $=3.98\times10^{-9}$.

  • Probabilities: the same factor multiplies the numerator and the denominator, so they do not change.
  • $Z$: it is multiplied by $3.98\times10^{-9}$, giving $6.08\times10^{-9}$.
  • $A$: since $-k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(Ze^{-\beta})$ $=-k_{\mathrm B}T\ln Z+1.00$, it rises by $1.00$ eV to $0.9782\ \mathrm{eV}$.
  • $U$: the probabilities are unchanged and each energy is larger by $1.00$, so it rises by $1.00$ eV to $1.0219\ \mathrm{eV}$.
  • $S=(U-A)/T$: $U$ and $A$ rise by the same amount, so it does not change. $S$ $=(0.02195+0.02181)/600$ $=7.29\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{eV\,K^{-1}}$, that is, $S/k_{\mathrm B}=0.846$.

The choice of the energy origin shifts the values of $A$ and $U$, but it does not affect the probabilities or the entropy (a quantity of differences, which can be measured).

Exercise 27.2: Gradient, convexity, and fluctuations of an exponential-family model

Solution

(1) Differentiating $Z_{\bm{\theta}}$ $=\sum_x\exp(\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bm{f}(x))$ and using $\partial\exp(\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bm{f})/\partial\bm{\theta}$ $=\bm{f}\exp(\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bm{f})$, we get

$$ \begin{aligned}\pdiff{\ln Z_{\bm{\theta}}}{\bm{\theta}}&=\frac{1}{Z_{\bm{\theta}}}\sum_x\bm{f}(x)\exp(\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bm{f}(x))\\ &=\sum_xp_{\bm{\theta}}(x)\bm{f}(x)=\Ex_{p_{\bm{\theta}}}[\bm{f}].\end{aligned} $$

(2) Differentiating $\ln p_{\bm{\theta}}(x)$ $=\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bm{f}(x)-\ln Z_{\bm{\theta}}$ and using (1) gives $\partial\ln p_{\bm{\theta}}(x)/\partial\bm{\theta}$ $=\bm{f}(x)-\Ex[\bm{f}]$, and hence $\partial p_{\bm{\theta}}(x)/\partial\bm{\theta}^{\tp}$ $=p_{\bm{\theta}}(x)(\bm{f}(x)-\Ex[\bm{f}])^{\tp}$. Differentiating the result of (1) once more,

$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial^{2}\ln Z_{\bm{\theta}}}{\partial\bm{\theta}\,\partial\bm{\theta}^{\tp}}&=\sum_x\bm{f}(x)\pdiff{p_{\bm{\theta}}(x)}{\bm{\theta}^{\tp}}\\ &=\sum_xp_{\bm{\theta}}(x)\bm{f}(x)\bigl(\bm{f}(x)-\Ex[\bm{f}]\bigr)^{\tp}\\ &=\Ex[\bm{f}\bm{f}^{\tp}]-\Ex[\bm{f}]\Ex[\bm{f}]^{\tp}=\Cov_{p_{\bm{\theta}}}[\bm{f}]. \end{aligned} $$

The mean negative log-likelihood is $-\ell(\bm{\theta})$ $=-\bm{\theta}^{\tp}\bar{\bm{f}}+\ln Z_{\bm{\theta}}$ (with $\bar{\bm{f}}$ $=\frac1N\sum_n\bm{f}(x_n)$). The first term is a linear function of $\bm{\theta}$, so the Hessian matrix equals $\Cov_{p_{\bm{\theta}}}[\bm{f}]$. For any $\bm{a}$, $\bm{a}^{\tp}\Cov[\bm{f}]\bm{a}$ $=\Var[\bm{a}^{\tp}\bm{f}]\ge0$, so the covariance matrix is positive semidefinite, and $-\ell$ is a convex function.

(3) Setting $\bm{\theta}\to-\beta$ (a scalar) and $\bm{f}\to E$, (1) and (2) become

$$ \begin{aligned}&\diff{\ln Z}{(-\beta)}=\Ex[E],\\ &\frac{\dd^{2}\ln Z}{\dd(-\beta)^{2}}=\frac{\dd^{2}\ln Z}{\dd\beta^{2}}=\Var[E].\end{aligned} $$

Hence $U=\Ex[E]=-\dd\ln Z/\dd\beta$ and $\dd U/\dd\beta$ $=-\dd^{2}\ln Z/\dd\beta^{2}$ $=-\Var[E]$. From $\beta=1/(k_{\mathrm B}T)$ we have $\dd\beta/\dd T$ $=-1/(k_{\mathrm B}T^{2})$, so

$$ \begin{aligned}C&=\diff{U}{T}=\diff{U}{\beta}\diff{\beta}{T}\\ &=(-\Var[E])\times\Bigl(-\frac{1}{k_{\mathrm B}T^{2}}\Bigr)\\ &=\frac{\Var[E]}{k_{\mathrm B}T^{2}}.\end{aligned} $$

Since a variance cannot be negative, we also see that the specific heat of the canonical distribution is never negative.

Exercise 27.3: Antiferromagnetic spins on a triangle

Solution

(1) When all three spins point the same way ($+++$, $---$), the products on the three bonds are all $+1$, so $E=3J$. In the other six states (two spins the same, one opposite), there is one pair pointing the same way (product $+1$) and two pairs pointing oppositely (product $-1$), so $E=J(1-1-1)=-J$. To make all three bonds antiparallel, we would need $s_1=-s_2$, $s_2=-s_3$, and $s_3=-s_1$; but the first two give $s_1=s_3$, which contradicts the third. In every state at least one bond is “unsatisfied” (frustration).

(2) $Z=6e^{\beta J}+2e^{-3\beta J}$.

(3) For $\beta J=1$: $Z$ $=6\times2.7183+2\times0.04979$ $=16.3097+0.0996$ $=16.4093$. The probability of being in one of the six lowest-energy states is $16.3097/16.4093=0.9939$. $\langle E\rangle/J$ $=[(-1)\times16.3097+3\times0.0996]/16.4093$ $=(-16.3097+0.2987)/16.4093$ $=-0.9757$. $S/k_{\mathrm B}$ $=\ln Z+\beta\langle E\rangle$ $=\ln16.4093+1\times(-0.9757)$ $=2.7978-0.9757$ $=1.8221$.

(4) As $T\to0$, only the six lowest-energy states remain, with equal probabilities, so $S/k_{\mathrm B}\to\ln6=1.792$. For a ferromagnet ($J\lt0$), the lowest energy is attained by just two states, $+++$ and $---$, so $S/k_{\mathrm B}\to\ln2=0.693$. In the antiferromagnetic triangle, frustration makes the lowest-energy states numerous, so entropy remains even at low temperature. It is this kind of degeneracy that underlies the entropy that remains even in the limit of absolute zero (residual entropy), which is discussed for antiferromagnets on the triangular and Kagome lattices and for the arrangement of hydrogen atoms in water ice.

Exercise 27.4: Variation with a trial Hamiltonian

Solution

(1) Setting $q=p_0$ in Theorem 27.1 gives $A\le A[p_0]=\Ex_{p_0}[E]-TS[p_0]$. Since $p_0$ itself is the Boltzmann distribution of $E_0$, applying the equality case of Theorem 27.1 to $E_0$ gives $A_0=\Ex_{p_0}[E_0]-TS[p_0]$, that is, $-TS[p_0]=A_0-\Ex_{p_0}[E_0]$. Substituting,

$$ \begin{aligned}A&\le\Ex_{p_0}[E]+A_0-\Ex_{p_0}[E_0]\\ &=A_0+\Ex_{p_0}[E-E_0].\end{aligned} $$

(2) For $E_0=-b(s_1+s_2)$ the spins are independent, with $Z_0$ $=(e^{\beta b}+e^{-\beta b})^{2}$ $=(2\cosh\beta b)^{2}$, $A_0$ $=-2k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(2\cosh\beta b)$, and $\Ex_{p_0}[s_i]=\tanh\beta b\equiv m$. By independence, $\Ex_{p_0}[E]$ $=-J\Ex[s_1]\Ex[s_2]$ $=-Jm^{2}$ and $\Ex_{p_0}[E_0]=-2bm$. Hence the right-hand side is

$$ R(m)=-2k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(2\cosh\beta b)-Jm^{2}+2bm . $$

Using $\beta b=\operatorname{artanh}m$ and $\cosh(\operatorname{artanh}m)$ $=1/\sqrt{1-m^{2}}$, we write it in terms of $m$.

$$ \begin{aligned} \beta R(m)&=-2\ln2+\ln(1-m^{2})\\ &\quad-\beta Jm^{2}+2m\operatorname{artanh}m . \end{aligned} $$

On the other hand, simplifying $\varphi(m)$ $=\tfrac{1+m}{2}\ln\tfrac{1+m}{2}+\tfrac{1-m}{2}\ln\tfrac{1-m}{2}$ gives $\varphi(m)$ $=-\ln2+\tfrac12\ln(1-m^{2})+m\operatorname{artanh}m$, so $\beta R(m)$ $=-\beta Jm^{2}+2\varphi(m)$. This agrees with Eq. (27.12) of the main text (the mean-field variational free energy per spin) with $N=2$, $n_{\mathrm{nn}}=1$, and $h=0$. Differentiating with respect to $m$ and setting the result to 0 gives $-2\beta Jm+2\operatorname{artanh}m$ $=0$, that is, $m=\tanh(\beta Jm)$. “Varying within the distributions of independent spins” and “varying with a trial Hamiltonian with an effective field $b$” are the same thing, and the optimal effective field is $b=Jm$.

Exercise 27.5: The maximum-entropy distribution of a positive quantity

Solution

(1) By the same calculation as in Theorem 27.2 (replacing the sum by an integral over $x\ge0$), $p(x)=e^{-\lambda x}/Z$. For $Z=\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda x}\dd x$ to be finite we need $\lambda\gt0$, and then $Z=1/\lambda$. The mean is $\Ex[x]$ $=\lambda\int_0^\infty xe^{-\lambda x}\dd x$ $=\lambda\times1/\lambda^{2}$ $=1/\lambda$, so the constraint gives $\lambda=1/\mu$. Therefore $p(x)=\mu^{-1}e^{-x/\mu}$.

(2) Since $-\ln p(x)=\ln\mu+x/\mu$, $H$ $=\Ex[-\ln p]$ $=\ln\mu+\Ex[x]/\mu$ $=\ln\mu+1$. (If the unit of $x$ is changed, $\ln\mu$ changes. The differential entropy depends on the unit.)

(3) For $\mu=2\ \mu\mathrm{m}$, $P(d\gt4)$ $=\int_4^\infty\tfrac12e^{-d/2}\dd d$ $=e^{-4/2}$ $=e^{-2}$ $=0.135$.

(4) The exponential distribution is monotonically decreasing with its maximum density at $d=0$, but the grain-size distribution of an actual sintered body often has a peak at some size and a tail extending to the right (a shape close to a lognormal distribution). If we fix the mean of $\ln d$ and the mean of its square, the maximum-entropy distribution becomes $p(d)\propto\exp(-\lambda_1\ln d-\lambda_2(\ln d)^{2})$ $=d^{-\lambda_1}e^{-\lambda_2(\ln d)^{2}}$, which has the form of a lognormal distribution (with $\lambda_2\gt0$). If we put into the constraints the means of whichever quantities we know, the shape of the distribution changes accordingly. Regarding the maximum-entropy distribution for the mean alone as a “model of the grain-size distribution” leaves information unused.

Exercise 27.6: Estimating atomic energies from the total energy alone

Solution

(1) The kernel values are $k(0,0)=k(1,1)=1$ and $k(0,1)=e^{-0.5}=0.6065$. From Eq. (27.17) (the covariance of the total energies),

$$ \begin{aligned} \Var[E_A]&=4\,k(0,0)=4,\\ \Var[E_B]&=k(0,0)+k(1,1)+2k(0,1)\\ &=2+1.2131=3.2131,\\ \Cov[E_A,E_B]&=\sum_{i\in A}\bigl[k(0,0)+k(0,1)\bigr]\\ &=2\times1.6065=3.2131 \end{aligned} $$

(in units of $\mathrm{eV^{2}}$).

(2) By the conditional-distribution formula,

$$ \begin{aligned} \Ex[E_B\mid E_A]&=\frac{3.2131}{4}\times(-8.0)\\ &=-6.426\ \mathrm{eV},\\ \Var[E_B\mid E_A]&=3.2131-\frac{3.2131^{2}}{4}\\ &=3.2131-2.5810\\ &=0.6321\ \mathrm{eV^{2}}. \end{aligned} $$

The standard deviation is $0.795\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(3) Since $\Cov[e_{\mathrm{at}}(0),E_A]$ $=2k(0,0)$ $=2$, we have $\Ex[e_{\mathrm{at}}(0)\mid E_A]$ $=2/4\times(-8.0)$ $=-4.0\ \mathrm{eV}$, and the variance is $1-2^{2}/4=0$. The two atoms of structure A have the same environment, so the total energy determines $-4.0$ eV per atom with certainty. On the other hand, for an atom with $G=1$, from $\Cov[e_{\mathrm{at}}(1),E_A]$ $=2e^{-0.5}$ $=1.2131$ we get $\Ex[e_{\mathrm{at}}(1)\mid E_A]$ $=1.2131/4\times(-8.0)$ $=-2.426\ \mathrm{eV}$, with variance $1-1.2131^{2}/4=0.632$ (standard deviation $0.795$ eV). Because we have not observed an environment with $G=1$, only part of the information on $G=0$ is carried over through the kernel similarity, and a large uncertainty remains. The prediction of $E_B$ in (2) is $-4.0+(-2.426)=-6.426$ eV, and all of its uncertainty comes from the atom with $G=1$. Note also that the value $-2.426$ eV depends strongly on the prior mean of 0 and on the kernel assumption, and is not a physically meaningful “atomic energy” (the caution in Section 27.9).

Exercise 27.7: The yardstick for potential errors and the design of training data

Solution

(1) At 300 K: $k_{\mathrm B}T$ $=0.02585\ \mathrm{eV}$ $=25.9\ \mathrm{meV}$, and $3\ \mathrm{meV}$ is $0.116$ times that. At 1500 K: $k_{\mathrm B}T$ $=0.1293\ \mathrm{eV}$ $=129.3\ \mathrm{meV}$, and $3\ \mathrm{meV}$ is $0.023$ times that. Per atom, the error is well below $k_{\mathrm B}T$.

(2) $200\times3\ \mathrm{meV}$ $=0.60\ \mathrm{eV}$. At 1500 K, $0.60/0.1293=4.64$ and $e^{4.64}\approx104$ times. Even if the per-atom error is small, when the errors point in the same direction they can change the ratio of probabilities between configurations by two orders of magnitude (in practice part of the errors cancel, so this is an upper-limit estimate).

(3) Examples:

  • Structures to add to the training data: snapshots of molecular dynamics near 1500 K (large thermal vibrations), liquid structures, structures with defects such as vacancies and interstitial atoms and structures in the middle of diffusion (near the saddle point), and structures with changed volume. With low-temperature crystals alone, the high-temperature atomic environments lie outside the training range (Section 27.7, Figure 27.6).
  • Active learning: run high-temperature molecular dynamics with the trained potential, and add the structures with large uncertainty (ensemble spread or predictive variance) after computing them from first principles (Figure 27.7).
  • Checks after training: besides the RMSE of the energy, compare the force errors and the errors in energy differences between configurations (defect formation energies, diffusion barriers) with $k_{\mathrm B}T$.
  • Evaluation on the target properties: compare the melting point, diffusion coefficient, thermal expansion, and so on with first-principles molecular dynamics (small systems) or experimental values. Test on structures not used in training (a different temperature or composition).
  • Monitor whether signs of extrapolation appear during molecular dynamics, such as atoms approaching each other abnormally closely.

Exercise 27.8: The transition temperature in the mean-field approximation

Solution

(1) $k_{\mathrm B}T_{\mathrm c}$ $=Jn_{\mathrm{nn}}$ $=0.020\times6$ $=0.120\ \mathrm{eV}$, $T_{\mathrm c}$ $=0.120/(8.617\times10^{-5})$ $=1393\ \mathrm{K}$.

(2) At $T=0.8\,T_{\mathrm c}$, $\beta Jn_{\mathrm{nn}}=1/0.8=1.25$. Iterating,

$$ \begin{aligned} m&:\ 1\to0.8483\to0.7858\to0.7540\\ &\quad\to0.7364\to0.7261\to0.7200\\ &\quad\to\cdots\to0.7104 . \end{aligned} $$

To three decimal places, $m=0.710$ (the value in Figure 27.3 (b) of the main text).

(3) The mean-field approximation gives $4J$, and the exact solution is $2J/\ln(1+\sqrt2)=2J/0.8814=2.269J$, so the ratio is $4/2.269=1.76$. As the number $n_{\mathrm{nn}}$ of interacting partners increases, the effective field felt by one spin becomes an average over many spins, and the fluctuation of that average (the part that the mean-field approximation neglects) becomes relatively small (the same idea as the variance of an average of independent quantities decreasing as $1/n$; Chapter 2, Section 2.8). Therefore the mean-field approximation becomes more accurate in three dimensions, and still more so on lattices with more partners. Conversely, in one dimension ($n_{\mathrm{nn}}=2$), fluctuations destroy the order, so the exact solution has no finite-temperature phase transition, whereas the mean-field approximation predicts $k_{\mathrm B}T_{\mathrm c}=2J$.

← Back to Chapter 27, “Connections to Materials Science and Statistical Mechanics — From the Partition Function to Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials”

Chapter 28 A Comprehensive Exercise in Materials Search — From Checking the Data to Proposing Additional Experiments

Exercise 28.1: The second EI

Solution

$d=\mu_*-f_{\mathrm{best}}=-3.371-(-3.426)=0.055$, $z=d/\sigma_*=0.055/0.104=0.529$. Substituting into the formula for expected improvement, $\mathrm{EI}=d\,\Phi(z)+\sigma_*\Normal(z\mid0,1)$, we obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{EI}&=0.055\times0.7015+0.104\times0.3469\\ &=0.0386+0.0361=0.0747. \end{aligned} $$

The value 0.074 in Table 28.6 was computed from the values before rounding ($\mu_*=-3.37108$, $\sigma_*=0.10397$, $f_{\mathrm{best}}=-3.42565$), which gives 0.0743. The difference comes from rounding the inputs to three digits.

Although the predicted mean exceeds the best value, the EI is smaller than at the first round, for two reasons. First, the best value itself rose from $-3.912$ to $-3.426$ with the first observation, so the margin $d$ by which the mean exceeds it decreased from 0.191 to 0.055. Second, the width $\sigma_*$ also shrank from 0.147 to 0.104. The EI is determined by both the “improvement measured by the mean” $d$ and the “prospect of luckily exceeding the best value by as much as the width” $\sigma_*$, and both became smaller.

Exercise 28.2: Covariance with a batch term

Solution

(1) Two samples in the same batch $j$ and under the same condition $\bm{x}$ are $y_1=f(\bm{x})+u_j+\varepsilon_1$ and $y_2=f(\bm{x})+u_j+\varepsilon_2$. Since $f$, $u_j$, $\varepsilon_1$, and $\varepsilon_2$ are independent, the bilinearity of covariance leaves only the terms involving the same random variable:

$$ \begin{aligned} \Cov[y_1,y_2]&=\Var[f(\bm{x})]+\Var[u_j]\\ &=\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma_b^{2}. \end{aligned} $$

(Since $\varepsilon_1$ and $\varepsilon_2$ are different, independent random variables, their covariance is 0.) Each variance is $\Var[y_i]=\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma_b^{2}+\sigma^{2}$, so the correlation coefficient is

$$ \rho_{\text{same}}=\frac{\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma_b^{2}}{\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma_b^{2}+\sigma^{2}} . $$

(2) For different batches $j\ne j'$, $u_j$ and $u_{j'}$ are independent, so $\Cov[y_1,y_2]=\sigma_f^{2}$, and the correlation coefficient is $\rho_{\text{diff}}=\sigma_f^{2}/(\sigma_f^{2}+\sigma_b^{2}+\sigma^{2})$.

With the estimates in the main text, $\sigma_f=0.404$, $\sigma_b=0.097$, $\sigma=0.043$, we have $\sigma_f^{2}=0.1632$, $\sigma_b^{2}=0.0094$, $\sigma^{2}=0.0018$, and the denominator is $0.1745$, so $\rho_{\text{same}}=0.1726/0.1745=0.989$ and $\rho_{\text{diff}}=0.1632/0.1745=0.935$. Two samples in the same batch are more strongly correlated.

(3) For fixed inputs, $\bm{f}\sim\Normal(m\bm{1},\bm{K})$, $\bm{u}=(u_1,\dots,u_J)^{\tp}\sim\Normal(\bm{0},\sigma_b^{2}\bm{I})$, and $\bm{\varepsilon}\sim\Normal(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\bm{I})$ are independent, and $\bm{y}=\bm{f}+\bm{Z}\bm{u}+\bm{\varepsilon}$ is a linear combination of Gaussian-distributed quantities, so it is Gaussian-distributed. The mean is $m\bm{1}$ and the covariance is $\bm{K}+\bm{Z}(\sigma_b^{2}\bm{I})\bm{Z}^{\tp}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}=\bm{K}_y$ (Eq. (28.9) of Chapter 28). Taking the logarithm of the multivariate Gaussian density gives

$$ \begin{aligned} &\ln p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{\theta})\\ &=-\tfrac12(\bm{y}-m\bm{1})^{\tp}\bm{K}_y^{-1}(\bm{y}-m\bm{1})\\ &\quad-\tfrac12\ln\abs{\bm{K}_y}-\tfrac N2\ln2\pi , \end{aligned} $$

where $\bm{K}_y=\bm{K}+\sigma_b^{2}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\tp}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}$ and $\bm{\theta}=(\ell_c,\ell_T,\sigma_f,\sigma,\sigma_b)$. The form is the same as the equation in Chapter 12; only $\bm{K}_y$ is replaced. As in Chapter 12, the computation is done with the Cholesky decomposition.

Exercise 28.3: Translating the prediction error into the language of conductivity

Solution

(1) $10^{0.253}=1.79$. The prediction of the conductivity of a sample fired in a new batch is typically off by a factor of about 1.8 (either too high or too low). Since $10^{-0.253}=0.56$, we may also say “0.56 to 1.8 times the prediction.”

(2) The observed value is $10^{-3.248}=5.65\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{S\,cm^{-1}}$ and the true value is $10^{-3.401}=3.97\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{S\,cm^{-1}}$. The ratio is $10^{-3.248+3.401}=10^{0.153}=1.42$, so the reported value is 40 % higher than the true value.

(3) This value was chosen because it was the largest among the five additional experiments, so it is quite likely to be inflated by noise and the batch offset (Chapter 28, Section 28.9.2). Before reporting it, remake the sample several times under the same condition, including other batches, and show the mean and standard deviation. Alternatively, attach the predicted mean of the Gaussian process and its width (after accumulating observations, $\mu_*\pm2\sigma_*$). For comparison, it is also good to put a baseline sample (a condition measured many times in the past) in the same batch and show the value with the batch offset subtracted (Exercise 28.4).

Exercise 28.4: Putting a baseline sample in each batch

Solution

(1) When measured in separate batches $j,j'$, $y_{\mathrm A}-y_{\mathrm R}=\bigl(g_{\mathrm A}-g_{\mathrm R}\bigr)$ $+(u_j-u_{j'})$ $+(\varepsilon_{\mathrm A}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm R})$. The four random variables are independent, so the variance of the difference is $2\sigma_b^{2}+2\sigma^{2}$ $=2\times0.0064+2\times0.0025$ $=0.0178$, and the standard deviation is $\sqrt{0.0178}=0.133$.

(2) In the same batch $j$, $u_j$ cancels, and $y_{\mathrm A}-y_{\mathrm R}=(g_{\mathrm A}-g_{\mathrm R})+(\varepsilon_{\mathrm A}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm R})$. The variance is $2\sigma^{2}=0.005$ and the standard deviation is $\sqrt{0.005}=0.071$.

(3) If we put the baseline sample in the same batch and take the difference, the uncertainty of the difference becomes roughly half (0.133 → 0.071). In each batch of the additional experiments, it is good to fire one baseline sample together with the samples of the new conditions. From the value of the baseline sample we can estimate the offset $u_j$ of that batch, so we can separate whether a new condition is good or bad from the batch offset. On the surrogate model side, include the batch term of Section 28.5.2 ($\sigma_b^{2}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\tp}$), and add the observations of the baseline sample to the data together with their batch numbers. Then, through the correlation between observations in the same batch, the Gaussian process automatically subtracts the offset when evaluating the new conditions.

Exercise 28.5: Start over with a different seed

Solution

Compute as follows (using the same functions as in the main text).

import ch28_capstone as cc
d, u = cc.clean_from_seed(20261113)
X, y, b = cc.stack(d["c"], d["T"]), d["y"], d["batch"]
fr, fg = cc.folds_random(len(y), 5, 20261112), cc.folds_group(b)
for key in ("mean", "gp"):
    rr = cc.cross_validate(key, X, y, b, fr)
    rg = cc.cross_validate(key, X, y, b, fg)
    print(key, cc.mae_rmse(y, rr["pred"]), cc.mae_rmse(y, rg["pred"]))
rg = cc.cross_validate("gp", X, y, b, fg)
print(cc.coverage(y, rg["pred"], rg["s_obs"]))

The results (Python 3.12.13, numpy 2.5.4, scipy 1.18.1) are as follows. The true batch offsets of this data set are, from B1 in order, $+0.070$, $-0.173$, $-0.018$, $+0.030$, $-0.053$.

  • Model that answers with the mean: random RMSE 0.277, batch-wise RMSE 0.295.
  • Gaussian process: random RMSE 0.081, batch-wise RMSE 0.230. Batch-wise coverage 0.825.

The conclusion is preserved. The Gaussian process beats the baseline (0.295) even batch-wise, but the error under random splits (0.081) is about one third of the batch-wise error (0.230) and is therefore optimistic. The coverage is 0.825, which is higher than for the data in this chapter (0.70) but does not reach the nominal 0.95. It is a value that falls within the results of 100 data sets generated in the same way (Table 28.5: mean 0.846, interquartile range 0.800–0.925).

Exercise 28.6: Coverage of an overconfident interval

Solution

The half-width of the interval is $a=1.96\times0.035=0.0686$. The probability that the error $e\sim\Normal(0,0.094^{2})$ falls inside the interval is, after standardization,

$$ \begin{aligned} P(\abs{e}\le a)&=2\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{0.0686}{0.094}\right)-1\\ &=2\,\Phi(0.730)-1\\ &=2\times0.767-1=0.534 . \end{aligned} $$

An interval with nominal 95 % coverage actually contains the truth only about 53 % of the time. This nearly agrees with the coverage of 0.55 under random splits in Section 28.7.4. The median predicted standard deviation there is 0.035, and the RMSE of the Gaussian process under random splits is about 0.10, so this simple calculation explains well the magnitude of the failure. If the width of the interval is shrunk to about 0.37 times (0.035/0.094), the coverage drops from 0.95 to 0.53.

Exercise 28.7: Checking a report

Solution

We list the main problems and how to fix them.

  1. Leakage in preprocessing: standardization and the correlation-based selection of descriptors are done on all 60 points before the split. The selection of descriptors in particular uses the target values, so the target values of the validation data enter the selection (in the numerical experiment of Chapter 6, Section 6.7, even irrelevant descriptors turned $R^{2}$ into 0.46). Fix: split first, and redo the standardization and selection within the training data of each fold (nested cross-validation).
  2. Splits that cross groups: the data from the three laboratories may have systematic offsets for each laboratory (instruments, procedures, ways of measuring). Under random splits, other samples from the same laboratory are on the training side, so the result is optimistic. Fix: split by laboratory (and further by batch). If the model is to be used in a new laboratory or a new batch, report the error of a split matched to that use.
  3. $R^{2}$ is the only metric: $R^{2}$ comes out high when the range of the target values is wide (Chapter 6, Section 6.10). Fix: show the RMSE or MAE with units, and place it alongside baseline values such as that of the model that answers with the mean. Also attach a scatter plot of predicted versus measured values and the coverage of the prediction interval.
  4. A single success: making one sample at the condition with the largest prediction and succeeding is not a proof that the method is effective (it may have been luck, or other methods might have found it too). Fix: show the results of many comparisons, on synthetic data and past data, against random search and the like with the same budget. Carry out the actual additional experiments for several candidates as well.
  5. Reporting the value of one sample as a “record high”: an observed value selected because it is the maximum comes out high (Chapter 28, Section 28.9.2). Fix: remake the sample under the same condition and show the mean and the spread, and also show the difference from the baseline sample within the same batch.
  6. No information for reproduction: the seed, the library versions, the hyperparameters and how they were chosen, the split assignments, and the checks and treatments of the data are not written. Fix: write the items of Table 28.9, and publish the data and the scripts (the FAIR principles).
  7. “Effective for searching for new materials” is an overstatement: what was verified is prediction within the range of known firing conditions. The performance of extrapolation to a new composition system has not been evaluated. Fix: limit the claim to the range that was evaluated, or perform an evaluation that leaves out a whole lineage (Chapter 6, Section 6.8).
